The Chordal Distance Transform of Geometric Loops and its Persistent Homology
James A. D. Binnie, Otto Sumray, Ka Man Yim

Abstract
We present an isometry and parametrisation invariant of embeddings of S 1 S^{1} into Euclidean space. We do so by representing the distance between pairs of points on the embedded circle as a function on a Möbius band, the two-point finite subset space of S 1 S^{1} . We call this function the chordal distance transform of the embedding. We show that the sublevel set persistent homology of the chordal distance transform satisfies the desired isometry and parametrisation invariance, and is a continuous transform with respect to the Whitney topology on the space of circle embeddings and the bottleneck distance in the space of persistence diagrams. We then considered the generic behaviour of the chordal distance transform for C 2 C^{2} and finite piecewise linear embeddings. In the C 2 C^{2} -case, we show that non-boundary critical points of the chordal distance transform are finite and non-degenerate on an open and dense subset of circle embeddings. Consequently, its persistent homology is pointwise finite dimensional for generic C 2 C^{2} -embeddings. In the finite piecewise linear case, we also find piecewise-continuous analogues of non-degenerate critical points, and give generic conditions for the homological critical points of the chordal distance transform to be non-degenerate. In order to gain a geometric interpretation of the chordal distance transform and its persistent homology, we give a geometric characterisation of the C 2 C^{2} and finite piecewise linear non-degenerate critical points. Finally, we consider how the chordal distance transform can be generalised to capture geometric features involving n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 points on an embedded shape, as a function on the n n -point finite subset space.



1
Introduction

Topological Data Analysis (TDA) concerns the statistical analysis of shapes. A leading paradigm for tackling this problem is to transform shapes into vectors features which express salient topological and geometric properties of the shape. In mathematical terms, this involves desigining a feature map
F
from a space of shapes, to a vector space
V
. Having expressed characteristics of shapes as vectors, classical statistical tools can then be applied to analyse datasets of shapes.
In this study, we consider how we can perform topological data analysis on
geometric loops
. Our work is motivated by problems in biological and chemical shape analysis, where structures such as cell contours and ring compounds are modelled as geometric loops
13
.

Figure 1
:
The chordal distance transform transforms a geometric loop represented by an embedding
\gamma
into a function
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
on the Möbius band. This gives an isometry invariant representation of our geometric loop. The persistent homology of the sublevel set filtration of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
yields a summary of the geometry of the loop that is invariant with respect to reparametrisations of
\gamma
.

The space of geometric loops is parametrised by the space of circle embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
into Euclidean space. However, applying statistical methods to the embeddings directly is a potentially challenging prospect, since there are infinitely many
equivalent
embeddings which represent the same geometric loop. As such, any statistical model inferred on finitely many observations of loop embeddings may fail to generalise well to unseen data. To overcome this challenge, we consider a feature map (
Figure

1
) which is a composition of two transformations:

F:\quad\mathrm{Emb}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)\xrightarrow{\pershomf}\mathbf{Dgm}

(1)

The first map is the
chordal distance transform

\mathcal{T}
, which is a topological realisation of the distance matrix of points along the circle embedding
\gamma
as a real-valued function
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
on a Möbius band. The Möbius band is homeomorphic to the two-point finite subset space
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
of the circle. However, as
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is dependent on how
\gamma
is parametrised, we apply
sublevel set persistent homology
(
\pershomf
)
20
;
23
;
24
to remove this dependency that is not related to the geometry of the loop. Persistent homology is a topological summary of the function that describes the changes in the of sublevel sets

\Gamma^{a}:=\{x\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\ :\ \mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(x)\leq a\}

(2)

as we vary the thresholding parameter
a\in[0,\infty)
. The persistent homology of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
in dimension
i
is the family of linear maps between homology groups of sublevel sets (with coefficients in some fixed field
\mathbb{F}
), induced by inclusion:

H_{i}(\Gamma^{\bullet}):(H_{i}(\Gamma^{a})\to H_{i}(\Gamma^{b}))_{0\leq a\leq b<\infty}

This family of linear maps, or persistence module, is represented by a persistence diagram
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
that describe the birth and death of homological features as we vary the filtration parameter
a
. Persistence diagrams can then be vectorised to produce features compatible with statistical and machine learning algorithms (see
4
for a survey of vectorisation methods for persistence diagrams).

C^{k}
,
k\geq 2
)

\mathcal{T}:\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})\to C^{k}(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)

k\geq 0

)

continuous (
Corollary

3.3
).

\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}

S^{1}
and Euclidean isometries for any topological embedding (
Proposition

3.5
)

\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})

Yes (
Propositions

5.6
and
5.7
)

On a generic (i.e. open and dense) subset of space of embeddings
\Xi\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
(
Theorem

4.3
)

Geometric characterisation of critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
(i.e. birth and death of features in
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
)

Non-degenerate critical points if generic conditions
(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
satisfied, geometric and homological description in
Definition

5.13
and
Theorem

5.14
respectively.

Non-degenerate on
\Xi
; described in
Theorem

4.18

Table 1
:
A summary of the key properties of
\mathcal{T}
. The continuity and invariance of
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}
holds for topological embeddings of circle into Euclidean space. We show for two special cases of embeddings – where they are either finite piecewise linear, or
C^{2}
– that we can have tame persistence modules and geometric characterisations of critical points with different Morse indices.

In this article, we focus on theoretical properties of the feature map
F
in
eq.

1
to justify its use in data analysis of geometric loops. In particular, we consider four desirable properties of
F
, namely:

1.

The continuity of
F
;

2.

The invariance of
F
with respect to Euclidean isometries and reparametrisations of the loop
\gamma
;

3.

The tameness of
F
(i.e. whether persistence diagrams
F(\gamma)=\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
are represented by finitely many points); and

4.

The geometric interpretation of the the persistence diagram
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
.

We give a summary of how we have addressed these questions in
Table

1
. For topological circle embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, we prove that
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}
is continuous and invariant with respect to reparametrisations of the embedding and Euclidean isometries in in
Corollary

3.3
and
Proposition

3.5
respectively. For tameness and geometric interpretation of
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
, we restrict ourselves two broad classes of circle embeddings, where the embedding
\gamma
is either
C^{2}
or finite piecewise linear (PL). The PL case arises in applications where we may only observe finitely many samples
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
of some loop, and we use a piecewise linear interpolation to approximate the underlying shape. They also arise naturally in chemistry where we can represent ring compound configurations as piecewise linear loops. The
C^{2}
case is also of independent theoretical interest from a differential geometry perspective. We are also practically motivated to study to
C^{2}
case as they describe smooth interpolations of finite data
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
using splines.

The restriction to
C^{2}
and PL embeddings allows us to take a Morse-theoretic approach to address the tameness of persistence diagrams
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
and the geometric interpretation of homological features described in
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
. Morse theory enables us to constrain the topology of the sublevel sets of functions with analytic properties of a function’s critical points. If a critical point is non-degenerate, then they correspond to the birth or death of death of a homological feature in the filtration
\Gamma^{a}
, as summarised in persistence diagrams
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
. Thus, we can relate the persistence diagram
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
to the underlying geometry of the loop
\gamma
by studying how non-degenerate critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
depend on the geometric and analytic properties of
\gamma
.

In the case where embeddings are
C^{2}
, we use tools from transversality theory in differential topology and classical (smooth) Morse theory to show that for a generic circle embedding
\gamma
, its chordal distance transform
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
only has finitely many non-degenerate critical points on the interior of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. As a consequence, for a generic embedding
\gamma
, each non-trivial birth or death of a homological feature in the filtration
\Gamma^{a}
can be associated with a non-degenerate critical point of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
, and the persistence diagram
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
is tame (
Theorem

4.3
). In the case where
\gamma
is PL, smooth Morse theory fails to apply. However, we can describe the filtration
\Gamma^{a}
of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
combinatorially instead, as
\Gamma^{a}
is homotopy equivalent to a filtration of a finite simplicial complex (
Proposition

5.6
). We then proceed to analyse the simplicial filtration using discrete/combinatorial Morse theory. As we can describe the homological changes with finite data, we can conclude that
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
is tame. This homotopy equivalence also gives a combinatorial algorithm that allows us to compute the persistent homology
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
exactly in the PL case. Furthermore, we show that if a set of generic conditions
(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
is satisfied for a PL embedding, then we can identify PL analogues of
C^{2}
-non-degenerate critical points in the PL-case (
Theorem

5.14
).

In the generic case described above, we can interpret the birth and death of homological features in
H_{i}(\Gamma^{\bullet})
by studying the geometric significance of the non-degenerate critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
associated with them. As
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
is two-dimensional manifold with boundary (in fact a Möbius band), the non-degenerate critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
in the interior of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
are either minima, saddles, or maxima; the birth of a feature in
H_{0}(\Gamma^{\bullet})
corresponds to a minimum, the death of a feature in
H_{1}(\Gamma^{\bullet})
corresponds to a maximum, and the death of a feature in
H_{0}(\Gamma^{\bullet})
or a birth of a feature in
H_{1}(\Gamma^{\bullet})
corresponds to a saddle point. In the
C^{2}
-case, we give a geometric description of minima, saddles, or maxima in terms of local tangent and curvature conditions of
\gamma
in
Theorem

4.18
. In the PL-case, we describe them geometrically in
Definition

5.13
and
Theorem

5.14

in terms of the
acuteness or obtuseness
of angles between line segments and chords.

Finally, we discuss how we may generalise the chordal distance transform to encode higher order geometric information about any embeddings
X\hookrightarrow\R^{d}
into Euclidean space. We propose a generalisation called the volume transform
\mathsf{Vol}_{k}
; the case where
X=S^{1}
and
k=1
recovers the chordal distance transform
\mathcal{T}
. We discuss challenges in deriving theoretical bounds for such generalisations, as higher order finite subset spaces of manifolds can be stratified spaces where standard differential topology and Morse theory for manifolds cannot be directly applied.

The Chordal Distance Transform

We propose the chordal distance transform
\mathcal{T}
as a representation of the geometry of an embedding
\gamma
, using the distance between pairs of points
\gamma(z_{1}),\gamma(z_{2})
on the geometric loop. Consider the continuous function
d_{\gamma}:S^{1}\times S^{1}\to\R
on the torus, where
d_{\gamma}(z_{1},z_{2})=\norm{\gamma(z_1) - \gamma(z_2)}_{2}
. Naïvely, we can represent the geometry of the loop with
d_{\gamma}
. However,
d_{\gamma}
contains redundant information as
d_{\gamma}
is symmetric when we permute
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
; the function on one half of the torus carries the same information as the other (see
Figure

2
). To remove this redundancy, we consider the induced function on the quotient space
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})=(S^{1}\times S^{1})/S_{2}
, which is homeomorphic to a Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
. The space
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
is the space of subsets
\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\subset S^{1}
of cardinality at most 2. Since
\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
defines a
chord
on the circle, we can consider
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
as the space of chords on a circle. The chordal distance transform is then a function that sends a chord
\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
to the distance between its end points
\norm{\gamma(z_1) - \gamma(z_2)}_{2}
in the embedded loop.

Definition 1.1
.

The chordal distance transform
\mathsf{T}:\mathrm{Emb}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})\to C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
is the function that sends a circle embedding
\gamma:S^{1}\hookrightarrow\R^{d}
to the unique continuous map
\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}:\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\to\R
on the quotient space
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
, such that
d_{\gamma}=\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}\circ q
:

(3)

Explicitly,
\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}(\{z_{1},z_{2}\})=\norm{\gamma(z_1) - \gamma(z_2)}_{2}
. The smoothed chordal distance transform
\mathcal{T}:\mathrm{Emb}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})\to C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
is give by.

\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}^{2}.

(4)

Remark 1.2
.

We mainly work with the smoothed chordal distance transform here for the purposes of deriving theoretical results, as
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is a
C^{k}
-map on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
as a manifold with boundary if
\gamma
is
C^{k}
. The theoretical properties we derive for
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
extend to
\pershomf(\mathsf{T}_{\gamma})
, as there is a bijection between the persistence diagrams of
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
and
\pershomf(\mathsf{T}_{\gamma})
(
Lemma

3.1
). In applications, we should use
\mathsf{T}
in practice, as is a 2-Lipschitz map from
\mathrm{Emb}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
to
C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
(
Lemma

3.2
).

Figure 2
:
We derive the chordal distance transform of a circle embedding
\gamma
from the distance matrix of pairs of points, as a function
d:S^{1}\times S^{1}\to\R
. Since the distance matrix is symmetric with respect to permutation
d(z_{1},z_{2})=d(z_{2},z_{1})
(visually represented by reflecting across the diagonal), we remove the redundant information introduced by symmetry by only considering the distance evaluated on pairs of points on the circle, modulo permutation
\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
. This gives us a function
\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}
on the two-point subset space
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
of
S^{1}
, which is homeomorphic to the Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
. We call this function the chordal distance transform of
\gamma
, as each
\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
in
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
corresponds to a chord on the circle, and
\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}(\{z_{1},z_{2}\})
encodes the length of each chord on the embedded loop. On the left, we illustrate the distance matrix of an ellipse
\gamma
with major axis = 2, minor axis = 1. On the right, we illustrate the chordal distance trasnform
\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}:\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\to\R
as derived from
d
.

((a))

Chordal distance transform of a circle.

((b))

Chordal distance transform of an ellipse.

Figure 3
:
Heat map representations of the chordal distance transform of a circle (
fig.

3(a)
) and an ellipse (
fig.

3(b)
), as a function on the Möbius band. The ellipse here has major axis
a=8
and minor axis
b=1
.

Related Work

Shape statistics

In shape analysis, a range of statistical summaries have been developed to quantify and compare the shape of data. TDA-based approaches include the Persistent Homology Transform (PHT)
57

and the Euler Characteristic Transform (ECT)
31
;
50

which
record the persistent homology, respectively Euler characteristic,
of the shape filtered in each direction. For broad classes of shapes, the PHT/ECT is injective and thus uniquely determines the underlying set under mild conditions.
However, both methods are not invariant to rotation/translation and require the shape to be first oriented,
although there exist methods to remove some of this sensitivity (e.g.
61
).
Persistent homology has been directly used to study the shapes of loops
for cell contours
13

and for knotted and unknotted proteins
11
.
More classical methods include Elliptical Fourier Descriptors (EFDs)
53
;
42

and Procrustes analysis
30
;
1
.
EFDs are essentially the Fourier expansions of the Cartesian coordinate functions of a parametrised closed contour.
EFDs can be normalised to become invariant to translation and rotation (and scale),
however, as with the PHT and ECT this normalisation is non-canonical; translation invariance is usually achieved
by centring the centroid at the origin.
Procrustes analysis provides a geometric alignment by optimally removing translational, rotational, and scale differences between shapes (often represented as landmark sets), which allow comparative studies of morphological variation once the extrinsic differences have been eliminated.

Persistent homology on distance matrices

Applying persistent homology to distance matrices as a filtered complex is a concept that has appeared in several works related to times series analysis and characterising dynamical systems. In an article by Takashi Ichinomiya
36
and David Hien’s PhD thesis
34
, the distance matrix of time series data, also known as a recurrence plot
22
, is represented as a a filtered cubical complex called the distance complex. In
36
the complex is constructed on the full distance matrix, whereas
34
restricts the complex to the upper triangular half of the distance matrix, as the distance matrix is symmetric across the diagonal. This is equivalent to a representation of the unordered two-point configuration space
\mathbf{F}_{2}([0,1])
of
[0,1]
as a cubical complex. Takashi
36
empirically demonstrated that features derived from the persistent homology of the distance complex can be used to distinguish periodic and chaotic behaviour in dynamical systems, and is useful in classification of electrocardiogram data. In
34
, Hien focused on understanding the theoretical properties of this construction, proving that the
0
th
persistent homology of the distance complex captures more information than the
1
st
persistent homology of the Vietoris-Rips complex of the time series as a point cloud
(
34
, Theorem 5.2.5)
.

The chordal distance transform can be thought of as an analogous construction if we interpret embeddings of
S^{1}
as cyclic time series data. The sublevel set filtration of the chordal distance transform is a continuous version of the distance complex for explicitly cyclic data. As we are not considering embeddings of
[0,1]
but rather of
S^{1}
, the space which is filtered in our case is a Möbius band
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\approx\mathcal{M}
, rather than one homeomorphic to
[0,1]^{2}
or
\mathbf{F}_{2}([0,1])
in
36
and
34
respectively. In our study of piecewise linear embeddings of
S^{1}
that interpolate finite cyclic data
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
(
Section

5
), we consider the chordal distance transform of such an embedding as a piecewise continuous function on
\mathcal{M}
as a manifold, which is different to the distance complex built on the point set
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
. The filtration of the distance complex is induced solely from the distances between points
x_{i},x_{j}
in the point set, whereas the chordal distance transform takes into account of distance between points on the piecewise linear embedding interpolated between
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
. As we see in
Section

5
, homological critical values of the chordal distance transform filtration of
\mathcal{M}
can be induced by interpolated points, whereas the distance complex only has distances between points in
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
as critical values.

Configuration and Finite subset spaces

Configuration spaces record the positions of finitely many distinct points in a topological space.
If this space is a manifold, the homotopy type of the configuration space is a homeomorphism invariant of the underlying manifold
and as such has been widely studied in algebraic topology

40
;
19
;
39
.
Finite subset spaces were introduced in the early 20th century
14
;
15

with more recent development by Handel
32
and Tuffley
54
;
56
providing homotopic results about finite subset spaces for general topological spaces, surfaces and graphs.
Configuration spaces on graphs have also appeared in application, e.g.
27
.

A related function
to the chordal distance transform has been used with configuration spaces in the inscribed squares problem.
In
Vaughan’s proof
59
(see Section 4 in
46
)
that any planar simple smooth curve contains a rectangle,
he defines a function
f:\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\to\R^{3}
where

f(\{x,y\})=(\frac{1}{2}(\gamma(x)+\gamma(y)),\|\gamma(x)-\gamma(y)\|).

If
f
is injective, it would then induce an embedding of
\mathbb{R}P^{2}
into
\R
,
which is not possible.
f
being injective implies
\gamma
contains an inscribed rectangle.

Outline

We give an outline of the content in this article.
Section

2
gives necessary mathematical background for the paper:
section

2.1
introduces configuration spaces and finite subset spaces. We in particular focus on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, the 2
nd
finite subset space of
S^{1}
, and show that it is homeomorphic to a Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
. We also give an explicit atlas of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
as a manifold with boundary, which we use to give local coordinates for functions on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
for the remainder of the paper.

Section

2.2
gives background on smooth Morse theory and persistent homology of sublevel set filtrations,
relating non-degenerate critical points of the birth and death of features in sublevel set persistent homology.
We introduce the notion of Morse-supported (
Definition

2.22
) for the functions under our consideration,
namely non-negative functions on a manifold with zero on the boundary,
later used in
section

4
.
For the discrete case we give background on Conley indices, used in
section

5
.
In
section

2.3
we give a brief account of strong Whitney topologies for function spaces, jet bundles, and transversality theory, which is necessary for the genericity result in
section

4.1
.

In
section

3
we provide some elementary desirable properties of the chordal distance transform and the persistent homology of the chordal distance transform . We prove that
\mathcal{T}
and
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}
are continuous transforms in
section

3.1
. In
section

3.2
, we show that that
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}
they are invariant with respect to reparametrisations and Euclidean isometries. Furthermore, we discuss how the persistent homology of functions on the Möbius band can depend on the choice of field coefficients for homology in
section

3.3
.

In
section

4
we focus on smooth embeddings of loops in Euclidean space.
In
section

4.1
, we prove the main result of this section (
Theorem

4.3
): the chordal distance transform of (twice-differentiable) embeddings are generically Morse-supported, and hence their associated persistence module is finite dimensional. We address the open and density aspects of genericity in
sections

4.1.2
and
4.1.3
respectively.
Having shown that the generic embeddings are Morse-supported, we analyse the non-degenerate critical points of the chordal distance transform in
section

4.2
, and give a geometric characterisation of the local minima, maxima and saddle points. This thus gives a geometric interpretation of the birth and death of homological features in the persistence diagrams
\pershomf_{i}\circ\mathcal{T}(\gamma)
.

Section

5
focuses on piecewise-linear embeddings of
S^{1}
into Euclidean space.
The main results is
Theorem

5.14
that gives necessary and sufficient geometric conditions
(K0)
,
(K1)
and
(K2)
for a point on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
to be a non-degenerate homological
critical point of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
.
This is achieved in two parts: first by constructing a filtered simplicial complex whose persistent homology is isomorphic to the sublevel set filtration of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}

(
section

5.3.1
).
We give a precise construction of the simplicial complex and the filtration values of its simplices, allowing the persistent homology to computed in practice.
Second, under certain genericity of the embedding
(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
,
we show that the Morse sets of this simplicial complex are simplices themselves
and we give a geometrically characterisation for a point in
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
to correspond to such a critical simplex and determine its index as an
n
-saddle (
section

5.4
).

In Section
6
we describe possible extensions of the chordal distance transform to functions on higher order finite subset spaces. We show that the chordal distance transform can be seen as a special case of the
k
-simplex volume transform that takes embeddings to functions on
(k+1)
th
finite subset spaces. We show that this transform is continuous and stable in
Theorem

6.6
.

Finally, in
Section

7
, we have a concluding discussion on possible applications of the chordal distance transform to biology, and theoretical challenges in generalising the Morse-theoretic framework to higher order finite subset spaces.

2
Background

In this section we introduce the spaces that we work with, the configuration spaces, the symmetric product and the
n
-th finite subset space. We provide examples and intuition of the spaces and give some topological properties. We cover the second finite subset space of the circle in detail as this is the space we use in the CDT. We provide the fundamentals of Morse theory and persistent homology that will be used later sections. Further to that, we introduce differential topology concepts such as, the Whitney topology on function spaces, jets and multi-jets. In the final part of this section we discuss results about subsets of positive reach.

2.1
Configuration Spaces and Finite Subset Spaces

The main spaces we will be considering throughout are: the ordered and un-ordered configuration spaces, the symmetric product and the
n
-th finite subset space. Throughout we will assume
X
is a Hausdorff topological space.

Definition 2.1
.

Given a space
X
, its
ordered

n
-
point configuration space
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)

\displaystyle:=\quantity{(x_1, \ldots, x_n) \ : \ x_i \in X,\ i \neq j \implies x_i \neq x_j }=\quantity(\prod_{i=1}^n X)\setminus\Delta

(5)

\displaystyle\mbox{where}\quad\Delta

\displaystyle=\quantity{(x_1,\ldots, x_n) \ : \ x_i = x_j \qq{for some} i\neq j}

(6)

Naturally the symmetric group
S_{n}
acts on
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
by permutation, which leads to the following:

Definition 2.2
.

Taking the orbit space of
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
by
S_{n}
, define the
unordered

n
-
point configuration space

\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X):=\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)/S_{n}.

(7)

This quotient is in fact a covering map as we assume
X
is Hausdorff and the action of
S_{n}
(a finite group) is free. For
n=1
the following is true,
X=\mathrm{Conf}_{1}(X)=\mathrm{UConf}_{1}(X)
.

Given a set
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset X
, we use the notation
\lbag{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}\rbag
all elements notated in the brackets are distinct. Using this notation, can write
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
as a set

\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)=\left\{{\lbag{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}\rbag\ :\ x_{i}\in X}\right\}.

(8)

Remark 2.3
.

The notation of configuration spaces has varied widely throughout the literature,
\mathcal{F}^{n}(X)
,
F(X,n)
,
B(X,n)
,
C_{k}(X).
We shall use of the notation
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
and
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
to denote the ordered and unordered configuration spaces.

Example 2.4
.

We now consider
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
for
n=2,3
:
We see that
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is just the plane without the diagonal
\Delta=\{(x,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is the space that we get by folding over the diagonal
\Delta
. This space is homeomorphic to the upper half-plane.
For
\mathrm{Conf}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
we have
\mathbb{R}^{3}
without the planes
x=y
,
y=z
and
x=z
. This yields a space split into
6
disjoint areas. The space
\mathrm{UConf}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, is then made by collapsing the
6
disjoint areas to one wedge. Hence, this space is homeomorphic to a half-space of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Example 2.5
.

For
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}((0,1))
we have a similar situation to
\mathbb{R}
, but with the constraints of lying inside the square
(0,1)^{2}
. We then have that
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}((0,1))
is homeomorphic to
\{(x,y)\in(0,1)^{2}\ :\ x<y\}
. For
\mathrm{Conf}_{3}((0,1))
, this is the unit box cut into 6 areas, and so when we identify them, we see that
\mathrm{UConf}_{3}((0,1))
is homeomorphic to the open 3-simplex. In
Figure

4
we see what a single area looks like.

Figure 4
:
Here we see
\mathrm{UConf}_{3}((0,1))
as cut out by the planes,
x=y
,
x=z
and
y=z
, where we consider
x<y<z
. This is homeomorphic to the open 3-simplex as described in
2.5
.

Remark 2.6
.

If
X
is a smooth
d
-manifold then both
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
and
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
are smooth
dn
-manifolds
40
.

If we do not limit ourselves to
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
, but allow the entire space
X^{n}
and the natural action of
S_{n}
, then we have the following space.

Definition 2.7
.

The
n
-th
symmetric product
of
X
, is defined to be the quotient

SP^{n}(X):=X^{n}/S_{n}

The action is no longer free, and so for
n\geq 2
the symmetric product is an orbifold for
X
a smooth manifold (see
2
Ex. 1.13 ). We can express
SP^{n}(X)
as the set of unordered
n
-tuples
[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
.

Although the symmetric product consolidates the symmetries of points of the space into one, we wish to lose all multiplicity of points that have the same set of points. For example, if we consider
n=3
then we would like to treat
(a,b,b)
as the same as
(a,a,b)
. However, in the symmetric product these two points have different equivalences classes. So we need to define a new space that will allow us to consolidate these points together.

We now describe the
n
-th
finite subset space
of
X
,
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
. This is not only a quotient of
X^{n}
but also a quotient of
SP^{n}(X)
, as we see in
Proposition

2.10
.

Definition 2.8
.

We define
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
to be be set of all non-empty finite subsets of
X
of cardinality at most
n

54
. The map
E_{n}:X^{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
given by

(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})\mapsto\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}\}

(9)

induces the quotient topology on
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
, the
n
th finite subspace set of
X
.

Using the notation in
eq.

8
, we can also equivalently express the set
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
as

\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)=\left\{{\lbag{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\rbag\ :\ x_{i}\in X,\ k\in[n]}\right\}=\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{UConf}_{k}(X).

(10)

We note that for
n=2
we have that
SP^{n}(X)=\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
but for
n\geq 3
this no longer is the case as the
S_{3}
equivalence classes of
(x,x,y)
and
(x,y,y)
are certainly different in the symmetric product. However, their images under
E_{3}
are the same, namely
\lbag{x,y}\rbag
.

Remark 2.9
.

The
n
-th finite subset space, a term used by Tuffley
54
;
55
;
56
, is also known as the (finite) Ran space
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(X)

6
;
43
.
It should be noted that the
n
-th finite subset space, as defined here, has been referred to as the symmetric product in the literature. We denote the
n
-th finite subset space as,
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
. Other notations for this space have been given by:
\text{Sub}(X,n)
,
\text{exp}_{n}(X)
,
X^{(n)}
and
\text{Ran}_{\leq n}(X)

32
;
54
;
15
;
6
.

Proposition 2.10
.

There exists a unique quotient map,
Q_{n}:SP^{n}(X)\rightarrow\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
such that
E_{n}=Q_{n}\circ q
.

Proof.

We have the quotient map
q:X^{n}\xrightarrow{/S_{n}}SP^{n}(X),
which is continuous by definition. It is open as if
U\subset X^{n}
is open then, the pre-image of
[U]_{S_{n}}
is the union
\bigcup_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sigma\cdot U
which is open in
X^{n}
. As
E_{n}
is continuous and is invariant under the
S_{n}
action on
X^{n}
. By the universal property of the quotient map, there exists a unique continuous map
Q_{n}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SP^{n}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/S_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!Q_{n}}

(11)

We have that
V\subset\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is open
\iff E_{n}^{-1}(V)
is open in
X^{n}

\iff E_{n}^{-1}(V)=q^{-1}(Q_{n}^{-1}(V))
is open in
X^{n}
(as the triangle commutes)
\iff Q_{n}^{-1}(V)
is open in
SP^{n}(X)
as
q
is a quotient map. Hence,
V
is open in
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
if and only if
Q_{n}^{-1}(V)
is open in
SP^{n}(X)
.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

The map
E_{n}
is closed (and therefore perfect)
32
. However, it need not be an open map. As, if we consider
X=\mathbb{R}
and
n=3
. We have that a point
(0,2,2)
has a neighbourhood
U=(-1,1)\times(\frac{3}{2},3)\times(\frac{3}{2},3)
however, the point
(0,0,2)\in E^{-1}_{3}(E_{3}(U))
but no neighbourhood of this point can lie wholly inside
E^{-1}_{3}(E_{3}(U))
.

2.1.1
Stratification of Finite Subset Spaces

As any element
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}\in\mathbf{F}_{k}(X)
is also an element of
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
for
n\geq k
, we have a filtration

\emptyset\subset\mathbf{F}_{1}(X)\subset\mathbf{F}_{2}(X)\subset\cdots\subset\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X)\subset\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)

(12)

The inclusions are in fact embeddings: the topology of
\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X)
endowed by the quotient map
E_{n-1}:X^{n-1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X)
coincides with the subspace topology derived from
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)

(
32
, Prop 2.04)
. This filtration is related to the decomposition of
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
into lower order unordered configuration spaces in
eq.

10
. We can regard this filtration as a
stratification
of
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
, where the strata — the difference between successive spaces
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)
and
\mathbf{F}_{i-1}(X)
in the filtration — are given by the ordered configuration spaces
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)\setminus\mathbf{F}_{i-1}(X)\approx\mathrm{UConf}_{i}(X)
. This stratification has been shown to be conically smooth (Definition A.5.5 of
44
) in
6
.

We give an explicit description of the stratification via the quotient map
E_{n}:X^{n}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)

E_{n}:(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n})\mapsto\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n}\},

(13)

and a partition of
X^{n}
. We split
X^{n}
into the following disjoint union; writing
X^{n}_{i}
as the subset of points
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in X^{n}
with
i
distinct entries, we have

X^{n}=X^{n}_{1}\sqcup X^{n}_{2}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup X^{n}_{n}

(14)

We note that
X^{n}_{n}=\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
. If we recall the generalised diagonal
\Delta^{n-1}\subset X^{n}
are the tuples
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
that have at most
n-1
distinct entries, then by definition

\Delta^{n-1}=X^{n}_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup X^{n}_{n-1}\mbox{\quad and\quad}X^{n}=\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)\sqcup\Delta^{n-1}.

(15)

We now describe the inclusion
\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
in the stratification
eq.

12
by decomposing
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)=E_{n}(X^{n})
in terms of the images of the components
E_{n}(\Delta^{n-1})
and
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
whose union is
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
. We first observe that the images
E_{n}(\Delta^{n-1})
and
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
are disjoint and thus partition
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
:

\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)=E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))\sqcup E_{n}(\Delta^{n-1}).

(16)

This follows immediately from the definitions of the sets
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
and
\Delta^{n-1}
. Since
E_{n}(\Delta^{n-1})\subset\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
are exactly the finite subsets with at most
n-1
elements,

E_{n}(\Delta^{n-1})=\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X).

(17)

Because
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
are tuples with
n
distinct elements, elements of
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
are exactly subsets
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
with cardinality
n
, thus
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))\cap\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X)=\emptyset
. We now show that
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
is in fact homeomorphic to
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
; consequently
eq.

16
implies the following.

Proposition 2.12
.

\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)\setminus\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X)=E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))\approx\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
.

Proof.

Recall from
Proposition

2.10
that
E_{n}=Q_{n}\circ q
. Thus, we can write
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
as
Q_{n}(q(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)))=Q_{n}(\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X))
. As
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)\subset SP^{n}(X)
are precisely the
n
unordered elements of
X
each with multiplicity 1, the restriction of
Q_{n}
to
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
is a continuous bijection of
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
onto its image. To show that it is a homeomorphism, we now show that if
V
is open in
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
, then
Q_{n}(V)
is open in
Q_{n}(\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X))
.

We note if
V
is open in
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
, then it is open in
SP^{n}(X)
as
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
is an open subspace of
SP^{n}(X)
. Also, as
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
is open in
X^{n}
,
Q_{n}(\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X))
is open in
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
. Hence, we shall show
Q_{n}(V)
is open in the whole of
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
.

If
V
is open in
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
then
q^{-1}(V)
is open in
X^{n}
. We have that
Q_{n}(V)
is open in
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
if and only if
E_{n}^{-1}(Q_{n}(V))
is open in
X^{n}
. Since
Q_{n}
restricted to
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
is a continuous bijection, we have
E_{n}^{-1}(Q_{n}(V))=q^{-1}\circ Q_{n}^{-1}(Q_{n}(V))=q^{-1}(V)
.
Hence,
Q_{n}(V)
is open in
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
. Thus the restriction of
Q_{n}
from
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
to
Q_{n}(\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X))=E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
is a homeomorphism.

∎

Furthermore, for
i\leq n
, because the subspace topology of
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)
as a subset of
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
coincides with the quotient topology endowed by
E_{i}
, we can show by induction that
Proposition

2.12
implies

E_{n}(X^{n}_{i})=\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)\setminus\mathbf{F}_{i-1}(X)\approx\mathrm{UConf}_{i}(X)

(18)

for every
i\leq n
in the stratification
eq.

12
.
This allows us to decompose
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
in the following manner, following
eq.

10
:

X^{n}
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
=
=
X^{n}_{1}
X^{n}_{2}
X^{n}_{n}=\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
E_{n}(X^{n}_{1})
E_{n}(X^{n}_{2})
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
X
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(X)
\mathrm{UConf}_{n}(X)
\downarrow E_{n}
\sqcup
\sqcup
\sqcup
\sqcup\dots\sqcup
\sqcup\dots\sqcup
\sqcup\dots\sqcup
\downarrow E_{n}
\downarrow E_{n}
\downarrow E_{n}

\,\approx

\,\approx

\,\approx

(19)

Furthermore, the boundary of
\mathrm{UConf}_{i}(X)
in
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is exactly
\mathbf{F}_{i-1}(X)
.

Proposition 2.13
.

The boundary of
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
in
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)\backslash E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))=E_{n}(\Delta^{n-1})\approx\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X)
.

Proof.

If
X
is hausdorff then for each point in
q\in\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
we can find a selection of open sets
U_{q_{i}}
such that
q\in\prod_{i=1}^{n}U_{q_{i}}
and
U_{q_{i}}\cap U_{q_{j}}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. Hence
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
is open. We have that
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
is open if and only if
E^{-1}_{n}(E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)))
is open in
X^{n}
which is precisely
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
and thus open. Hence,
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
is open in
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
.

So the boundary of
E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
in
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is
\partial E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))=\text{cl}({E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))})\backslash\text{int}(E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)))=\text{cl}(E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))\backslash E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))
. Suppose that there exists an
x\in\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
such that
x\notin\text{cl}(E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)))
. Then there exists an open set
U\subset\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
such that
x\in U
and
U\cap E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))=\emptyset
. We have that
U
is open when
Y:=\{q\in X^{n}:[q]_{E_{n}}\in U\}
is open. If
q\in Y
then
q\notin\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)
hence when
q\in Y
,
q
has two entries the same, w.l.o.g.
q=(q_{1},q_{1},\dots,q_{n-1})
as
X
is hausdorff we have that
q\in U_{q_{1}}\times U_{q_{1}}\times\prod_{i=2}^{n-1}U_{q_{i}}\subset Y
where
U_{q_{i}}\cap U_{q_{j}}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. This means that
U_{q_{1}}
must be the singleton
\{q_{1}\}
, as
X
isn’t given the discrete topology, this set isn’t open (we exclude the discrete topology as all boundaries are empty). Hence we have that all
x\in\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
are in
\text{cl}(E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X)))
. So we have that,
\partial E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))=\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)\backslash E_{n}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X))=E_{n}(\Delta^{n-1})\approx\mathbf{F}_{n-1}(X)
.
∎

Proposition 2.14
.

The boundary of
E_{n}(X^{n}_{i})
in
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)\subset\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)\backslash E_{n}(X^{n}_{i})=E_{i}(\Delta^{i-1})\approx\mathbf{F}_{i-1}(X)
.

Proof.

We have that
E_{n}(X^{n}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}E_{i}(X^{i}_{i})\approx\mathrm{UConf}_{i}(X)
, which is open in
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)
. By proposition 2.04 of
32
we have that
E_{n}(X^{n}_{i})
is open in
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)\subset\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
. The closure of
E_{n}(X^{n}_{i})
in
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)\subset\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)
, with an analogous argument as the above proposition. Hence, in
\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)\subset\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
we have,
\partial E_{n}(X^{n}_{i})=\mathbf{F}_{i}(X)\backslash E_{n}(X^{n}_{i})=E_{i}(\Delta^{i-1})\approx\mathbf{F}_{i-1}(X)
.

∎

Figure 5
:
A cut and gluing argument showing that
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})=(S^{1}\times S^{1})/S_{2}
is the Möbius band. Expressing the torus as a square with opposite sides identified, the
S_{2}
action ‘folds’ the torus across the dashed diagonal. The resultant quotient space is the triangle with the indicated pair of edges identified with the orientation given in the diagram. To see that this space is homeomorphic to a Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
, we cut along the opposite diagonal giving us two triangles and another identification. We then identify the vertical and horizontal sides of the original triangle to obtain the square on the right, with a pair of opposing edges identified with opposite orientation.

2.1.2
The Second Finite Subset Space of the Circle as a Möbius band

((a))

An illustration of the universal covering
\bar{\scre}
of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, where we choose a square fundamental domain (demarcated by dashed orange lines) for the
\Z
-action on
[0,1]\times\R
. The action maps successive pairs of the orange dotted lines onto one another with
opposite
orientation,

((b))

An illustration of the universal covering
\bar{\scre}
of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, where we choose a triangular fundamental domain (demarcated by blue dashed lines) for the
\Z
-action on
[0,1]\times\R
. The
\Z
-action on a vertical blue edge of the triangular fundamental domain first reflects it across the red diagonal, and then translates it up by one vertical unit onto the horizontal edge.

Figure 6
:
An illustration of
Proposition

2.15
, which describes how the two point finite subset space
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
can be expressed as a Möbius band by relating the universal coverings
\tilde{\scre}
and
\bar{e}
of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
and
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
respectively, by quotient maps
q
and
\tilde{q}
. The quotient map
q\circ\tilde{\scre}
can be decomposed into a composition
\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q}
, where
\tilde{q}
is the quotient of
\R^{2}
by a
(\Z\rtimes S_{2})
-action; the diagonal strip
\approx[0,1]\times\R
(as a subset of
\R^{2}
) is not only a fundamental domain of the
(\Z\rtimes S_{2})
-action, but also homeomorphic to the quotient space. The map
\bar{\scre}
is the quotient by the glide reflection, a
\Z
-action on the infinite strip. We also note that the diagonal
\Delta\subset S^{1}\times S^{1}
(indicated by the red-dashed lines) is sent to the circle boundary of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
; this is a special case of
Proposition

2.13
. It can also be seen by first lifting the diagonals to its preimage in
\R^{2}
(also indicated as red dashed lines; as they form the boundary of the manifold with boundary
\R^{2}/(\Z\rtimes S_{2})=[0,1]\times\R
, and
\bar{\scre}
is a covering map which sends manifold boundaries to manifold boundaries, the image of
\Delta
under
q
is the boundary of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
.

In this section, we recall that
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
is homeomorphic to the Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
. This observation was first made in
14
, and a visual explanation can be found in
(
54
, §2.2)
, which we reproduce and explain in
Figure

5
. Here we use a more technical construction due to
48
. Morton’s construction involves deriving the universal covering
\bar{\scre}
of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by an infinite strip
[0,1]\times\R
, through the universal covering
\tilde{\scre}
of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
by
\R^{2}
. Recall we have a universal covering of
S^{1}
by
\scre:\R\to S^{1}
given by
\scre(t)=e^{2\pi\imath t}
, and a universal covering of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
given by products
\tilde{\scre}=\scre\times\scre:\R^{2}\to S^{1}\times S^{1}
. We first observe that we can obtain a quotient map from
\R^{2}
to
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by composing
\tilde{\scre}:\R^{2}\to S^{1}\times S^{1}
with
q:S^{1}\times S^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. Morton showed that this successive quotient by a
\Z^{2}
-action on
\R^{2}
and an
S_{2}
-action on
S^{1}\times S^{1}
is equivalent to a quotient by the action of a semi-direct product
\Z^{2}\rtimes S_{2}
on
\R^{2}
. Furthermore, the quotient
q\circ\tilde{e}
can be decomposed into another pair of successive quotients
\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q}
, where the first map
\tilde{q}
is the quotient of
\R^{2}
by the action of a normal subgroup
N\cong\Z\rtimes S_{2}
of
G
, and
\bar{\scre}
is the universal covering of the Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
by a
\Z
-action on
\R^{2}/N\approx[0,1]\times\R
. We summarise his construction in the
Proposition

2.15
below, and give details of the precise construction in
Appendix

A
.

Proposition 2.15

(
48
)
.

Let
q:S^{1}\times S^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
be the quotient of the torus by the
S_{2}
-action
\sigma\cdot(z_{1},z_{2})=(z_{\sigma(1)},z_{\sigma(2)})
, and
\tilde{e}:\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow S^{1}\times S^{1}
be the universal covering map of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
. Then there are quotients by group actions
\tilde{q}:\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\R^{2}/N\xrightarrow{\approx}[0,1]\times\R
, and
\bar{\scre}:[0,1]\times\R\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, such that the following diagram commutes:

(20)

Furthermore,

(i)

\bar{\scre}
is the universal covering of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
as a Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
; and

(ii)

There is an embedding
\lambda:[0,1]\times\R\hookrightarrow\R^{2}
of
\tilde{q}
whose image is
\{(x_{1},x_{2})\ :\ x_{2}-x_{1}\in[0,1]\}
, and
\tilde{q}\circ\lambda=\iden_{[0,1]\times\R}
.

(iii)

The boundary
\partial\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, homeomorphic to
S^{1}
, is the set of points
\{\{z\}\ :\ z\in S^{1}\}=\mathbf{F}_{1}(S^{1})=q(\Delta)
, where
\Delta
is the diagonal
\{(z,z)\ :\ z\in S^{1}\}\subset S^{1}\times S^{1}
.

Using
Proposition

2.15
, we can show that the map
q\circ\tilde{\scre}:\R^{2}\to\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
admits local inverses. That is, for every
w\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, there is a neighbourhood
W\ni w
such that there is an embedding
\varphi:W\hookrightarrow\R^{2}
such that
(q\circ\tilde{\scre})\circ\varphi=\iden_{W}
. We construct
\varphi
using lifts of the covering map
\bar{\scre}
, along with the embedding
\lambda
. By taking neighbourhoods for each
w\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, we obtain a collection of coordinates charts
\Phi=\{(\varphi,W)\}
for
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. We show that such charts can be chosen such that the collection forms an atlas for
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, which is a manifold with boundary as it is homeomorphic to a Möbius band.

Proposition 2.16
.

For every
w\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, there is a neighbourhood
W\ni w
such that there is an embedding
\varphi:W\hookrightarrow\R^{2}
, so that
(\bar{e}\circ\tilde{q})\circ\varphi=\iden_{W}
. If a collection of such neighbourhoods
\mathcal{W}=\{W_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
is an open cover of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, then any choice of such charts
\Phi=\{(\varphi_{j},W_{j})\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
for each
W_{j}
is an atlas for
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
.

Proof.

Since
\bar{\scre}
is a covering map, for
W
sufficiently small neighbourhood of
w\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, we can find an embedding
\bar{\varphi}:W\hookrightarrow[0,1]\times\R
. Composing it with the embedding
\lambda:[0,1]\times\R\hookrightarrow\R^{2}
in
Proposition

2.15
, we have an embedding
\varphi=\lambda\circ\bar{\varphi}:W\hookrightarrow\R^{2}
. Because
\tilde{q}\circ\lambda=\iden_{[0,1]\times\R}
, and
\bar{\scre}\circ\bar{\varphi}=\iden_{W}
,

(\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q})\circ\varphi=\bar{\scre}\circ(\tilde{q}\circ\lambda)\circ\bar{\varphi}=\bar{\scre}\circ\bar{\varphi}=\iden_{W}.

We now show that any collection
\Phi
of such coordinate charts form an atlas. For
W,W^{\prime}\subset\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
with non-empty intersection that admit lifts
\bar{\varphi},\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}
respectively of
W
and
W^{\prime}
, we have some
n\in\Z
such that
n\star\bar{\varphi}(W\cap W^{\prime})=\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}(W\cap W^{\prime})
. Let
T:\bar{\varphi}(W\cap W^{\prime})\to\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}(W\cap W^{\prime})
be that homeomorphism; then we have a transition map
\varphi(W\cap W^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\approx}\varphi^{\prime}(W\cap W^{\prime})
, given by

(\lambda Tq\rvert_{\varphi(W)})\circ\varphi\rvert_{W\cap W^{\prime}}=\lambda T\circ(q\lambda\rvert_{\bar{\varphi}(W)})\circ\bar{\varphi}\rvert_{W\cap W^{\prime}}=\lambda(T\bar{\varphi}\rvert_{W\cap W^{\prime}})=\lambda\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}\rvert_{W\cap W^{\prime}}=\varphi^{\prime}\rvert_{W\cap W^{\prime}}.

(21)

where in the first and second equality we used the fact that
\varphi=\lambda\bar{\varphi}
, and
q\lambda\rvert_{\bar{\varphi}(W)}=\iden_{\bar{\varphi}(W)}
respectively.
∎

Corollary 2.17
.

Let
\varphi:W\to\R^{2}
be a chart for
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, as described in
Proposition

2.16
. Then the map
\psi:=\tilde{\scre}\circ\varphi:W\to S^{1}\times S^{1}
is an embedding of
W
such that
q\circ\psi=\iden_{W}
.

Proof.

Because of the commutative square in
eq.

20
, we can also show that
\psi:W\to S^{1}\times S^{1}
is an embedding:

q\circ\psi=q\circ(\tilde{\scre}\circ\varphi)=(q\circ\tilde{\scre})\circ\varphi=(\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q})\circ\varphi=\iden_{W}.

Because
q\rvert_{\psi(W)}
is a continuous bijective map, and
\psi
is also a continuous bijection onto its image, we thus establish that
\psi
is an embedding (homeomorphism onto its image). Furthermore,
q\circ\psi(W)=W
, so
\psi(W)\subset q^{-1}{\left({W}\right)}
.
∎

Furthermore, we show that the coordinate charts
\varphi:W\hookrightarrow\R^{2}
inherit from the coordinate charts of the choice of
S_{2}
-orbit representatives
\psi(W)
in
S^{1}\times S^{1}
. We first establish some notation. If
\{(\theta_{i},U_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an atlas for
S^{1}
, then we let
\Theta=\{(\vartheta_{ab},U_{ab})\}_{a,b\in\mathcal{I}}
denote the induced atlas of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
, where
\vartheta_{ab}:=\theta_{a}\times\theta_{b}
, and
U_{ab}:=U_{a}\times U_{b}
.

Proposition 2.18
.

There exists an open cover
\mathcal{W}=\{W_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, and an atlas
\{(\theta_{i},U_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
for
S^{1}
whose charts
\theta_{i}
are lifts of the universal covering
\scre:\R\twoheadrightarrow S^{1}
, where the following are satisfied for any
j\in\mathcal{J}
:

•

There is a local embeddings
\psi_{j}:W_{j}\hookrightarrow S^{1}\times S^{1}
, such that
q\circ\psi_{j}=\iden_{W_{j}}
;

•

There exists some
a,b\in\mathcal{I}
where
\psi_{j}(W_{j})\subseteq U_{ab}=U_{a}\times U_{b}
; and

•

For
\vartheta_{ab}:=\theta_{a}\times\theta_{b}
, the map
\varphi_{j}=\vartheta_{ab}\circ\psi_{j}:W_{j}\to\R^{2}
is a chart.

Furthermore, the set of charts constructed as thus
\Phi=\{(\varphi_{j},W_{j})\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
is an atlas for
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
.

Proof.

We prove this proposition by showing that the atlas for
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
in
Proposition

2.16
satisfies these conditions, where the open cover consists of neighbourhoods
W
of all
w\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. For each such
W
, we have a chart
\varphi=\lambda\circ\bar{\varphi}
where
\bar{\varphi}:W\hookrightarrow[0,1]\times\R
is a lift of the covering map
\bar{\scre}
. The lift
\bar{\varphi}
is completely determined by where it sends
w
in
\bar{\scre}^{-1}{\left({w}\right)}
. Since
\lambda
is an embedding, this is equivalent to saying
\varphi
is completely determined by where it sends
w
in
(\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q})^{-1}{\left({w}\right)}
. Recall from
Proposition

2.16
that these charts form an atlas regardless of the choice of representative of
w
in
(\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q})^{-1}{\left({w}\right)}
.

We now note that the subset
F=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in[0,1)\times[0,1)\ :\ x_{2}-x_{1}\in[0,1]\}\subset\R^{2}
is in the image of
\lambda
, and contains exactly one point in the fibre
(\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q})^{-1}{\left({w}\right)}
for any
w\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. Let us set the charts
\varphi:(W,w)\hookrightarrow(\R^{2},(x,y))
in the atlas to be those that sends
w
to
(x,y)\in F
. For each
(x,y)\in F
, consider
\tilde{U}_{xy}=\tilde{U}_{x}\times\tilde{U}_{y}
, where
\tilde{U}_{x}=(x-1/2,x+1/2)
. We note that
\scre\rvert_{\tilde{U}}
is a homeomorphism onto its image
U_{x}:=\scre(\tilde{U}_{x})\subset S^{1}
. Let
\theta_{x}:U_{x}\to\tilde{U}_{x}
be the lift of the covering
{\scre}
at
U_{x}\subset S^{1}
such that
{\scre}\theta_{x}=\iden_{U_{x}}
. As these are lifts of covering maps from Euclidean space, the collection of such charts
\{(\theta_{x},U_{x})\}_{x\in[0,1)}
form atlas for
S^{1}
which owes its transition maps to the
\Z
-action on
\R
. Similarly, the product of charts
\Theta=\{(\vartheta_{xy},U_{xy})\}_{x,y\in[0,1)}
also form an atlas for
S^{1}\times S^{1}
, where
\vartheta_{xy}(U_{xy})=\tilde{U}_{xy}
, and
\tilde{\scre}\circ\vartheta_{xy}=\iden_{U_{xy}}
.

Now let us assume we have chosen the neighbourhood
W
of
w
to be sufficiently small such that
\varphi(W)\subset\tilde{U}_{xy}
, which we are allowed to do as
\varphi
is continuous. Let
\psi=\tilde{\scre}\circ\varphi:W\to S^{1}\times S^{1}
be the embedding such that
q\circ\psi=\iden_{W}
, as described in
Corollary

2.17
. As
\psi(W)=\tilde{\scre}\circ\varphi(W)\subset\tilde{\scre}(\tilde{U}_{xy})=U_{xy}
, we can apply
\vartheta_{xy}
to the image of
\psi
; thus

\vartheta_{xy}\circ\psi=(\vartheta_{xy}\circ\tilde{\scre}\rvert_{\varphi(W)})\circ\varphi=\varphi.

∎

The existence of compatible atlases implied by
Proposition

2.18
allows us to translate local coordinate descriptions of symmetric maps of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
to its induced map on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. If
f:S^{1}\times S^{1}\to X
satisfies
f(z_{1},z_{2})=f(z_{2},z_{1})
, then recall there exists a unique map
\bar{f}
such that
