\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

If we restrict
\bar{f}
to a cover element
W_{j}\subset\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
from the atlas
\Phi
, we can express
\bar{f}\rvert_{W_{j}}
in local coordinates
\varphi_{j}(V)\subset\R^{2}
via
\bar{f}\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}
. We can then apply
Proposition

2.18
to relate it to a local coordinate expression of
f
on a choice of orbit representatives
\psi_{j}(W_{j})\subset S^{1}\times S^{1}
:

\displaystyle\bar{f}\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\bar{f}\circ\vartheta_{ab}\circ\psi_{j}^{-1}=(\bar{f}\circ\psi_{j}^{-1})\circ\vartheta_{ab}^{-1}\rvert_{\varphi_{j}(W_{j})}=(\bar{f}\circ q\rvert_{\psi_{j}(W)})\circ\vartheta_{ab}^{-1}\rvert_{\varphi_{j}(W_{j})}

\displaystyle=f\circ\vartheta_{ab}^{-1}\rvert_{\varphi_{j}(W)}.

(22)

We see immediately if
X
is a manifold, and
f\in C^{k}(S^{1}\times S^{1},X)
, then
\bar{f}\in C^{k}(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),X)
. Expressing functions on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
in local coordinates of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
is necessary for the differential topology arguments in
Section

4
.

2.2
Morse Theory and Persistent Homology

We now briefly introduce the standard theory surrounding the fundamental areas we use to prove our results. We give a basic introduction of the terms and main ideas in persistent homology, and discuss how smooth Morse theory can help describe the persistent homology of sublevel set filtrations of manifolds with boundary by smooth functions. We also touch on ideas from Conley index theory to help describe the persistent homology of filtered simplicial complexes.

2.2.1
Persistent Homology of Sublevel Set Filtrations

Let
f:X\to\R
be a continuous function, and
X^{t}:=f^{-1}(-\infty,{t}]
be the sublevel set of
f
under
t
. Consider then following filtration of sublevel sets, indexed over
\R
. That is, a collection of inclusions maps
X^{s}\hookrightarrow X^{t}
, whenever
s\leq t
:

X^{\bullet}:(X^{s}\xhookrightarrow{s\leq t}X^{t})

(23)

Let
H_{i}(-)
denote the
i
-th singular homology functor with field coefficients
\mathbb{F}
, that takes maps between topological spaces to linear maps between their degree
i
homology groups. Passing the inclusions in the filtration
X^{\bullet}
to maps between homology groups we obtain a sequence of maps, the
i
th
-
persistence module

(
16
, sec. 1.1)
of the sublevel set filtration of
X
:

H_{i}(X^{\bullet}):\quad\left(H_{i}(X^{s})\xrightarrow{s\leq t}H_{i}(X^{t})\right)

(24)

A persistence module
\mathbb{V}
over
\mathbb{R}
is an
\R
-indexed family of vector spaces
V^{s}
, equipped with linear maps

v^{t}_{s}:V^{s}\rightarrow V^{t}

for
s\leq t
, which satisfy the commutation relation
v_{s}^{t}\circ v_{r}^{s}=v_{r}^{t}
whenever
r\leq s\leq t
, and
v_{t}^{t}=\iden_{V^{t}}
.

We say a persistent module is quadrant tame or
q-tame
, if the rank of each map
v_{s}^{t}
is finite for
s<t
. If all vector spaces
V^{t}
are finite-dimensional, then the persistence module is said to be
pointwise finite dimensional
or simply
tame
. If
X
is homeomorphic to the geometric realisation of a finite simplicial complex, and
f
is continuous, then the persistent homology of the sublevel set filtration
H_{i}(X^{\bullet})
is q-tame
(
16
, Theorem 2.22)
.

Given a persistence module, we can obtain a practically convenient invariant called a persistence diagram. We define persistence diagrams for q-tame persistence modules, following
(
16
, Prop. 2.3)
, based on the rank functions of the persistence module and the notion of persistence measures. Let
\bar{\R}=\R\cup\{\pm\infty\}
, and consider the open extended upper half plane
\mathcal{H}\subset\bar{\R}^{2}

\mathcal{H}:=\{(s,t)\ :\ -\infty\leq s<t\leq\infty\}.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of all rectangles contained in
\mathcal{H}

\mathcal{R}=\{[a,b]\times[c,d]\ :\ a<b<c<d\}.

For a persistence module
\mathbb{V}
, let
r^{t}_{s}:=\rank v^{t}_{s}
be the
rank function
that assigns to every pair
s\leq t
the rank of the linear map
v^{t}_{s}
.
The
persistence measure
of a q-tame persistence module
\mathbb{V}

(
16
, Prop. 2.3)
is then the function
\mu_{\mathbb{V}}:\mathcal{R}\to\Z
,

\mu_{\mathbb{V}}([a,b]\times[c,d])=r^{c}_{b}-r^{c}_{a}-r^{d}_{b}+r^{d}_{a},

(25)

Since the image of the composition of linear maps satisfies
\imag(fg)\cong\imag g/(\ker f\cap\imag g)
, we can write
eq.

25
in the following form, following
(
16
, Prop. 2.5)
:

\mu_{\mathbb{V}}([a,b]\times[c,d])=\dim\left(\frac{\imag v_{b}^{c}\cap\ker v_{c}^{d}}{\imag v_{a}^{c}\cap\ker v_{c}^{d}}\right).

(26)

As such, the persistence measure is non-negative. From
eq.

26
,
\mu_{\mathbb{V}}([a,b]\times[c,d])
is zero if and only if
v_{c}^{d}
is injective and
v_{a}^{b}
is surjective. We can interpret the persistence measure on
\mu_{\mathbb{V}}([a,b]\times[c,d])
as the dimension of the vector space consisting of equivalence classes of vectors that are ‘born’ between
[a,b]
in the filtration, and ‘die’ between
[c,d]
.

This interpretation of the persistence measure as an enumeration of equivalence classes of vectors in the persistence module can be made precise using the following formulation in
16
. Let
\mathbf{Dgm}
be the space of multisets of
\mathcal{H}
that are finite on each
R\in\mathcal{R}
; we call such multisets
persistence diagrams
. Consider a q-tame persistence module
\mathbb{V}
. By
(
16
, Theorem 2.8, Corr. 2.15)
, there exists a unique persistence diagram
\mathrm{dgm}(\mu_{\mathbb{V}})
, such that the measure evaluated on rectangles
R\in\mathcal{R}
is given by the cardinality of points in
\mathrm{dgm}(\mathbb{V})\cap R
:

\mu_{\mathbb{V}}(R)=\#\{(b,d)\in\mathrm{dgm}(\mathbb{V})\mid(b,d)\in R\}.

(27)

We call points
(b,d)
in
\mathrm{dgm}(\mathbb{V})
a feature, and
b
and
d
the birth and death times of the feature in the filtration respectively.

Remark 2.19
.

The standard way to define persistence diagrams of pointwise finite dimensional persistence modules over
\R
is via interval decomposition
20
. A priori, the persistent homology of sublevel set filtrations
H_{i}(X^{\bullet})
are not necessarily tame, and therefore their persistence module may not admit an interval decomposition.

Recall if we consider continuous functions on a finitely triangulable space, then the persistence module
H_{i}(X^{\bullet})
is q-tame
(
16
, Theorem 2.22)
. As such, we can consider persistent homology as a function

\pershomf_{i}:C(X,\mathbb{R})\;\longrightarrow\;\mathbf{Dgm}

which assigns to each
f\in C(X,\mathbb{R})
the diagram
\pershomf_{i}(f):=\mathrm{dgm}(H_{i}(X^{\bullet}))
, where
X^{\bullet}
is the sublevel set filtration of
X
by
f
. Following
20
, we say the a map
f

tame
if the persistence module
H_{i}(X^{\bullet})
is pointwise finite dimensional, and the persistence diagram
\pershomf_{i}(f)
is finite for all
i
. The space of persistent diagrams
\mathbf{Dgm}
admits a metric, the
bottleneck distance
(see section 4.2 of
16
for a full construction). The map
\pershomf_{i}
is in fact 1-Lipschitz with respect to the bottleneck distance.

Theorem 2.20
.

(Stability)
(
16
, Cor 4.17)
Let
X
be a triangulable space with continuous functions
f,g:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Then the persistence diagrams of the sublevel set filtrations satisfy
d_{B}(\pershomf_{i}(f),\pershomf_{i}(g))\leq\|f-g\|_{\infty}
for any integer
i
.

This property ensures that small perturbations of the input function lead to only small changes in the corresponding persistence diagram, making persistent homology a stable tool for data analysis.

Remark 2.21
.

Consider the persistence module
H_{i}(X^{\bullet})
derived from a filtration of a space
X
by a continuous function
f
. For any pair
X^{s},X^{t}
in the sequence where
s<t
, we can consider the long exact sequence for relative homology

\cdots\rightarrow H_{i}(X^{s})\rightarrow H_{i}(X^{t})\rightarrow H_{i}(X^{t},X^{s})\rightarrow H_{i-1}(X^{t})\rightarrow\cdots.

Exactness of this sequence implies

H_{i}(X^{s})\to H_{i}(X^{t})

is an isomorphism for all
i

if and only if

H_{i}(X^{t},X^{s})=0

for all
i
. We say a parameter
t
is a
homological regular value
of the filtration, if for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
we have
H_{i}(X^{t-\epsilon},X^{t+\epsilon})=0
for all
i
; else it is a
homological critical value
. Given the characterisation of persistence diagrams in
eq.

26
, only homological critical values can be a birth or death of a feature in
\mathrm{dgm}(H_{i}(X^{\bullet}))
.

2.2.2
Smooth Morse Theory for Manifold with Boundaries

For a smooth function
f\in C^{k}(M,\R)
on a manifold
M
, let us denote
M_{a}=f^{-1}(-\infty,{a}]
as the sublevel set of
f
under
a
. We recall a critical point
p
of
f
is where
\differential{f}(p)=0
. A critical point is non-degenerate, if for any local chart
\varphi:U\to\R^{m}
of
p
, the Hessian matrix
(H_{f})_{ij}=\partialderivative{(f \circ\inv{\varphi})}{x_i}{x_j}
has non-zero determinant. The Morse index
\lambda(p)
of a non-degenerate critical point is the number of negative eigenvalues of
H_{f}
. A function is called a Morse function of all its critical points are non-degenerate.

Morse theory allows us to analyse the changes in homotopy type when we include a sublevel set
M_{a}\hookrightarrow M_{a+t}
into another. We refer the reader to many excellent texts in literature for a treatment of Morse theory, such as
47
;
8
;
52
. The key insight from Morse theory is that the homotopy type of the sublevel set
M_{a+t}
only changes as
t
passes through critical values of
f
. In particular, if
f
is a Morse function, then the change in homotopy type is governed by the Morse indices of the critical points. If
[a_{-},a]
contains only one critical value
b
, lying in
(0,a]
, then
M_{a_{-}}
is homotopy equivalent to
M_{a}
with
\lambda(p)
-dimensional cells
e^{\lambda(p)}
attached along the boundary, for every critical point
p
in
f^{-1}{\left({b}\right)}
(
(
52
, Theorem 2.2.3, Remark 2.2.5)
,
(
47
, §3.2)
):

M_{a}\simeq M_{a_{-}}\cup e^{\lambda(p_{1})}\cup\cdots\cup e^{\lambda(p_{n})}.

(28)

Consequently, the relative homology of the pair
(M_{a},M_{a-})
is given by the following (
(
47
, §5)
,
(
45
, Theorem A.4.1-2)
,
(
52
, Theorem 2.2.3)
):

H_{\bullet}(M_{a},M_{a-})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{H}_{\bullet}(S^{\lambda(p_{i})}).

(29)

In our application of smooth Morse theory, we are interested in the case where the manifold has non-empty boundary. This case is treated in
58
. As our main object of study is a smooth function on the Möbius band, that is positive on the interior and zero on the boundary, we restrict ourselves to the following class of functions.

Definition 2.22
.

For
k\geq 2
, consider
f\in C^{k}(M,\R)
a non-negative function on a compact manifold with boundary
(M,\partial M)
such that
f^{-1}(0)=\partial M
. We say
f
is
Morse-supported
, if the following conditions are satisfied:

(
M1
)

Restricted to the interior
\interior{M}
of
M
, the map
f
is a Morse function (its critical points are non-degenerate);

(
M2
)

f\rvert_{\interior{M}}
has only finitely many critical points; and

(
M3
)

There is some sufficiently small
a>0
, such that for all
t\in[0,a]
, the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}=f^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
is a deformation retract of the sublevel set
f^{-1}[0,a]
.

If
0
is a regular value of
f
, then the second condition
(M3)
follows from the standard deformation lemma of Morse theory of regular level sets (see
(
8
, Theorem 3.20)
for statement in particular for manifolds with boundary). However, we state this extra condition as the boundary if the Möbius band is a critical level set of the chordal distance transform
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
with critical value 0.

We adapt the observations of
58
on persistent homology of Morse functions on manifolds with boundary for the case of Morse-supported functions in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
f
be a Morse-supported function with critical values
0\leq a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{N}=\infty
. For each critical value
a_{i}
, let
C_{i}
be the set of critical points in
f^{-1}{\left({a_{i}}\right)}
. Then:

•

For
a_{i}\leq s\leq t<a_{i+1}
,
H_{\bullet}(M_{s})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\bullet}(M_{t})
; and

•

For
s\in(a_{i-1},a_{i}]
, and
t\in(a_{i},a_{i+1})
,

H_{n}(M_{t},M_{s})\cong\bigoplus_{p\in C_{i}}\tilde{H}_{n}(S^{\lambda(p)}).

(30)

Consequently, the persistence module
H_{n}(M_{\bullet})
is pointwise finite dimensional for any
n
, and
a_{i}
are precisely the homological critical values of the filtration.

We now give a sufficient condition for
f\in C^{2}(M,\R)
having finitely many non-degenerate critical points, a necessary condition for a function being Morse-supported. Recall if
M
is compact and without boundary, then a Morse function
f
must have finitely many critical points. We can show that on a manifold with boundary, we can similarly control the cardinality of critical points on the interior of the manifold, if we can isolate them from the boundary.

Lemma 2.24
.

Consider
f\in C^{2}(M,\R)
where
M
is a compact manifold with boundary
\partial M
. If there is a neighbourhood
V
of
\partial M
on which there are no critical points of
f
on
V\setminus\partial M
, then
f
being Morse on the interior of
M
implies there are finitely many critical points in the interior of
M
.

Proof.

The proof follows that for compact manifolds without boundary. Suppose there are infinitely many critical points of
f
on the interior. Since
M
is compact we can consider a convergent sequence
(p_{n})
of such critical points, which by continuity of
\differential{f}
, must converge to some critical point
p\in M
. Because
V
isolates the boundary, this critical point must be on the interior. Since
f
is Morse on the interior,
p
is non-degenerate and hence isolated; however this contradicts there being an infinite sequence
p_{n}\to p
. Hence there cannot be infinitely many critical points on the interior.
∎

2.2.3
Morse Theory for Filtered Simplicial Complexes

Suppose
X
is a finite simplicial complex, and we have a a filtration of subcomplexes
X^{\bullet}
of
X
over
\R
. If the filtration is a sublevel set filtration of a function
f:X\to\R
which is monotone with respect to the face poset of the simplicial complex, we say
f
is a
filter function
.

Consider the sequence of inclusions

\emptyset=X^{a_{0}}\subset\cdots\subset X^{a_{i}}\subset X^{a_{n}}=X^{a_{i+1}}\subset\cdots\subset X

(31)

where
a_{0}<\cdots<a_{n}
are the finitely many values of
\R
where new simplices are added to the filtration. In this case, each sublevel set
X^{a}
is a subcomplex of
X
. In order to describe the homological critical values of the persistence module
H_{i}(X^{\bullet})
, we wish to consider how individual additions of simplices in a filtration contribute to the relative homology
H_{k}(X^{a_{i}},X^{a_{i-1}})
. We recall some observations from Conley-Morse theory that relates the relative homology to how the additional simplices are attached to an existing complex in the filtration.

Consider an inclusion of finite simplicial complexes
X^{-}\hookrightarrow X
. Let
S=X\setminus X^{-}

and denote
\mathrm{cl}(S)
to be the closure of
S
(the smallest subcomplex in
X
containing
S
). We call
L=\mathrm{cl}(S)\cap X^{-}
the
link
of
S
; that is, the faces of simplices in
S
that are in
X^{-}
rather than
S
. Borrowing terminology from combinatorial dynamics, we call connected components
S_{i}
of
S=\bigsqcup_{i}S_{i}
the
Morse sets
of the filtration, and call
L_{i}=\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\cap X^{-}
the links of the Morse set
S_{i}
. It follows from the definitions that
L_{i}
are closed in
X
and
L=\bigcup_{i}L_{i}
. We also have the following characterisation.

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
S_{i}
be a connected component of
X\setminus X^{-}
where
X^{-}
is a subcomplex of
X
a finite simplicial complex. Then
\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})=S_{i}\sqcup L_{i}
, for
L_{i}=\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\cap X^{-}
.

Proof.

Suppose there is some simplex
\sigma\in\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\setminus S_{i}
that is not in
L_{i}
. Since
L_{i}=\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\cap X^{-}
and
X
is a complex,
\sigma
must be in set
S=X\setminus X^{-}
. Furthermore since
\sigma
is in the closure of
S_{i}
,
\sigma
is connected to
S_{i}
. However, since
S_{i}
is a connected component,
\sigma
must be in
S_{i}
as well, which is a contradiction of
\sigma\in\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\setminus S_{i}
but not in
L_{i}
. Thus,
\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\setminus S_{i}\subseteq L_{i}
. On the other hand, since
S_{i}\cap X^{-}=\emptyset
, and
L_{i}\subseteq X^{-}
, we have
L_{i}\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\setminus S_{i}
. Therefore
L_{i}=\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\setminus S_{i}
.
∎

We refer to the relative homology group

\mathrm{Con}_{\bullet}(S_{i}):=H_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})

(32)

as the
Conley index
of the Morse set
S_{i}
.
If the Conley index of
S_{i}
is trivial, then we also say
S_{i}
is
regular
. Otherwise we say
S_{i}
is a
critical set
.
In particular, if
H_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})\cong\tilde{H}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, then we call the Morse set
S_{i}
an
n
-saddle
in the spirit of smooth Morse theory. We let
\lambda(S_{i})=n
denote the Morse index of the saddle. We note that if
\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})
is contractible, then the Conley index of
S_{i}
is characterised by the homology of its link: by the reduced homology long exact sequence of the pair
(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})
we have an isomorphism for homology degree
k\geq 1
:

{H}_{k}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})\cong\tilde{H}_{k}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})\xrightarrow{\cong}\tilde{H}_{k-1}(L_{i}).

(33)

For
k=0
, if
L_{i}
is empty, then
H_{0}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})=\mathbb{F}
; else
H_{0}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})=0
.

Using excision-type arguments, we relate the relative homology
H_{\bullet}(X,X^{-})
to the Conley indices.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
(X,X^{-})
be a pair of finite simplicial complexes. Then the inclusion of pairs
(\mathrm{cl}(S),L)\hookrightarrow(X,X^{-})
induces an isomorphism

\bigoplus_{i}\mathrm{Con}_{\bullet}(S_{i})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\bullet}(X,X^{-}).

(34)

where
S_{i}
are connected components of
X\setminus X^{-}
.

Proof.

Consider relative simplicial chain complexes
C_{\bullet}(X,X^{-})
and
C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S),L)
. We can check by direct computation that the inclusion of pairs
(\mathrm{cl}(S),L)\hookrightarrow(X,X^{-})
induces an isomorphism of chain complexes
C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S),L)\xrightarrow{\cong}C_{\bullet}(X,X^{-})
. Furthermore, we can also check by direct computation that
C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S),L)
is isomorphic to a direct sum of relative chain complexes associated to the Morse sets and their links
\bigoplus_{i}C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})
. To show this, we first note that for
i\neq j
, because
S_{i}\cap x_{j}=\emptyset
, we have

\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\cap\mathrm{cl}(x_{j})=(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\setminus S_{i})\cap(\mathrm{cl}(x_{j})\setminus x_{j})=L_{i}\cap L_{j}.

Hence if we consider the relative Mayer-Vietoris short exact sequence of chain complexes

0\to C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\cap\mathrm{cl}(x_{j}),L_{i}\cap L_{j})\to C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})\oplus C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(x_{j}),L_{j})\to C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\cup\mathrm{cl}(x_{j}),L_{i}\cup L_{j})\to 0,

The fact that
C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\cap\mathrm{cl}(x_{j}),L_{i}\cap L_{j})
is trivial implies we have an isomorphism of chain complexes

C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})\oplus C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(x_{j}),L_{j})\xrightarrow{\cong}C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})\cup\mathrm{cl}(x_{j}),L_{i}\cup L_{j}).

Inductively we thus have an isomorphism of chain complexes
\bigoplus_{i}C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S_{i}),L_{i})\to C_{\bullet}(\mathrm{cl}(S),L)
, inducing an isomorphism of homology groups.
∎

As such, if all critical Morse sets of a filtration are saddles, then the rank of
H_{n}(X,X^{-})
is given by the number of
n
-saddles. Via the relative homology long exact sequence, we can thus relate the contribution of each Morse set to a homological change
H_{\bullet}(X^{-})\to H_{\bullet}(X)
in one degree, of rank precisely one. We distinguish this filtration with the following nomenclature.

Remark 2.27
.

In the framework of Conley-Morse theory for combinatorial dynamics, the partition of the complex
X
into
S,X^{-}
, and
S
into a disjoint union
S_{i}
, is a
combinatorial multivector field
; that is, a partition of the complex into locally closed subsets. The individual partition elements
S_{i}
of
X^{-}
are called
multi-vectors
, and the link is also called a mouth. Our definition of the Conley index coincides with theirs; we refer the reader to
(
49
, §2)
for an extensive account of Conley theory in the combinatorial setting.

2.3
Differential Topology

Function Space Topologies

We now define the weak and strong (Whitney) topology on
C^{k}(M,N)
, following
(
35
, §2.1)
. We denote by
C^{k}_{W}(M,N)
and
C^{k}_{S}(M,N)
the space of functions endowed with weak and strong topology respectively.

We use introduce a (sub)base to define these topologies. Let
M,N
be
C^{k}
manifolds. Consider local charts
\varphi:U\to\R^{m}
and
\psi:V\to\R^{n}
of
U\subset M
and
V\subset N
, and
K\subset U
compact. If
f\in C^{k}(M,N)
is such that
f(K)\subset V
, consider maps
g\in C^{k}(M,N)
, such that
g(K)\subset V
, and for
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\norm{D^r(\psi f \inv{\varphi})\rvert_x - D^r(\psi g \inv{\varphi})\rvert_x}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon

\displaystyle\forall x\in K,\quad r=0,\ldots,k.

(35)

Then the
weak subbasic neighbourhood
of
f

(
35
, P. 35, §2.1)
is defined as

\mathcal{N}(f;(\varphi,U),(\psi,V),K,\epsilon):=\{g\in C^{k}(M,N)\ :\ g(K)\subset V,\ \text{ and $g$ satisfies \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:jet_nbhd_property}}\}.

(36)

The
weak
(or
C^{k}
compact-open) topology on
C^{k}(M,N)
has the collection
\{\mathcal{N}(f;(\varphi,U),(\psi,V),K,\epsilon)\}
of subsets of maps (varying over
U,V
open,
K
compact,
\epsilon>0
) as a subbase. In other words, any
f
has a neighbourhood consisting of finitely many intersections of weak subbasic neighbourhood. A sequence of maps
(f_{i})
in
C^{k}(M,N)
converges to another
f
in
C^{k}(M,N)
in weak topology, if for any
W
that is a finite intersection of weak subbasic neighbourhoods, there is some
j
such that
f_{i}\in W
for
i\geq j
. In other words, convergence in weak topology is equivalent to requiring for any choice of
K
compact, and compatible charts
\varphi,\psi
, the
uniform convergence
of
D^{r}(\psi f_{i}\varphi^{-1})
to
D^{r}(\psi f\varphi^{-1})
on the compact subset
\varphi(K)\subset\R^{m}
, for
r=0,\ldots,k
.

On the other hand, the
Whitney
or
strong
topology on
C^{k}(M,N)
is given by a base consisting of subsets of the form
\bigcap_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{N}(f;(\varphi_{i},U_{i}),(\psi,V_{i}),K_{i},\epsilon_{i})
, where
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
and
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
are locally finite covers of
M
and
N
. Since the index set is only locally finite, the strong topology is finer than the weak topology. Convergence in strong topology is a more stringent condition, requiring maps to coincide everywhere but a compact subset
K
, and converge uniformly on such
K
(see
(
41
, 41.7 & 41.10)
). However, if
M
is compact, then the weak and strong topologies coincide
(
35
, §2.1)
.

Remark 2.28
.

If the target manifold
N
is some Euclidean space
\R^{n}
, then we let
\mathcal{N}(f;(\varphi,U),K,\epsilon)
denote the weak subbasic neighbourhood where
\psi
is the identity on
\R^{n}
and the neighbourhood
V
consists of maps
g
s.t.
\norm{f-g}<\epsilon
on all
K
.

Remark 2.29
.

We recall some salient further facts about topological properties of subsets of
C^{r}_{S}(M,N)
. We recall a
residual
subset of a topological space is a countable intersection of open dense subsets.

1.

C^{r}_{S}(M,N)
is a Baire space: any residual subset is dense (
(
35
, Theorem 2.4.4)
).

2.

For
r\geq 1
, the set of
C^{r}
embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}^{r}({M},{N})
is open in
C^{r}_{S}(M,N)
(
(
35
, Theorem 2.1.4)
).

3.

If
U
is an open set of manifold
M
, the restriction
f\mapsto f\rvert_{U}
as a function from
C^{r}(M,N)
to
C^{r}(U,N)
is continuous for the weak topology, but not necessarily for the strong topology (
(
35
, Problem 2.1.16)
).

Jets

We can also describe the weak and strong topologies on
C^{r}(M,N)
in terms of
jets
, which is a convenient framework for manipulating derivatives of smooth maps between manifolds. We follow the exposition of
(
28
, §2)
and
(
35
, §4)
and refer the reader to them for a full formal exposition. Let
M
and
N
be
C^{k}
manifolds; for
x\in M
and
y\in N
, consider the set of maps

C^{k}({M},{N})_{x,y}=\Bqty{f \in\FSpace{k}{M}{N} \ : \ f(x) = y}.

(37)

For
r\leq k
a positive integer, and (any and all) local charts
\varphi:(U,x)\to(\R^{m},0)
and
\psi:(V,y)\to(\R^{n},0)
at
x,y
respectively, consider the equivalence relation on
C^{k}({M},{N})_{x,y}

f\sim_{x,r}g\quad\mbox{if}\quad D^{j}{(\psi f\varphi^{-1})}\rvert_{0}=D^{j}{(\psi g\varphi^{-1})}\rvert_{0}\quad\forall\ j\in 0,\ldots,r.

(38)

Note that the equivalence relation does not depend on the choice of charts. We define the set of
r
-jets
J^{r}({M},{N})_{x,y}
from
x
to
y
, to be
C^{k}({M},{N})_{x,y}/\sim_{r}
, and the set of
r
-jets from
M
to
N
to be

J^{r}({M},{N})=\bigsqcup_{x\in M,\ y\in N}J^{r}({M},{N})_{x,y}.

(39)

We can write any element of
J^{r}({M},{N})
as
(x,y,[f]_{x,r})
, where
x
and
y
are respectively the sources and targets of
f
, and
[f]_{x,r}
is the equivalence class of
f
under
\sim_{x,r}
. For any
f\in C^{k}({M},{N})
, the
r
-
prolongation
of
f
is the map
j^{r}f\in C^{k-r}({M},{J^{r}({M},{N})})
, where
j^{r}f(x)=(x,f(x),[f]_{x,r})
. We equip
J^{r}({M},{N})
with the topology where open sets are
J^{r}({U},{V})=\bigsqcup_{x\in U,y\in V}J^{r}({M},{N})_{x,y}
for
U\subseteq M
,
V\subseteq N
open.

We now explicitly derive a canonical atlas of
J^{r}({M},{N})
given atlases

\varphi_{a}:U_{a}\to\Bqty{\varphi_a: U_a \to\R^m}^{m}
and
\psi_{b}:V_{b}\to\Bqty{\psi_b: V_b \to\R^n}^{n}
of
M
and
N
respectively. For
(x,y,[f])\in J^{r}({U_{a}},{V_{b}})
, we can specify
[f]
as the truncated Taylor polynomial expansion of
f_{ab}:=\psi_{b}f\varphi_{a}^{-1}:\R^{m}\to\R^{n}
at
\varphi(x)
to order
r
. Let
A^{j}_{m}
be the
\R
-vector space of real polynomials in
m
-variables of degree
\leq j
with constant term = 0, and define
B^{j}_{m,n}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A^{j}_{m}
. We have a bijection
\zeta_{ab}:J^{r}({U_{a}},{V_{b}})\to\varphi_{a}(U_{a})\times\psi_{b}(V_{b})\times B^{j}_{m,n}
, where

(x,y,[f]_{x,r})\mapsto(\varphi_{a}(x),\psi_{b}(y),Df_{ab}\rvert_{\varphi(x)},\ldots,D^{r}f_{ab}\rvert_{\varphi(x)}).

(40)

In fact, with the topology we have chosen for
J^{r}({M},{N})
,
\zeta_{ab}
is a homeomorphism between
J^{r}({U_{a}},{V_{b}})
and a Euclidean space. The collection of maps
\Bqty{\zeta_{ab}, \Jet{r}{}{U_a}{V_b})}
satisfy the axioms for an atlas, as such this imposes a manifold structure for
J^{r}({M},{N})
(we refer the reader to
(
28
, §2)
and
(
35
, §4)
for details).

Since jets carry information about higher order derivatives, they can also be used to define the weak and strong
C^{k}
topologies. The prolongation of
f\in C^{k}({M},{N})
as an assignment from
C^{k}({M},{N})
to the set of continuous maps between
M
and
J^{k}({M},{N})
.

\displaystyle j^{k}:C^{k}({M},{N})

\displaystyle\to C^{0}({M},{J^{k}({M},{N})})

(41)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto j^{k}f.

The strong (weak) topology on
C^{k}({M},{N})
is precisely
the coarsest topology such that prolongations
j^{k}
are continuous, when
C^{0}({M},{J^{k}({M},{N})})
is endowed with the strong (weak) topology
(
41
, 41.9-10)
.

Multi-jets

The set of
s
-fold
r
-jets of
C^{k}({M},{N})
, is defined to be

J^{r}_{s}({M},{N}):=\{(j^{r}f(x_{1})\ldots,j^{r}f(x_{s}))\ :\ f\in C^{k}({M},{N}),(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)\}.

(42)

The set of
s
-fold
r
-jets is a submanifold of
J^{r}({M},{N})^{s}
, where the charts are inherited from those of
J^{r}({M},{N})^{s}
as a product manifold:

\Bqty{(\zeta_{a_1, b_1}, \ldots, \zeta_{a_s, b_s}), \Jet{r}{}{U_{a_1}}{V_{b_1}} \times\ldots\times\Jet{r}{}{U_{a_s}}{V_{b_s}}}.

We can similarly prolong
f\in C^{k}({M},{N})
to an
n
-fold
r
-jet
j^{r}_{s}f:\mathrm{Conf}_{s}(M)\to J^{r}_{s}({M},{N})
:

j^{r}_{s}f:(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\mapsto(j^{r}f(x_{1})\ldots,j^{r}f(x_{s})).

(43)

Transversality

We follow the exposition on transversality in
(
35
, §2)
and
(
28
, §4)
. Let
M
and
N
be differentiable manifolds. A differentiable map
f
intersects a submanifold
W
of
N
transversely on
V\subseteq M
, if either
f(V)\cap W=\emptyset
, or where
x\in V
is mapped into
W
,

T_{f(x)}W+\differential{f}_{x}(T_{x}M)=T_{f(x)}M.

(44)

We denote this by
f\pitchfork_{V}W
. If
V=M
, then we simply write
f\pitchfork W
. If
W
is a Whitney stratified space, then we say
f\pitchfork_{V}W
if
f\pitchfork_{V}W_{i}
as manifolds for all strata
W_{i}
.

Note that if
\rank\differential{f}
is less than the codimension of
W
in
N
where
f(M)\cap W
, then the
f\pitchfork W
implies
f(M)\cap W=\emptyset
. We exploit this fact in the proof of
Proposition

4.15
, where the genericity of a transversality condition implies the genericity of a property defined by an empty intersection due to this dimensionality argument.

The main theorem in transversality theory that we exploit in this paper is the following by Damon
(
21
, Corollary 1.11)
; we state a specific version of the theorem as presented in
5
(Theorem 9.1).

Theorem 2.30

(Damon)
.

Consider
k\geq r+1
and
s\geq 2
. Let
M,N
be
C^{k}
manifolds where
M
is compact, and
V\subset\mathrm{Conf}_{s}(M)
and
W\subset J^{r}_{s}({M},{N})
be closed Whitney stratified subsets. Then the following subset of
C^{k}({M},{N})
with Whitney
C^{k}
-topology is
residual
:

\Gamma=\Bqty{f \in\FSpace{k}{M}{N} \ : \ \prolong{r}{s}f \transverse_V W}.

(45)

Furthermore, if
V
is compact, then
\Gamma
is also open in Whitney
C^{k+1}
-topology.

Since
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({M},{N})
is open in
C^{k}_{S}(M,N)

(
41
, 5.3)
, if
\Gamma
is residual in
C^{k}_{S}(M,N)
, then
\Gamma\cap\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({M},{N})
is also residual in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({M},{N})
.

2.4
Subsets of Positive Reach

The reach of a closed subset
A\subset\R^{d}
of Euclidean space was introduced by
25
to quantify the size of tubular neighbourhoods of
A
. Let
A^{\epsilon}=\{x\in\R^{d}\ :\ d(x,A)\leq\epsilon\}
denote the
\epsilon
-neighbourhood of
A
in
\R^{d}
. A tubular neighbourhood of
A
is an
\epsilon
-neighbourhood of
A
, such that for any point
x
in the
A^{\epsilon}
has a unique nearest neighbour on
A
. The
reach
of
A
, denoted by
\tau
, is the supremum over all
\epsilon
’s such that
A^{\epsilon}
is a tubular neighbourhood of
A
. If
\tau>0
, we say
A
has
positive reach
.

Subsets of positive reach include convex subsets (where
\tau=\infty
), and compact
C^{2}
-submanifolds
(
25
, Remarks 4.20-21)
. We recall some results in literature where
\tau
is shown to be a useful characteristic length scale of a closed subset
A
, below which the local geometry of
A
can be well approximated by Euclidean data. First, we note a result from
12
, which describes how Euclidean distances bound the geodesic distance
d_{A}
in
A
, as induced by the ambient Euclidean distance.

Lemma 2.31

(
(
12
, Lemma 3)
)
.

If
A\subset\R^{d}
is a closed set, and
p,q\in A
have Euclidean distance
\norm{p-q}<2\tau
, then

\norm{p-q}\leq d_{A}(p,q)\leq 2\tau\arcsin\frac{\norm{q-p}}{2\tau}.

(46)

In Federer’s introduction of the reach
25
, he shows that the reach also controls the rate at which a tangent plane approximation of a manifold at a point deteriorates as we increase the size of the neighbourhood.

Theorem 2.32

(
(
25
, Theorem 4.18)
)
.

Let
\tau
be the reach of a a submanifold
M\subset\R^{d}
. For
T_{p}
the tangent plane of
M
at
p
, the distance of
q\in M
to
T_{p}
is bounded above by

d(q,T_{p})\leq\frac{\norm{q-p}^{2}}{2\tau}.

(47)

Note that
d(q,T_{p})
is the length of the orthogonal projection of the vector
q-p
onto the plane
-p+T_{p}
. Since
d(q,T_{p})\leq\norm{q-p}
, the bound is only meaningful when
\norm{q-p}\leq 2\tau
.

In the case where
M=\gamma(S^{1})
is the image of a
C^{1}
-embedding of
S^{1}
, we can obtain the following expression which is useful in controlling the critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. Since
d(q,T_{p})=\norm{p-q}|\sin\measuredangle(q-p,\dot{\gamma}(p))|
,

\left\langle{\dot{\gamma}(p)},{\gamma(q)-\gamma(p)}\right\rangle^{2}=\norm{\dot{\gamma}(p)}^{2}{\|\gamma(q)-\gamma(p)\|}^{2}\cos^{2}\measuredangle(q-p,\dot{\gamma}(p))\geq 1-\quantity(\frac{\|\gamma(q) - \gamma(p)\|}{2\tau})^{2}.

(48)

3
General Properties of the Persistent Homology of Chordal Distance Transforms

In this section, we give some general properties of CDT for general topological embeddings of circles. We show that
\mathcal{T}:\mathrm{Emb}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})\to C^{k}(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
is a continuous transform of circle embeddings into functions that satisfy favourable symmetry properties. We also discuss how the choice of field coefficients for persistent homology can affect the persistence diagrams
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
.

We first note that any property we prove about the persistence diagram
\pershomf\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
also holds for
\pershomf(\mathsf{T}_{\gamma})
, as there is a bijection between the diagrams.

Lemma 3.1
.

The homeomorphism
\Phi:\R^{2}_{\geq 0}\to\R^{2}_{\geq 0}

given by

\Phi:(s,t)\mapsto(\frac{1}{2}s^{2},\frac{1}{2}t^{2})

induces a bijection between the persistence diagrams

\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}(\gamma)
and

\pershomf\circ\mathsf{T}(\gamma)
.

Proof.

By definition,
the sublevel sets

\{w\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\ :\ \mathsf{T}_{\gamma}(w)\leq t\}

and

\{w\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\ :\ \mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(w)\leq\frac{1}{2}t^{2}\}

are equal for all
t\geq 0
,
hence the corresponding persistence modules, and thus their diagrams, only differ by a monotonic
reparametrisation
s\mapsto s^{2}/2
of
[0,\infty)
.
∎

3.1
Continuity

We first show that if
\gamma:S^{1}\to X
is continuous, then
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}
are also continuous functions on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. We respectively endow
C(S^{1},X)
and
C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
with the metrics

d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})=\sup_{z\in S^{1}}d_{X}(\gamma(z),\tilde{\gamma}(z)),\quad d(f,\tilde{f})=\sup_{\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})}|f(\{z_{1},z_{2}\})-\tilde{f}(\{z_{1},z_{2}\}|.

These metrics metricise the weak (a.k.a compact-open) topologies of
C(S^{1},X)
and
C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
respectively.
As a simple application of the triangle inequality, we can show that
\mathsf{T}
is Lipschitz continuous.

Lemma 3.2
.

The distance transforms

\mathsf{T},\mathcal{T}:C(S^{1},X)\to C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
are continuous maps between function spaces. In particular,
\mathsf{T}
is a 2-Lipschitz map.

Proof.

Consider

d(\mathsf{T}\gamma,\mathsf{T}\tilde{\gamma})=\sup_{\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})}|d_{X}(\gamma(z_{1}),\gamma(z_{2}))-d_{X}(\tilde{\gamma}(z_{1}),\tilde{\gamma}(z_{2}))|.

The term within the supremum can be bounded via the application of the triangle inequality twice, and obtain that
\mathsf{T}
is 2-Lipschitz continuous:

\displaystyle d(\mathsf{T}\gamma,\mathsf{T}\tilde{\gamma})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})}(d_{X}(\gamma(z_{1}),\tilde{\gamma}(z_{1}))+d_{X}(\gamma(z_{2}),\tilde{\gamma}(z_{2})))

\displaystyle=2\sup_{z\in S^{1}}d_{X}(\gamma(z),\tilde{\gamma}(z))=2d(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma}).

Since
\mathcal{T}
is the composition of
\mathsf{T}
with a continuous map it follows that
\mathcal{T}
is also continuous.
∎

Having defined a continuous function
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}:\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\to\R
, we can construct a sublevel set filtration and consider its persistent homology. Since
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
is a manifold with boundary and thus finitely triangularisable, continuous functions on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
always have
q
-tame persistent homology
(
16
, Theorem 2.22)
. Thus, we can consider the persistence diagrams
\pershomf_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
in the metric space of diagrams
\mathbf{Dgm}
, equipped with bottleneck distance. Because the map
\pershomf
is a continuous transformation of functions to the space of diagrams from
Lemma

3.2

and by the stability theorem (
theorem

2.20
), the composition of
\mathcal{T}:C(S^{1},X)\to C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
with
\pershomf_{i}
yields a continuous function
\pershomf_{i}\circ\mathcal{T}
from embeddings to persistence diagrams.

Corollary 3.3
.

\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}:C(S^{1},X)\to\mathbf{Dgm}
continuous w.r.t. bottleneck distance on
\mathbf{Dgm}
. Furthermore,
\pershomf\circ\mathsf{T}:C(S^{1},X)\to\mathbf{Dgm}
is 2-Lipschitz.

3.2
Symmetry invariance

As
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is purely a function of distance between points in Euclidean space, it is an isometry invariant: any isometry
\omega:\R^{d}\to\R^{d}
leaves the chordal distance transform invariant. If
\gamma
is transformed by an isometry
\alpha:\R^{d}\to\R^{d}
of Euclidean space, then the transformed map
\alpha\circ\gamma:S^{1}\to\R^{d}
has the same chordal distance transform as
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}=\mathcal{T}_{\alpha\circ\gamma}
only depends on distances. Explicitly,

\mathcal{T}_{\alpha\circ\gamma}(\{z_{1},z_{2}\})=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\alpha\circ\gamma(z_1) - \alpha\circ\gamma(z_2)}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\gamma(z_1) - \gamma(z_2)}^{2}=\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(\{z_{1},z_{2}\}).

(49)

Importantly for application, these observations mean that two different circle embeddings do not need to be centred nor aligned by rotation in the ambient Euclidean space in order to obtain geometric features that meaningfully compare their geometry.

However, the chordal distance transform is nonetheless sensitive to reparametrisations of
\gamma
. By
reparametrisation
, we mean a homeomorphism
\omega:S^{1}\to S^{1}
. Under this transformation, we can re-parametrise the curve
\gamma\circ\omega:S^{1}\to\R^{d}
, and obtain a different embedding
\gamma\circ\omega
that has the same image, but potentially different chordal distance transform:
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma\circ\omega}
may not be the same as
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. Nonetheless, because sublevel sets of the functions
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma\circ\omega}
are
homeomorphic
, the persistent homology of chordal distance transform is invariant with respect to parametrisation.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\omega:S^{1}\to S^{1}
be a homeomorphism. Then
\omega
induces an isomorphism between persistence modules of the sublevel sets filtrations of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma\circ\omega}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. Consequently,
\pershomf_{\bullet}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma\circ\omega})=\pershomf_{\bullet}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
.

Proof.

By the universal property of quotient maps, the homeomorphism which
\omega
induces on the product space
\omega\times\omega:S^{1}\times S^{1}\to S^{1}\times S^{1}
also induces a homeomorphism
\tilde{\omega}:\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\to\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, given by

\tilde{\omega}(\{z_{1},z_{2}\})=\{\omega(z_{1}),\omega(z_{2})\}.

(50)

We can then write
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma\circ\omega}=\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\circ\tilde{\omega}
. This implies for any point
\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
where
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma\circ\omega}\leq a
, there is another point
\tilde{\omega}(\{z_{1},z_{2}\})
where
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is less than
a
, and vice versa. In other words,
\tilde{\omega}:\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\to\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
restricts to a homeomorphism between sublevel sets of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma\circ\omega}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
at the same level. Because
\tilde{\omega}
is a homeomorphism on the whole of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, by restriction we have commutative diagrams where the vertical maps are homomorphisms:

\hbox to172.83pt{\vbox to54.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 86.41595pt\lower-27.3206pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(51)

Applying the singular homology functor to this diagram, we obtain an isomorphism between the resulting persistence modules of the sublevel sets filtrations of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma\circ\omega}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. Hence the map
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}:C^{k}(S^{1},\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbf{Dgm}

is invariant to homeomorphic reparametrisations of
S^{1}
.
∎

We summarise these invariant properties of
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}
with the following proposition.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{T},\mathsf{T}:\mathrm{Emb}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})\to C(\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}),\R)
be the chordal distance transforms on topological embeddings of circles in Euclidean space. If
\alpha:S^{1}\to S^{1}
is a homeomorphism, and
\omega:\R^{d}\to\R^{d}
is a Euclidean isometry, then the feature maps
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}
and
\pershomf\circ\mathsf{T}
are invariant with respect to such transformations:

\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\omega\circ\gamma\circ\alpha})=\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}),\quad\pershomf(\mathsf{T}_{\omega\circ\gamma\circ\alpha})=\pershomf(\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}).

(52)

3.3
Dependency of Persistent Homology on coefficient field.

As the Möbius band is homotopic to a circle,
it has relative homology groups

as

if

0

.

showing the upper bound in Proposition
