is not always an equality.

4
Smooth Circle Embeddings in Euclidean Space

We now restrict to the case where the loop is smoothly mapped to some Euclidean space
\gamma\in C^{k}(S^{1},\R^{d})
. In
Proposition

4.1
, we show that
\mathcal{T}
is a continuous map from
C^{k}(S^{1},\R^{d})
to
C^{k}(\mathcal{M},\R)
. The other results of this section focus on embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}^{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})\subset C^{k}(S^{1},\R^{d})
of
S^{1}
into Euclidean space. In
Section

4.2
, we relate non-degenerate critical points of the distance function
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
of a smooth embedding
\gamma
to local geometric quantities of the
\gamma
, such as local curvature. Furthermore, we show that local geometric properties of
\gamma
determine the Morse index of such critical points. We then show in
Section

4.1
that our analysis of critical points in
Section

4.2
applies to a
generic
(open dense) set of smooth embeddings, on which their distance functions are Morse-supported (
Definition

2.22
). This result is summarised in
Theorem

4.3
. We address the open and dense criteria separately in
Sections

4.1.2
and
4.1.3
respectively.

For the technical discussions in this section, we often invoke the fact that we can choose an atlas for
\mathcal{M}
such that is compatible with an atlas for
S^{1}
, which we have shown in
Proposition

2.18
. This allows us to manipulate derivatives of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
in terms of derivatives of
\gamma
, in some shared set of local coordinates. We recall since
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is induced by a symmetric function on
S^{1}\times S^{1}
, we have an atlas
\Theta=\left\{{(\vartheta_{i},U_{i})}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of
S^{1}
, and an atlas
\Phi=\left\{{(\varphi_{j},W_{j})}\right\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
of
\mathcal{M}
, such that on
\varphi_{j}(W_{j})\subset\R^{2}
, there are
i_{1},i_{2}\in\mathcal{I}
where

\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}{\left({t_{1},t_{2}}\right)}=\norm{\gamma\circ\fibre{\vartheta_{i_1}}{t_1} - \gamma\circ\fibre{\vartheta_{i_2}}{t_2}}^{2}

(53)

Since
\gamma\in C^{k}(S^{1},\R^{d})
, the derivatives of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}
are also
k
-times continuously differentiable, so
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\in C^{k}(\mathcal{M},\R)
.

Given these atlases, we can compute the gradient and Hessian of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
in local coordinates. Abusing notation, for local coordinates
(t_{1},t_{2})
on
W_{j}\subset\mathcal{M}
, we write

\partialderivative{\DT_\gamma}{t_a}:=\partialderivative{t_a}( \DT_\gamma\circ\inv{\varphi_j}),

(54)

and similarly for higher derivatives. Likewise for
t
a local coordinate for
U_{i}\subset S^{1}
, we write

\gamma(t):=(\gamma\circ\vartheta_{i}^{-1})(t),\quad\dot{\gamma}(t):=\derivative{t}(\gamma\circ\vartheta_{i}^{-1})\mbox{\quad and\quad}\ddot{\gamma}(t):=\derivative[2]{t}(\gamma\circ\vartheta_{i}^{-1}).

(55)

With this notation, we can express the gradient and Hessian as follows:

\displaystyle\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \pdv{t_1} \DT_\gamma\\ \pdv{t_2}\DT_\gamma\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \innerprod{\dot{\gamma}(t_1)}{ \gamma(t_1)- \gamma(t_2)} \\ \innerprod{\dot{\gamma}(t_2)}{ \gamma(t_2)- \gamma(t_1)} \endlx@physics@matrix)

(56)

\displaystyle\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\pdv[2]{\DT_\gamma}{t_1} & \pdv{\DT_\gamma}{t_1}{t_2} \\
\pdv{\DT_\gamma}{t_1}{t_2} & \pdv[2]{\DT_\gamma}{t_2}\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix p( \gamma(t_1)- \gamma(t_2), \dot{\gamma}(t_1), \ddot{\gamma}(t_1)) & r(\dot{\gamma}(t_1), \dot{\gamma}(t_2))\\
r(\dot{\gamma}(t_1), \dot{\gamma}(t_2)) & q(\gamma(t_1)- \gamma(t_2), \dot{\gamma}(t_2), \ddot{\gamma}(t_2)) \endlx@physics@matrix),

(57)

\displaystyle\text{where }\ p(v,x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\norm{x_1'}^{2}+\langle{x_{1}^{\prime\prime}},{v}\rangle

(58)

\displaystyle q(v,x_{2}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\norm{x_2'}^{2}-\langle{x_{2}^{\prime\prime}},{v}\rangle

\displaystyle r(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\langle{x_{1}^{\prime}},{x_{2}^{\prime}}\rangle.

The compatibility of atlases
\Theta
and
\Phi
allow us to relate the Whitney-
C^{k}
topologies on
C^{k}(S^{1},\R^{d})
and
C^{k}(\mathcal{M},\R)
. Recall from
Section

2.3
that Whitney topology of smooth functions on compact manifolds is defined via uniform convergence of derivatives relative to (any) choice of charts on compact subsets of the manifold. By relating derivatives of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
on a chart from
\Phi
to that of
\gamma
on a chart from
\Theta
, we are able to show that
\mathcal{T}
is continuous under Whitney topology.

Proposition 4.1
.

\mathcal{T}:C^{k}(S^{1},\R^{d})\to C^{k}(\mathcal{M},\R)
is continuous under Whitney
C^{k}
-topology.

Proof.

We want to show for each open subset
\mathsf{U}\subset C^{k}(\mathcal{M},\R)
, the preimage
\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}}\right)}
is open in
C^{k}(S^{1},\R)
. It suffices to show that for any
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\in\mathsf{U}
, and a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
\mathsf{U}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})\subseteq U
, there is a some neighbourhood
\mathsf{V}(\gamma)
of
\gamma
such chat
\mathsf{V}(\gamma)\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}(\gamma)}\right)}
. If that is so, then we can write

\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}}\right)}=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}}\right)}}\mathsf{V}(\gamma),

and thus
\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}}\right)}
is open.

Consider the charts
\Theta=\left\{{(\theta_{i},U_{i})}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of
S^{1}
, and an atlas
\Phi=\left\{{(\varphi_{j},W_{j})}\right\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
of
\mathcal{M}
, as described in
Proposition

2.18
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is compact, the weak and strong topologies coincide. Thus, an open neighbourhood
\mathsf{U}
of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
in
C^{k}(\mathcal{M},\R)
can be expressed as a finite intersection of weak subbasic neighbourhoods
eq.

36

\mathsf{U}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})=\bigcap_{a=1}^{m}\mathsf{U}_{a},\quad\mathsf{U}_{a}=\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma},(\varphi_{a},W_{a}),(\iden_{\R^{d}},O_{a}),K_{a},\epsilon_{a})

(59)

We note that the restriction that the image of maps in the neighbourhood be within
O_{a}\subset\R
is subsumed by the bound
\norm{ f - \DT_\gamma}<\epsilon_{a}
on
K_{a}
. be within We claim we can find a neighbourhood

\mathsf{V}_{a}=\mathcal{N}({\gamma},(\theta_{b},U_{b}),(\iden_{\R^{d}},V_{b}),Q_{b},\delta_{a})\cap\mathcal{N}({\gamma},(\theta_{c},U_{c}),(\iden_{\R^{d}},V_{c}),Q_{c},\delta_{a}),

(60)

such that
\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{V}_{a})\subset\mathsf{U}_{a}
. If that is so, then we the following finite intersection of open sets is an open set satisfying
\mathsf{V}(\gamma)\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}(\gamma)}\right)}
:

\mathsf{V}(\gamma):=\bigcap_{a=1}^{m}\mathsf{V}_{a}\subset\bigcap_{a=1}^{m}\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}_{a}}\right)}=\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}(\gamma)}\right)}.

We now prove the existence of
\mathsf{V}_{a}
such that
\mathsf{V}_{a}\subset\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}_{a}}\right)}
. We first recall from the definition of the weak subbasic neighbourhood, that
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}\in\mathsf{U}_{a}
, iff for all
(t_{1},t_{2})\in K_{a}\subset\varphi_{a}(W_{a})
, and
r=0,\ldots,k

\norm{D^r (\DT_\gamma\circ\inv{\varphi_a})\rvert_{({t_1,t_2})} - D^r(\DT_{\tilde{\gamma}} \circ\inv{\varphi_a})\rvert_{({t_1,t_2})} }<\epsilon_{a}

(61)

Because charts
\Theta
and
\Phi
are coupled in the manner described in
Proposition

2.18
, there is a pair of charts
(\theta_{b},U_{b})
and
(\theta_{c},U_{c})
in
\Theta
, such that for
(t_{1},t_{2})\in\varphi_{a}(W_{a})\subset\theta_{b}(U_{b})\times\theta_{c}(U_{c})=:\vartheta_{bc}(U_{bc})
,

\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\circ\varphi_{a}^{-1}{\left({t_{1},t_{2}}\right)}=\norm{\gamma\circ\fibre{\theta_{b}}{t_1} - \gamma\circ\fibre{\theta_{c}}{t_2}}^{2}.

(62)

We make two observations.
First, since
K_{a}\subset\varphi_{a}(W_{a})\subset\vartheta_{bc}(U_{bc})
, we can obtain subsets
Q_{b}\subset\theta_{b}(U_{b})
and
Q_{c}\subset\theta_{c}(U_{c})
, which are projections of
K_{a}
onto the respective factors, which are compact as
K_{a}
is compact and projections are continuous. Furthermore,
K_{a}\subseteq Q_{b}\times Q_{c}
. Second, we observe that
D^{r}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\circ\varphi_{a}^{-1})
is a polynomial in
D^{l}(\gamma\circ\theta_{b}^{-1})
and
D^{l}(\gamma\circ\theta_{c}^{-1})
for
l=0,\ldots,r
. Hence there is some sufficiently small
\delta_{a}>0
, such that if for
r=0,\ldots,k

\displaystyle\norm{D^r(\gamma\circ\inv{\theta_b})\rvert_x-D^r(\tilde{\gamma} \circ\inv{\theta_b})\rvert_t }

\displaystyle<\delta_{a},\quad\forall x\in Q_{b}

\displaystyle\norm{D^r(\gamma\circ\inv{\theta_c})\rvert_x-D^r(\tilde{\gamma} \circ\inv{\theta_c})\rvert_t }

\displaystyle<\delta_{a},\quad\forall x\in Q_{c},

then the condition in
eq.

61
for
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}\subset\mathsf{U}_{a}
is satisfied. The set of
\tilde{\gamma}
satisfying these conditions are precisely those in the neighbourhood
\mathsf{V}_{a}
, as defined in
eq.

60
. Hence, we have shown that there exists an open neighbourhood
\mathsf{V}_{a}
of
\gamma
, such that
\mathsf{V}_{a}\subset\mathcal{T}^{-1}{\left({\mathsf{U}_{a}}\right)}
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Recall weak and strong (Whitney) topology are distinct when the domain manifold is not compact.
Consider the distance transform restricted to the interior
\interior{\mathcal{M}}
given by
\gamma\mapsto\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\rvert_{\interior{\mathcal{M}}}
. This function is continuous in weak topology because restriction of domain is continuous in weak topology. However,
C^{k}_{S}(\interior{\mathcal{M}},\R)
is too fine a topology for
\mathcal{T}
to be continuous.

4.1
Genericity of Embeddings that have Morse-supported chordal distance transform

We now prove one of our main contributions. We show that the subset of smooth embeddings of
S^{1}
into Euclidean space whose chordal distance transform is Morse-supported (
Definition

2.22
) is generic: that is, it is both open and dense in Whitney
C^{k}
-topology, for
k\geq 2
. We recall the Morse-supported condition contains two constraints; the first two,
(M1)
and
(M2)
, require that the critical points in the interior be non-degenerate, and there be finitely many of them. The last,
(M3)
, ensures that homotopy type of sublevel sets of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
are constant as they are extended from the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
where
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is zero. We first show in
Section

4.1.1
how restricting chordal distance transform to embeddings constrains the problem. If
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
for
k\geq 2
, then
(M3)
is automatically satisfied (
Proposition

4.8
, and
(M1)
implies
(M2)
(
Corollary

4.5
). Consequently, the genericity of Morse-supported
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
only depends on the genericity of
(M1)
. We then complete the proof of
Theorem

4.3
by showing that
(M1)
holds for chordal distance transform of embeddings on an open and dense subset in
Sections

4.1.2
and
4.1.3
respectively.

Theorem 4.3
.

Consider
C^{k}
-embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
for
k\geq 2
. Let
\Xi
be the subset of embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
whose chordal distance transform are Morse-supported (
Definition

2.22
):

\Xi:=\{\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})\ :\ \mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\text{ satisfies\penalty\ \lx@cref{refnum}{M1}, \lx@cref{refnum}{M2} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{M3}}\}.

(63)

Then
\Xi
is open and dense in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
in Whitney
C^{k}
-topology. Furthermore, this implies
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
and
\pershomf(\mathsf{T}_{\gamma})
are pointwise finite dimensional (tame) on
\Xi
.

Proof.

We defer the proof that
\Xi
is open and dense to
Proposition

4.11
and
Proposition

4.16
respectively. If
\Xi
is the set of embeddings where
(M1)
,
(M2)
and
(M3)
are satisfied, then due to the characterisation of Morse-supported functions in
Lemma

2.23
,
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
is tame. Note that this also implies
\pershomf(\mathsf{T}_{\gamma})
is tame due to the correspondence between
\pershomf(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
and
\pershomf(\mathsf{T}_{\gamma})
described in
Lemma

3.1
.
∎

4.1.1
Critical Points Near the Boundary

We first consider the behaviour of the distance function near the boundary of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. In
Lemma

4.4
, we first show that for
C^{2}
-embeddings, there are no critical points in the interior of
\mathcal{M}
that are arbitrarily close to the boundary. Thus, we can apply
Lemma

2.24
to show that
(M1)
provides sufficient conditions for
(M2)
. We also use
Lemma

4.4
and show in
Proposition

4.8
that
(M3)
is satisfied for all embeddings
\gamma
: there is some regular level
a>0
such that
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
is a deformation retract of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}(-\infty,{a}]
.

In order to establish a lower bound on interior critical values of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
, we make use of the fact that
C^{2}
compact submanifolds of Euclidean space have positive reach
\tau>0

(
25
, Remarks 4.20-21)
. This constrains the separation of the loop from the tangent plane at any given point on the loop. This places constraints on how close two distinct points along the loop can be paired to form an interior critical point of the distance function.

Lemma 4.4
.

Consider for
k\geq 2
an embedding
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
with reach
\tau>0
. Then the interval
(0,2\tau)
are regular values of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
.

Proof.

Since
\gamma
is a
C^{2}
embedding of a compact manifold, it has positive reach
(
25
, Remarks 4.20-21)
. We first recall how the reach constrains the variation of a separation vector
\gamma(t_{2})-\gamma(t_{1})
away from the tangent space of the curve spanned by
\dot{\gamma}(t_{1})
at
t_{1}
: as a consequence of
(
25
, Theorem 4.18)
we recall
eq.

48
: whenever
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
, we have a bound on the Euclidean inner product

\left\langle\frac{\dot{\gamma}(t_{i})}{\norm{\dot{\gamma}(t_i)}},\frac{\gamma(t_{2})-\gamma(t_{1})}{\|\gamma(t_{2})-\gamma(t_{1})\|}\right\rangle^{2}\geq 1-\quantity(\frac{\|\gamma(t_2) - \gamma(t_1)\|}{2\tau})^{2},\quad\text{ for }i=1,2.

(64)

If
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(t_{1},t_{2})=\norm{\gamma(t_1) -\gamma(t_2)}\in(0,2\tau)
, then the right hand side is bounded away from zero. This inequality then bounds
\differential{(\DT_{\gamma})}
away from zero, given
eq.

56
. Hence there are no critical values
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
in
(0,2\tau)
.

∎

Corollary 4.5
.

If
k\geq 2
an embedding
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, and all critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
on the interior
\interior{\mathcal{M}}
are non-degenerate
(M1)
, then there are only finitely many critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
on the interior
\interior{\mathcal{M}}

(M2)
.

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

2.24
: if we have a neighbourhood of
\partial\mathcal{M}
where
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
has no interior critical points, and
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is Morse on the interior, then there are only finitely many interior critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. Given
Lemma

4.4
, we can take
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}[0,2\tau)
as such a neighbourhood of
\partial\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The lower bound on the non-zero regular values in
Lemma

4.4
is tight. Consider
\gamma
being the circle of constant radius
r
. Then the reach is
\tau=r
, and the only non-zero critical value is the maximum
\max\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}=2r
. Note that the maximum is attained by all pairs
\{(z,-z)\ :\ z\in S^{1}\}
in
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
constituting the diameter of the circle.

We now show that the absence of interior critical points near the boundary is sufficient to prove
(M3)
hold for all
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
of embeddings: for
a<2\tau
, the boundary
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=\partial\mathcal{M}
is a deformation retract of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}(-\infty,{a}]
. We note the technical complication that
0
is critical value of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
, which limits the application of the standard deformation lemma between sublevels sets of regular values
(
8
, Theorem 3.20)
; nonetheless, we can find an auxiliary function
h
such that sublevels sets of
h
coincide with that of
f
near the boundary, and regular on and near the boundary too. Applying the standard deformation lemma to sublevel sets of
h
then yields the deformation retraction of sublevel sets of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
near the boundary.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
for
k\geq 2
. If, for some
a>0
, we have an interval of regular values
(0,a]
for
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=\partial\mathcal{M}
is a deformation retract of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}(-\infty,{a}]
.

Proof.

As a shorthand let
f=\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. We first construct the auxiliary function
h:\mathcal{M}\to\R
.

We first note that the distance function
\nu:\mathcal{M}\to\R
to the boundary is a smooth function off the image of the axis of symmetry of the infinite strip
[0,1]\times\R
under the quotient
[0,1]\times\R\twoheadrightarrow[0,1]\times\R/\Z=\mathcal{M}
. Let us construct a function
h=f+\sigma\circ\nu
, where
\sigma
is a smooth, monotone non-decreasing bump function, such that
\sigma(0)=0,\sigma^{\prime}(0)=1
,
\sigma(y)=1
for
y\geq\epsilon
, and
\sigma(y)\in(0,1)
for
y\in(0,\epsilon)
. Here we choose
\epsilon>0
to be sufficiently small such that
\nu
is smooth on the sublevel set
\nu^{-1}(-\infty,{\epsilon}]
, and
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}<a
on such a sublevel set. The latter constraint is possible because
f
is continuous, implying there being a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
such that
f(\lbag{p,q}\rbag)<a
.

We now claim that
h^{-1}(-\infty,{a+1}]=f^{-1}(-\infty,{a}]
. First,
f\leq a
implies
h\leq a+\sigma\circ\nu\leq a+1
. Conversely,
h\leq a+1
implies
f\leq a+(1-\sigma\circ\nu)
. The upper bound is only strictly greater than a iff
\sigma\circ\nu<1
, which is only possible if
\nu<\epsilon
. However we have chosen
\epsilon
to be sufficiently small such that
\nu<\epsilon
implies
f<a
. Hence
h\leq a+1
implies
f\leq a
.

Since
\sigma(\nu(x))=0
iff
x\in\mathcal{M}
, we also have
h^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=f^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=\partial\mathcal{M}
.

We now show that the
\epsilon
parameter can be further tuned, such that
[0,a+1]
are regular values of
h
.
Without loss of generality we consider a chart
\varphi:U\to\R^{2}
such that the distance to
\partial\mathcal{M}
is expressed as the distance to the diagonal boundary
t_{1}=t_{2}
of the infinite strip
[0,1]\times\R
, i.e.
\nu=t_{2}-t_{1}
. Then,

\norm{\nabla h}^{2}=\norm{\nabla f}^{2}+(2\sigma^{\prime})(-1,1)\cdot\nabla f+2(\sigma^{\prime})^{2}.

(65)

On
f^{-1}[0,a]
, the first term is positive for
\nu>0
; the last term is positive everywhere on
\nu\in[0,\epsilon)
. To ensure that
\norm{\nabla h}^{2}
is positive on
\nu\in[0,\epsilon]
, we need to choose
\epsilon
to be sufficiently small, such that on
\nu^{-1}[0,\epsilon]
, we have
(-1,1)\cdot\nabla f>0
(since by construction
\sigma^{\prime}>0
). By Taylor expansion, we obtain

\partialderivative{h}{t_1}=\langle{\dot{\gamma}(t_{1})},{\gamma(t_{1})-\gamma(t_{2})}\rangle=-\norm{\dot{\gamma}(t_1)}^{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})+\frac{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2}}{2}M

(66)

where in the remainder term of the Taylor expansion, the term
M
has magnitude bounded only by finite global bounds on the norm of
\dot{\gamma}
and
\ddot{\gamma}
. Thus for sufficiently small
\epsilon_{1}>0
for
t_{2}-t_{1}
we can bound
\partialderivative{h}{t_1}
away from zero. By symmetry the same analysis applies to
\partialderivative{h}{t_2}
and we can find a sufficiently small
\epsilon_{1}>0
for
t_{2}-t_{1}
to bound
\partialderivative{h}{t_2}
away from zero. Since
t_{2}-t_{1}
expresses the distance to the boundary in local coordinates, by taking
\epsilon=\epsilon_{1}
, we satisfy
\norm{\nabla h}^{2}>0
on this chart. Since
\mathcal{M}
is compact we can take a finite open cover and take
\epsilon>0
to be the minimum of all such
\epsilon_{1}
’s across the charts to ensure
\nabla h\neq 0
on
f^{-1}[0,a]=h^{-1}[0,a+1]
.

Because
[0,a+1]
are regular values of
h
, the standard Morse deformation lemma of regular sublevel sets
(
8
, Theorem 3.20)
implies
h^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
is a deformation retract of
h^{-1}{[0,a+1]}
. Since
h^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=f^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
and
h^{-1}[0,a+1]=f^{-1}[0,a]
, we obtain the desired result.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

For
k\geq 2
and
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, there is some sufficiently small
a>0
, such that for all
t\in[0,a]
, the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
is a deformation retract of the sublevel set
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}(-\infty,{t}]
(condition
(M3)
,
Definition

2.22
).

Proof.

From
Lemma

4.4
, we have for
k\geq 2
, the existence of some regular interval
(0,a]
of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
.
Lemma

4.7
then implies
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=\partial\mathcal{M}
is a deformation retract of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}(-\infty,{a}]
.
∎

4.1.2
Openness

We now turn to showing that
C^{2}
-embeddings that have Morse-supported distance functions are
open
in Whitney topology (
Proposition

4.11
). We approach this problem by covering
\mathcal{M}
with a neighbourhood
L
of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
, and a compact subset
K
of the interior. We then consider the distance critical points near the boundary and in the interior separately. We address the critical points near the boundary in
Lemma

4.9
, where we show that we can fix
L
such that there are no critical points on
L
for distance functions of embeddings in a neighbourhood
\mathsf{U}
of a Morse-supported embedding. As such, for embeddings in
\mathsf{U}
, the only critical points in the interior are contained in some fixed
K\subset\interior{\mathcal{M}}
. We then show in
Lemma

4.10
that for a Morse-supported embedding, we can find a neighbourhood
\mathsf{W}
of embeddings such that critical points are non-degenerate in
K
. On the neighbourhood
\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{W}
of
\gamma
, the combination of both lemmas then account for all of the separation critical points of embeddings, and show them to be finite and non-degenerate.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
k\geq 1
. Assume for
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, the distance transform
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\in C^{k}({\mathcal{M}},{\R})
has finitely many critical points in the interior
\interior{\mathcal{M}}
. Then there is an open neighbourhood
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
of
\gamma
on which the following holds: there is a neighbourhood
L\subset\mathcal{M}
of
\partial\mathcal{M}
, whereby
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
has no critical points in
L
, for all
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathsf{U}
.

Proof.

Let
\{\lbag{a_{1},b_{1}}\rbag,\ldots,\lbag{a_{n},b_{n}}\rbag\}
be the set of critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
on the interior of
\mathcal{M}
, and consider
R=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}\subset S^{1}
, the projection of the critical points onto
S^{1}
. We colloquially call
R
the critical sites of
\gamma
. Because
R
is finite, we can cover
S^{1}
with closed intervals
J_{1},\ldots,J_{m}
with non-empty interior, such that each
J_{i}
contains at most one element of
R
, and
S^{1}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\interior{J_{i}}
.

Let
q:S^{1}\times S^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{M}
be the quotient map, and consider

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=q\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}(J_{i}\times J_{i})\right)=\{\{u,v\}\ :\ \exists i\in[m],\ \text{s.t.}\ u,v\in J_{i}\}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}L_{i}

\displaystyle L_{i}

\displaystyle=q(J_{i}\times J_{i})=\{\{u,v\}\ :\ u,v\in J_{i}\}

We now show that by construction,
\partial\mathcal{M}\subset L
, and
L
is a neighbourhood of
\partial\mathcal{M}
. For any
\lbag{u}\rbag\in\partial\mathcal{M}
, the point
u\in S^{1}
is in the interior of some
J_{i}
, and thus there is some
\delta
such that the neighbourhood
\{\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\ :\ d(u,v_{1}),d(u,v_{2})<\delta\}
of
\lbag{u}\rbag
is contained in
L_{i}
. Hence
L
is a neighbourhood of
\partial\mathcal{M}
.

Furthermore, a point
\lbag{u,v}\rbag\in L\setminus\partial\mathcal{M}
cannot be a critical point of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
, for the following reasons. By construction, there is some
J_{i}
such that
u,v\in{J_{i}}
, and
J_{i}
can at most admit one critical site. Thus,
v_{1},v_{2}
cannot both be critical sites, which is a necessary condition for
\lbag{u,v}\rbag\notin\partial\mathcal{M}
being an interior critical point of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
.

We now show that there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
\gamma
in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, such that for
\tilde{\gamma}\in U
, the interior critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
are not in
L
. We show this by contradiction. Suppose there is no such open neighbourhood of
\gamma
in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
. Then there is a sequence of embeddings
\gamma_{n}\to\gamma
in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
such that for each
\gamma_{n}
, there is some interior critical point
\lbag{u_{n},v_{n}}\rbag\in L\setminus\partial\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{n}}
. By construction there is some
i(n)
such that
\lbag{u_{n},v_{n}}\rbag\in L_{i(n)}
. Furthermore, because the number
m
of cover elements of
L
is finite, there is some fixed
i\in[m]
and a subsequence of
(\gamma_{n})
where by each
\gamma_{n}
in the subsequence there is an interior critical point
\lbag{u_{n},v_{n}}\rbag\in L_{i}
. Furthermore, because
q
is continuous and
J_{i}\times J_{i}
is compact,
L_{i}=q(J_{i}\times J_{i})
is compact, and thus we can extract a further subsequence of
\gamma_{n}
such that
\lbag{u_{n},v_{n}}\rbag
converges to some point in
L_{i}
. Because
u_{n}\neq v_{n}
, and
u_{n},v_{n}
are on an interval
J_{i}
, we can choose
u_{n}<v_{n}
for all
n
w.o.l.g., and convergence of
\lbag{u_{n},v_{n}}\rbag
implies
u_{n}\to u
and
v_{n}\to v
for some
u,v\in J_{i}
.

Suppose then
u\neq v
, i.e. the sequence
\lbag{u_{n},v_{n}}\rbag\to\lbag{u,v}\rbag
converges to some point in the interior of
\mathcal{M}
. Because
\lbag{u_{n},v_{n}}\rbag
is an interior critical point of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{n}}
,

\langle\gamma_{n}(u_{n})-\gamma_{n}(v_{n}),\dot{\gamma_{n}}(u_{n})\rangle=\langle\gamma_{n}(u_{n})-\gamma_{n}(v_{n}),\dot{\gamma_{n}}(v_{n})\rangle=0.

(
\dagger
)

Recall
\gamma_{n}\to\gamma
under Whitney
C^{k}
topology. This implies uniform convergence of
\gamma_{n}
and
\dot{\gamma_{n}}
to
\gamma
and
\dot{\gamma}
on
J_{i}
; thus

\langle\gamma(u)-\gamma(v),\dot{\gamma}(u)\rangle=\langle\gamma(u)-\gamma(v),\dot{\gamma}(v)\rangle=0.

This implies
\lbag{u,v}\rbag\in\interior{\mathcal{M}}
is an interior critical point of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. However, because
\lbag{u,v}\rbag\in L_{i}
, it cannot not an interior critical point. Thus we conclude that
\lbag{u_{n},v_{n}}\rbag\to\lbag{u}\rbag
on
\partial\mathcal{M}
.

We now show that this sequence is a contradiction of
\gamma
being an embedding. Let
\gamma^{(j)}\in C^{k}(S^{1},\R)
denote the
j^{\text{th}}
-component of
\gamma
. We can then rewrite
(\dagger)
as

\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\gamma^{(j)}_{n}(u_{n})-\gamma^{(j)}_{n}(v_{n}))\dot{\gamma}^{(j)}_{n}(u_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\gamma^{(j)}_{n}(u_{n})-\gamma^{(j)}_{n}(v_{n}))\dot{\gamma}^{(j)}_{n}(u_{n})=0.

(
\ddagger
)

Because
u_{n},v_{n}\in J_{i}
and
J_{i}
is a closed interval, the mean value theorem implies there is some
x_{n,j}\in(u_{n},v_{n})
such that
\gamma^{(j)}_{n}(u_{n})-\gamma^{(j)}_{n}(v_{n})=\dot{\gamma}^{(j)}_{n}(x_{n,j})(u_{n}-v_{n})
. Note that
x_{n,j}\to u
for all
j
. Because
u_{n}\neq v_{n}
, we can thus rewrite
(\ddagger)
as

\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dot{\gamma}^{(j)}_{n}(x_{n,j})\dot{\gamma}^{(j)}_{n}(u_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dot{\gamma}^{(j)}_{n}(x_{n,j})\dot{\gamma}^{(j)}_{n}(v_{n})=0

Since we have uniform convergence of
\dot{\gamma}_{n}\to\dot{\gamma}
on
J_{i}
, we then conclude that
\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\dot{\gamma}^{(j)}(u))^{2}=0
, which contradicts
\gamma
being an embedding. Hence there must be an open neighbourhood of
\gamma
in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, on which the critical points of the distance transform cannot have interior critical points in
L
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
k\geq 2
and consider
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, such that
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\rvert_{\interior{\mathcal{M}}}
is Morse on
\interior{\mathcal{M}}
. For any compact
K\subset\interior{\mathcal{M}}
, there is an open neighbourhood
\mathsf{W}\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
of
\gamma
, such that the critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
on
K
are all non-degenerate for all
\tilde{\gamma}\in W
.

Proof.

Consider the compatible pair of atlases
\Theta=\left\{{(\theta_{i},U_{i})}\right\}
for
S^{1}
, and
\Phi=\left\{{(\varphi_{j},W_{j})}\right\}
for
\mathcal{M}
, as described in
Proposition

2.18
. Recall for any
j
there is some pair of
i,i^{\prime}
such that
\varphi_{j}(W_{j})\subset\theta_{i}(U_{i})\times\theta_{j}(U_{j})\subset\R^{2}
. For
f\in C^{k}(\mathcal{M},\R)
, let
f_{j}=f\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}:\varphi_{j}(W_{j})\to\R
. The map
f
being Morse on the interior of
\mathcal{M}
implies on
W_{j}\cap\interior{\mathcal{M}}
,

\mu_{j}(t_{1},t_{2}):=(\det H_{f_{i}}\rvert_{(t_{1},t_{2})})^{2}+\norm{\nabla{f_j}}^{2}>0.

(
\star
)

Since
W_{j}
is an open cover of
\mathcal{M}
, consider the cover of
K
given by
K_{j}=W_{j}\cap K
, and
\varphi_{j}(K_{j})
. Because
K_{j}
is compact and
\varphi_{j}
a diffeomorphism,
\varphi_{j}(K_{j})
is compact. Since
f_{j}
is in
C^{2}
, and the R.H.S of
(\star)
is a polynomial is the first and second derivatives of
f_{j}
, the function
\mu_{j}
is also continuous. Due to
\varphi(K_{j})
being compact, the minimum of
\mu_{j}
on
\varphi_{j}(K_{j})
is attained by some
\eta_{j}>0
.

Consider another map
\tilde{f}\in C^{2}(\mathcal{M},\R)
and define
\tilde{\mu}_{j}
analogously.
Since
\mu_{j}
is a polynomial in the first and second derivatives of
f_{j}
, there is some choice of
\epsilon_{i}>0
such that if

\norm{D^rf_j - D^r\tilde{f} _j}<\epsilon_{i},\ \text{ on }\varphi_{j}(K_{j}),\ r=1,2

(
\star\star
)

then
\tilde{\mu}_{j}>\eta_{j}/2>0
on
\varphi_{j}(K_{j})
.

Consider then
f=\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. Recall from
Proposition

2.18
that the
r
th
-order derivatives of
f_{j}
is also a polynomial in components of
\gamma_{i}:=\gamma\circ\theta_{i}
and
\gamma_{i^{\prime}}:=\gamma\circ\theta_{i^{\prime}}
, along with their derivatives up to order
r
. Since
\varphi_{j}(K_{j})\subset\theta_{i}(U_{i})\times\theta_{i^{\prime}}(U_{i^{\prime}})
, consider
Q_{jl}\subset\theta_{l}(U_{l})
, which is the projection of
\varphi_{j}(K_{j})
onto one of the factors in
\theta_{i}(U_{i})\times\theta_{i^{\prime}}(U_{i^{\prime}})
for
l=i,i^{\prime}
. Note that
Q_{jl}
is compact, because
\varphi_{j}(K_{j})
is compact, and
\theta_{l}
is continuous. By continuity of polynomials there is some
\delta_{j}>0
such that if

\norm{D^r
\gamma_l - D^r\tilde{\gamma}_l }<\delta_{j},\ \text{ on }Q_{jl},\ r=0,1,2,\ l=i,i^{\prime},

(
\star\star\star
)

then
(\star\star)
holds. This in turn implies
\tilde{\mu_{j}}>0
for
\tilde{f}=\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
, which is equivalent to saying all critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
in
K_{j}
are non-degenerate.

We now observe that the set of embeddings
\tilde{\gamma}
satisfying
(\star\star\star)
constitute a
C^{k\geq 2}
weak subbasic neighbourhood
\mathsf{W}_{j}
of
\gamma
, on which all critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
in
K_{j}
are non-degenerate. Because
K
is compact, it is covered by finitely many
K_{j}=K\cap W_{j}
. Therefore on the finite intersection
\mathsf{W}=\cap_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{W}_{j}
, which is again a neighbourhood of
\gamma
in Whitney
C^{k}
-topology, for all
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathsf{W}
, all critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
in
K
are non-degenerate.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

For
k\geq 2
, the subset of embeddings
\Xi\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
on which the distance transform is Morse-supported is open in Whitney
C^{k}
-topology.

Proof.

Because the distance transform
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
of
\gamma\in\Xi
has finitely many critical points on
\interior{\mathcal{M}}
, consider the open neighbourhood
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
of
\gamma
as described in
Lemma

4.9
: for all
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathsf{U}
, there exists a neighbourhood
L
of
\partial\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
has no critical points on
L
. Then consider
K\subset\interior{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\interior{L}\cup\interior{K}=\interior{\mathcal{M}}
. Because
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\rvert_{\interior{\mathcal{M}}}
is Morse due to
\gamma\in\Xi
, by
Lemma

4.10
there exists an open neighbourhood
\mathsf{W}\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
of
\gamma
, such that for all
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathsf{W}
, all critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
in
K
are non-degenerate. Consider then the open neighbourhood
\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{W}
of
\gamma
in
\mathrm{Emb}^{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, and
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{W}
. Since
\interior{L}\cup\interior{K}=\interior{\mathcal{M}}
and there are no critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
on
L
, all critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
in
\interior{\mathcal{M}}
are non-degenerate and thus
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}\rvert_{\interior{\mathcal{M}}}
is Morse. Thus for any
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, there is an open neighbourhood
\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{W}
on which
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
satisfies
(M1)
for
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathsf{U}\cap\mathsf{W}
. Since
(M3)
is automatically satisfied (
Proposition

4.8
), and
(M1)
implies
(M2)
(
Corollary

4.5
), we conclude that the subset
\Xi
on which
(M1)
,
(M2)
and
(M3)
are all satisfied for their distance transforms is open in Whitney
C^{k}
-topology.
∎

4.1.3
Density

Having shown that Morse-supported embeddings are an open subset of embeddings, we now show that they are dense using the multi-jet transversality theorem of
Theorem

2.30
. We apply it to show that embeddings whose chordal distance transform has no interior degenerate critical points (satisfying
(M1)
) are dense in Whitney
C^{k}
-topology. We recall that
Theorem

2.30
relates to maps whose multi-jets intersect a Whitney-stratified space in multi-jet space transversally. Our proof proceeds by framing embeddings whose chordal distance transform violate
(M1)
as those which intersect a Whitney stratified subset
\chi_{0}
of the multi-jet space
J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
. We call this the
degenerate locus

\chi_{0}
. By showing this in
Lemma

4.12
, we convert the problem into showing that the embeddings whose multi-jets avoid
\chi_{0}
in
J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
are dense. Using dimensionality arguments, we then show in
Proposition

4.15
that for embeddings, the avoidance of
\chi_{0}
is equivalent to transverse intersection of the mult-jet with
\chi_{0}
. Finally, because
Proposition

4.15
implies transverse intersections with
\chi_{0}
are dense, we show in
Proposition

4.16
that embeddings whose multijets avoid
\chi_{0}
are dense in Whitney
C^{k}
-topology, and thus the subset of embeddings with Morse-supported chordal distance transform are dense in Whitney
C^{k}
-topology.

The Degenerate Locus

Let us consider the two-fold jet bundle
J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
for maps in
C^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
for
k\geq 2
. Let
Z=\left\{{(\zeta_{i},Z_{i})}\right\}
be an atlas of
J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
induced by an atlas
\Theta=\{(\theta_{i},U_{i})\}
on
S^{1}
. Consider the prolongation
j^{2}_{2}f:\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(S^{1})\to J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
of a map
f\in C^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
. On a chart
\zeta_{i}:Z_{i}\to\R^{6d+2}
, we can express
j^{2}_{2}f
in local coordinates as

\displaystyle\zeta((j^{2}_{2}f)(\theta_{1}^{-1}{\left({t_{1}}\right)},\theta_{1}^{-1}{\left({t_{1}}\right)})

\displaystyle=(t_{1},f(t_{1}),\dot{f}(t_{1}),\ddot{f}(t_{1}),f(t_{2}),f(t_{2}),\dot{f}(t_{2}),\ddot{f}(t_{2})),

(67)

(here we abuse notation and let
f(t_{i}):=f\circ\theta^{-1}(t_{i})
, and similarly for the derivatives; see
eq.

55
). We let
(t_{1},x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime\prime},t_{2},x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime\prime})

denote the local coordinates on any such chart, where
t_{i}\in\R
are local coordinates for
S^{1}
, and
x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime},x_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\R^{d}
.

We aim to represent the degenerate locus
\chi_{0}
as a set of polynomial constraints on each chart. We first consider critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
, and represent them as the intersection between the image of
j^{2}_{2}f
and the
critical locus

C_{0}
. Due to the characterisation of gradients in
eq.

56
, consider a local map
C_{i}:\zeta_{i}(Z_{i})\to\R^{2}
, given in local coordinates by

C_{i}(t_{1},x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime\prime},t_{2},x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime\prime})=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\quad\mbox{where}\quad v=x_{1}-x_{2}.

(68)

For a fixed
f\in C^{k}(S^{1},\R^{d})
, the evaluation of
C_{i}\circ\zeta_{i}^{-1}
on
j^{2}_{2}f
yields the gradients of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
in local coordinates given by the atlas
\Theta
on
S^{1}
. We note that the zero-set of the gradient
C_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
is independent on the choice of chart
\zeta_{i}
on
Z_{i}
.

We can similarly represent the degeneracy of the Hessian as a polynomial constraint on each chart. Let us write the Hessian matrix of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
in local coordinates as a map on each chart
H_{i}:\zeta_{i}(Z_{i})\to\R^{3}
, valued in the space of real symmetric
2\times 2
matrices:

\displaystyle H_{i}(t_{1},x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime\prime},t_{2},x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

(69)

where functions
p,q,r
are defined in
eq.

58
. For a fixed
f\in C^{k}(S^{1},\R^{d})
, the evaluation of
H_{i}\circ\zeta_{i}^{-1}
on
j^{2}_{2}f
yields the Hessian of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
in local coordinates. Let
D_{i}:=\det\circ H_{i}:\zeta_{i}(Z_{i})\to\R
, where
\det(p,q,r)=pq-r^{2}
. Then the zero-set
D_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
characterises where maps have degenerate Hessians, and is independent of the choice of chart
\zeta_{i}
on
V_{i}
.

Having assembled maps
C_{i},D_{i}
on which characterise critical points and degeneracy of Hessians on
V_{i}
, we can define the degenerate locus
\chi_{0}
. Consider
\chi_{i}=(C_{i},D_{i})\to\R^{2}
. The intersection of
j^{2}_{2}f
with
\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0,0}\right)}
characterises the degenerate critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
on the local coordinate patch
Z_{i}
of the jet space. Given an atlas
Z=\left\{{(\zeta_{i},Z_{i})}\right\}
, we define the degenerate locus across the whole
J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
by gluing together the zero sets across each
Z_{i}
:

\chi_{0}=\bigcup_{i}\zeta_{i}^{-1}(\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)})

(70)

We remark that
\chi_{0}
is independent of choice of atlas. Since
\chi_{0}
is an algebraic set on any chart, it is a Whitney stratified subset of
J^{2}_{2}({M},{\R^{d}})

(
60
, p.36-38)
.

Lemma 4.12
.

For
k\geq 2
and
f\in C^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, its chordal distance transform
\mathcal{T}_{f}:\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\to\R
has no degenerate critical points on its interior
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
(
(M1)
), if and only if
j^{2}_{2}f\cap\chi_{0}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Recall
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})\subset\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
is double-covered by
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(S^{1})
. A point
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag\in\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})=\interior{\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})}
is a degenerate critical point of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
, iff the lift of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
to
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(S^{1})
has
(z_{1},z_{2})
as degenerate critical point. Consider then
j^{2}_{2}f:\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(S^{1})\to J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
. We observe then that
(z_{1},z_{2})
is a degenerate critical point of the lift of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
on
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(S^{1})
, iff on a chart for
(z_{1},z_{2})
,

\chi_{i}(\zeta((j^{2}_{2}f)(z_{1},z_{2}))=0.

(71)

This follows directly from the construction of
\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
. Because
\chi_{0}
is the union over all fibres of
\chi_{i}

eq.

70
, an multijet
j^{2}_{2}f
has empty intersection with
\chi_{0}
, iff
\mathcal{T}_{f}
has no degenerate critical points.
∎

The Degenerate Locus Near Multi-Jets of Embeddings

We now work to show that the transverse intersection between the degenerate locus
\chi_{0}
and a jet
j^{2}_{2}f
is empty if
f
is a
C^{2}
-embedding. We proceed by describing the local topology of
\chi_{0}
near a point
\xi\in\chi_{0}\cap j^{2}_{2}f
. It suffices to work on a local chart
\zeta_{i}:Z_{i}\to\R^{6d+2}
, where
\chi_{0}\cap V_{i}
can be described by
C_{i}=D_{i}=0
. We first focus on the condition where
D_{i}=0
, expressing the degeneracy of the Hessian of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
. Since the Hessian is a
2\times 2
matrix, a degenerate Hessian is either zero, or has rank one. We consider the two cases individually.

Lemma 4.13
.

Suppose
f\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
for
k\geq 2
, and consider
\xi\in\chi_{0}\cap j^{2}_{2}f\cap V_{i}
. If
H_{i}(\xi)\neq 0
, then
\chi_{0}
on a neighbourhood of
\xi
is a codimension-3 submanifold of
J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
.

Proof.

Since
H_{i}
is degenerate and non-zero on
\xi
, we restrict ourselves to considering intersections
\xi\in\chi_{0}\cap j^{2}_{2}f
, at which
H_{i}
has rank 1. The Jacobian of
\chi_{i}
at
\xi
is given by

\differential{\chi_i}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}

(72)

As
H(\xi)\neq 0
, the constraint that
D_{i}(\xi)=\det H_{i}(\xi)=pq-r^{2}=0
implies at least one of
p,q\neq 0
. Furthermore, because
v\neq 0
, the row vectors in the Jacobian are linearly independent and thus the Jacobian has rank 3 at
\xi
. Since
H\neq 0
is an open condition,
\chi_{i}:\zeta_{i}(Z_{i})\to\R^{3}
is a submersion on some open ball
U
around
\xi
. By the submersion theorem,
\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\cap U
is a submanifold with codimension 3.
∎

We proceed to describe the case where
H_{i}(\xi)=0
for
\xi\in\chi_{0}\cap j^{2}_{2}f\cap V_{i}
. Since
H(\xi)=0
iff
p(\xi)=q(\xi)=r(\xi)=0
, we see by substitution into
eq.

72
is
\differential{\chi}
rank 2 at
\xi
and no longer surjective, thus
\xi
is no longer a regular point of
\chi
, and the argument in
Lemma

4.13
no longer applies in this case. We deal with this by explicitly describing the local stratification of
\chi_{0}
on a neighbourhood of
\xi
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Consider
\xi\in\chi_{0}\cap V_{i}\cap j^{2}_{2}f
for
f\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, for
k\geq 2
. If the Hessian
H_{i}(\xi)=0
vanishes at
\xi
, then
\xi
lies on a co-dimension 5 stratum of
\chi_{0}
, and is a singular point of
\chi_{0}
.

Proof.

In this proof we let
n=6d+2
be the dimension of
J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
. Consider
\xi\in\chi_{0}\cap V_{i}\cap j^{2}_{2}f
, which is equivalent to
\xi\in\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\cap j^{2}_{2}f
. Let us rewrite the map
\chi_{i}:V_{i}\to\R^{3}
as the composition

\chi_{i}:V_{i}\xrightarrow{(H_{i},C_{i})}\R^{3}\times\R^{2}\xrightarrow{(\det,\Id)}\R\times\R^{2}.

Let
\Upsilon=(H_{i},C_{i})
. We can then write
\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
as

\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=((\det,\Id)\circ\Upsilon)^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=\Upsilon^{-1}{\left({(\det,\Id)^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}}\right)}=\Upsilon^{-1}{\left({{\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\times\Bqty{(0,0)}}\right)}.

Furthermore, if
H_{i}(\xi)=0
, then
\xi\in\Upsilon^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
. We show that
\Upsilon
is a submersion on a neighbourhood
U
of
\xi
. We first compute the Jacobian matrix of
\Upsilon
:

\differential\Upsilon=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}=\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}.

(73)

Since
\xi\in\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\cap j^{2}_{2}f
for an embedding
f
, we have
v\neq 0
and
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\neq 0
on a neighbourhood
U
of
\xi
. We show that this implies on
U
, the Jacobian matrix has full rank, and thus
\Upsilon|_{U}
is a submersion. We prove by contradiction: suppose the rows are not linearly independent; that is, there are coefficients
(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\neq 0
, such that

\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}\pmqty{\lx@physics@matrix\endlx@physics@matrix}=0.

(74)

Since
v\neq 0
at
\xi
, the fact that the bottom right
3\times 2
blocks of the Jacobian is zero implies
a_{0}=a_{1}=0
. This leaves us with the system of equation

\displaystyle a_{3}x_{1}^{\prime}+a_{4}x_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle a_{2}x_{2}^{\prime}-a_{3}v

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle a_{2}x_{1}^{\prime}-a_{4}v

\displaystyle=0

Since
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},v\neq 0
at
\xi
, either
a_{2}=a_{3}=a_{4}=0
, or they are all non-zero. If we substitute
x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}^{\prime}
for
v
in the top equation, then we are left with
2(a_{3}a_{4}/a_{2})v=0
. Since
v\neq 0
and
a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}
are all non-zero, we reach a contradiction. Thus
\Upsilon
is a submersion on a neighbourhood
U
of
\xi
.

If we choose
U
sufficiently small, the submersion theorem then implies, we can chose a surjective chart
\mu:(U,\xi)\to(\R^{n},0)
such that
\Upsilon\rvert_{U}\circ\mu^{-1}:\R^{n}\to\R^{5}
is an orthogonal projection onto the first five coordinates. Thus,
\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\cap U
is locally homeomorphic to the following subset in
\R^{n}
:

\displaystyle\mu(\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\cap U)

\displaystyle=\mu\quantity(\fibre{\Upsilon\rvert_U}{(\fibre{{\det}}{0} \times\Bqty{(0,0)}) \cap\Upsilon(U)})

\displaystyle=(\Upsilon\rvert_{U}\circ\mu^{-1})^{-1}{\left({({\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\times\Bqty{(0,0)})\cap\Upsilon(U)}\right)}

\displaystyle=\quantity(\fibre{{\det}}{0} \times\Bqty{(0,0)} )\times\quantity(\qty(\fibre{{\det}}{0} \times\Bqty{(0,0)} ) \times\R^{n-5})^{n-5}\cap\mu(U)=\quantity(\fibre{{\det}}{0} \times\Bqty{(0,0)} )\times\R^{n-5}

where the last equality is due
\mu
being surjective.
We now give an explicit Whitney stratification of
{\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\subset\R^{3}
. Recall it is the polynomial
\det(w)=w_{1}w_{2}-w_{3}^{2}=0
. We claim
{\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
admits a Whitney stratification
\{0\}\subset{\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
; in other words,
{\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
decomposes into strata
{\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}=W^{(0)}\sqcup W^{(2)}
, where
W^{(2)}={\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\setminus\{0\}
, and
W^{(0)}=\{0\}
. The differential

\differential{(\det)}=w_{2}\differential{w_1}+w_{1}\differential{w_2}-2w_{3}\differential{w_3}

is only singular at the origin
w_{1}=w_{2}=w_{3}=0
; Hence
W^{(2)}={\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\cap\{w\neq 0\}
is a locally closed, two-dimensional submanifold of
\R^{3}
. By definition,
W^{(0)}
is zero dimensional. We show that Whitney condition (B) is satisfied in this case. In this case, this amounts to showing the following: for a sequence
y_{i}\in W^{(2)}
such that
y_{i}\to 0
such that the secant line
\ell_{i}
between line
y_{i}
and 0 tends to some line
\ell
, and the tangent planes
\mathsf{T}_{i}
at
y_{i}
converges to some plane
\mathsf{T}
, then
\ell\subset T
. In fact, in our case, the secant line
\ell_{i}
always lies in the tangent planes
\mathsf{T}_{i}
. Firstly, for any point
w=(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})\in W^{(2)}
, note that a point along the secant line
t\cdot(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
to the origin lies in
W^{(2)}
too (for
t\neq 0
), since the condition
w_{1}w_{2}-w^{3}=0
is invariant w.r.t. rescaling. Hence
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
as a tangent vector in
T_{w}W^{(2)}\subset T_{w}\R^{3}
, and the whole secant
t\cdot(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
is in the tangent plane
\mathsf{T}_{w}
to
W^{(2)}
at
w
. Thus, for any sequence
y_{i}\in W^{(2)}
, the secant
\ell_{i}
between
y_{i}
and
0
lies in the tangent plane
\mathsf{T}_{i}
at
y_{i}
, thus if
\ell_{i}\to\ell
and
\mathsf{T}_{i}\to\mathsf{T}
, we have
\ell\subset\mathsf{T}
.

Having obtained a stratification of
{\det}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}
, we can stratify the embedding of
\chi_{0}
on the neighbourhood
U
of
\xi
, recalling
\mu
is a diffeomorphism:

\displaystyle\mu(\chi_{i}^{-1}{\left({0}\right)}\cap U)

\displaystyle=\quantity(\fibre{{\det}}{0} \times\Bqty{(0,0)} )\times\R^{n-5}

\displaystyle=\quantity((W^{0} \sqcup W^{(2)}) \times\Bqty{(0,0)} )\times\R^{n-5}

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,0,0)\times\quantity((0,0,0,0,0) \times\R^{n-5} )^{n-5}\sqcup W^{(2)}\times\{(0,0)\}\times\quantity( W^{(2)} \times\{(0,0)\} \times\R^{n-5})^{n-5}.

where the last equality is due to
W^{(0)}=(0,0,0)\in\R^{3}
. Since
\mu(\xi)=0
, we conclude that
\xi
lies on a co-dimension 5 stratum of
\chi_{0}
.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
k\geq 2
. If
f\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, then
j^{2}_{2}f
is transverse to the degenerate locus
j^{2}_{2}f\pitchfork\chi_{0}
in
J^{2}_{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, if and only if
j^{2}_{2}f\cap\chi_{0}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Recall
\chi_{0}
is a Whitney stratified space. In
Lemmas

4.13
and
4.14
, we have shown that
j^{2}_{2}f
intersects
\chi_{0}
at strata with codimension at least three. If the intersection is non-empty and transverse, then at such intersections
\rank\differential(\prolong{2}{2}f)
must be greater or equal to the codimension of the strata of
\chi_{0}
at intersections. Since
j^{2}_{2}f
is an immersion of
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(S^{1})
, this implies
\rank\differential(\prolong{2}{2}f)=\dim\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(S^{1})=2
, which is less than the codimension of the
\chi_{0}
strata at intersections. Thus, the dimensional constraints imposed by transversality
eq.

44
implies the intersection
j^{2}_{2}f\cap\chi_{0}
is empty.
∎

Having characterised embeddings whose chordal distance transform satisfy
(M1)
as those with transversal intersections of two-fold 2-jets with the critical locus, we can then apply the transversality theorem
Theorem

2.30
to show that such embeddings are dense in Whitney
C^{k}
topology, for
k\geq 2
. We note that naive application of
Theorem

2.30
would imply density for
k\geq 3
; we extend this to
k=2
by borrowing the argument used in the proof of Theorem 9.25 in
5
.

Proposition 4.16
.

For
k\geq 2
, the subset of embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
whose chordal distance transform satisfy
(M1)
are dense in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
under Whitney
C^{k}
-topology. Hence the subset of embeddings
\Xi\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
(
eq.

63
) whose chordal distance transform are Morse-supported are dense in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
under Whitney
C^{k}
-topology.

Proof.

We first recall for every
1\leq k\leq r
, the
C^{r}
-embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}^{r}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
are a dense subset of the
C^{k}
-embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}^{r}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})

(
35
, Theorem 2.7)
, under Whitney
C^{k}
-topology. Suppose
k\geq 2
; density implies for any open Whitney-
C^{k}
neighbourhood
U
of
f\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, we can choose some
f_{1}\in U
such that
f_{1}\in\mathrm{Emb}^{r}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
(for
k\geq 3
, we choose
f_{1}=f
). Consider then
r\geq 3
. Because residual subsets are dense in Whitney
C^{r}
-topology
(
35
, Theorem 4.4)
,
Theorem

2.30
implies that we can choose some
f_{2}\in\mathrm{Emb}^{r}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
in any open Whitney-
C^{r}
neighbourhood
V
of
f_{1}
, such that
j^{2}_{2}f_{2}
intersects
\chi_{0}
transversely. By
Propositions

4.15
and
4.12
, this is equivalent to saying
\mathcal{T}_{f_{2}}
has no non-degenerate critical points in the interior of
\mathcal{M}
. Because
k\leq r
, any Whitney
C^{r}
-neighbourhood
V
of
f_{1}
(as a
C^{r}
-embedding), is also a Whitney
C^{k}
-neighbourhood of
f_{1}
(as a
C^{k}
-embedding). Since
f_{1}\in U
, and
U
is Whitney
C^{k}
open, we can choose the Whitney
C^{k}
neighbourhood
V
of
f_{1}
such that
V\subset U
, which implies
f_{2}\in U
. Hence for any
f\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, and any Whitney
C^{k}
neighbourhood
U
of
f
, we can find a
f_{2}\subset U
a
C^{k}
-embedding, such that
\mathcal{T}_{f_{2}}
has only non-degenerate critical point on the interior. Because of
Propositions

4.8
and
4.5
,
\mathcal{T}_{f_{2}}
also satisfies
(M2)
and
(M3)
. Thus
\Xi
is dense in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
under Whitney
C^{k}
-topology.
∎

4.2
Geometric Characterisation of Critical Points

Suppose
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is Morse-supported for an embedding
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
, we can relate the Morse index of interior critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
with geometric characteristics of the curve from the formulae for the gradient and Hessian
eqs.

56
and
57
.
In that case, the entries of the Hessian
eq.

58
at
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag
can be simplified as

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=1-\kappa_{12},

(75)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=1-\kappa_{21},

(76)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=-\cos{\theta_{12}},

(77)

where
v_{ij}=x_{j}-x_{i}
,
\kappa_{ij}:=\langle x_{i}^{\prime\prime},v_{ij}\rangle
and
\cos{\theta_{12}}=\langle x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\rangle
.

The Morse index of a critical point
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag
in the interior
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
of
\mathcal{M}
can be read off the entries of the determinant of the Hessian. If
\det H<0
, then
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag
is a saddle point; if
\det H>0
, then
p
and
q
is of the same sign; if that sign is positive, then
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag
is a minimum; if that sign of negative, then
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag
is a maximum. We shall now give a geometric interpretation of these critical points.

The critical point condition

\langle x_{1}^{\prime},v_{12}\rangle=\langle x_{2}^{\prime},v_{21}\rangle=0

(78)

gives that

x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}^{\prime}
are perpendicular to
v_{12}=-v_{21}=x_{2}-x_{1}
.
We will say two segments centred at
x_{1}
and
x_{2}

are
opposing
if this condition is satisfied.
We can now simply restrict ourselves to the 3-dimensional space spanned by
\{x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},v_{12}\}
.
The determinant of the Hessian
pq-r^{2}
is

\frac{1}{\|x_{1}^{\prime}\|\|x_{2}^{\prime}\|}\left[(1-\kappa_{12})(1-\kappa_{21})-\cos^{2}{\theta_{12}}\right].

(79)

where
\kappa_{ij}=\langle x_{i}^{\prime\prime}/\|x_{i}^{\prime}\|,v_{12}\rangle

and
\theta_{12}
is the angle between
x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}^{\prime}
.

To keep the following analysis simple,
we shall assume
|\kappa_{12}|=|\kappa_{21}|=:\kappa
.
Now (after removing the positive scaling) expression
79
becomes

\sgn(\kappa_{12})\sgn(\kappa_{21})\kappa^{2}-(\sgn(\kappa_{12})+\sgn(\kappa_{21}))\kappa+\sin^{2}{\theta_{12}}.

(80)

We can now give a geometric characterisation of critical points, as shown in Figure
7
.

\kappa_{12}
\kappa_{21}

Figure 7
:
Classification of critical points in terms of
\kappa_{12}
and
\kappa_{21}
for
\theta_{12}=\pi/3
where
d>2
(
eq.

79
).
Displayed are two dimensional cartoons of the 9 classes of critical point,
organised by
\sgn(\kappa_{ij})
and Morse index.

Curve away:
\sgn{\kappa_{12}}=\sgn(\kappa_{21})=-1
.

This case is particularly simple;
the expression
80

is always positive,
p
is positive, and thus
all opposing segments that curve away are local minima.

Curve towards:
\sgn(\kappa_{12})=\sgn(\kappa_{21})=1
.

This occurs when two opposing segments are curving towards each other.
Then, for
0\leq\kappa<1-\cos\theta_{12}
we have a local minimum,
for
\kappa>1+\cos{\theta_{12}}
we have a local maximum
and for
1-\cos{\theta_{12}}<\kappa<1+\cos{\theta_{12}}
we have a saddle point.

Curve together:
\sgn(\kappa_{12})=-\sgn(\kappa_{21})
.

WLOG assume
\sgn(\kappa_{12})=1
.
If
-\sin{\theta_{12}}<\kappa<\sin\theta_{12}

we have a local minimum,
and if
\kappa<-\sin{\theta_{12}}
or
\kappa>\sin{\theta_{12}}

we have a saddle point.

For the
\theta=0
case, where
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}
and
v_{12}
are coplanar, we can give further intuition.
Define the radius of curvature relative as
\rho=\|v_{12}\|/\kappa=|\langle x_{1}^{\prime\prime}/\|x_{1}^{\prime}\|,v_{12}/\|v_{12}\|\rangle|^{-1}
.
Consider the circle with centre the midpoint of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
with radius
\frac{1}{2}\|v_{12}\|
.
If the radius of curvature
\rho
is less than the radius
\frac{1}{2}\|v_{12}\|
we have a local maximum,
if it is greater, we have a saddle point.

Example 4.17
.

Consider an ellipse embedded in
\R^{2}
with major axis
2a
and minor axis
2b
.
There are exactly two critical points lying in
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
corresponding to the major and minor axes.
The radius of curvature on each side of the major axis is
b^{2}/a

and
a^{2}/b
for the minor axis.
As
b^{2}/a<a
the major axis corresponds to a local (and global) maximum,
and as
a^{2}/b>b
the minor axis corresponds to a saddle point.

We summarise these observations in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.18
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is Morse-supported for an embedding
\gamma\in\mathrm{Emb}^{2}({S^{1}},{\R^{d}})
.
For a point
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag\in\mathcal{M}
define

x_{i}=\gamma(z_{i})
,

v_{ij}=x_{j}-x_{i}
,
\kappa_{ij}:=\langle x_{i}^{\prime\prime}/_{\|}x_{i}^{\prime}\|,v_{ij}\rangle
and
\cos{\theta_{12}}=\langle x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\rangle
.
Then
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag

is critical for
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
if

\langle x_{1}^{\prime},v_{12}\rangle=\langle x_{2}^{\prime},v_{21}\rangle=0

If
(1-\kappa_{12})(1-\kappa_{21})<\cos^{2}{\theta_{12}}
this is a saddle point.
Otherwise by non-degeneracy
(1-\kappa_{12})(1-\kappa_{21})>\cos^{2}{\theta_{12}}
.
Then,
if
\kappa_{12},\kappa_{21}>1
this is a local maximum,
else if
\kappa_{12},\kappa_{21}<1
, this is a local minimum.

5
Piecewise-Linear Loops

In this section, we consider the case where
\gamma
is finite piecewise linear.
Similar to the analysis in
section

4
for the smooth case, we wish to geometrically interpret the features in
\pershomf_{\ast}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
for generic embeddings
\gamma
by relating the birth and death of such features to non-degenerate critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
, and characterise these non-degenerate points geometrically.
However, the chordal distance transform of a PL loop is only piecewise smooth, and thus the smooth Morse theory used in
section

4
cannot be applied to the PL case. While there is a Morse theory for piecewise smooth functions
9
;
3
for generalised (Clarke) critical points
17
;
18
,
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
may admit degenerate critical points that are excluded from the analysis of
9
;
3
(see
Remark

5.3
). We also remark that even though
\gamma
is piecewise linear, neither
\mathsf{T}_{\gamma}
nor
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
are piecewise linear themselves, excluding the application of piecewise linear Morse theory
7
;
26
.

As such, we take a discrete approach by constructing a filtered simplicial complex
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
that is homotopy equivalent to the sublevel set filtration
\Gamma^{\bullet}
of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. The filtered complex
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
is the nerve of a filtered covering
\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}
of sublevel sets of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. This allows us to apply the Conley index theory (described in
section

2.2.3
) for the Morse sets of
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
to describe the birth and death of features in
\pershomf_{\ast}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
. In
section

5.1
, we first restrict our attention to finite piecewise linear embeddings
\gamma:S^{1}\to\R^{d}
which have no two edges parallel (see conditions
(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
). This allows us to subsequently characterise the Morse sets of the filtration
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
in terms of analogues of smooth non-degenerate critical points called
n
-saddles (see
Section

2.2.3
).
Therefore, we are able to compute the homological critical values of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}

via the combinatorial object
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
.
In
section

5.3
we explicitly describe the filtration of the simplicial complex
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
, which allows us to exactly compute the persistence diagrams
\pershomf_{\ast}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
using algorithms for computing persistent homology.
Then in
section

5.4
we show
that for an embedding satisfying the non-degeneracy conditions
(C1)
-
(C3)

all Morse sets (apart from at value
0
) are simplices, along with some of their faces,
and so can be thought of a critical points, in analogy with the smooth case.
Finally in
section

5.4.1

we build a bijective correspondence between such critical simplices and points in
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})

whose images under
\gamma

satisfy a local geometric condition (
Definition

5.13
).
This condition is in terms of acuteness/obtuseness of the four angles created by a separation (chordal) vector,
and from which the index of the critical simplex can be read off.
The Morse type of these critical simplices bears similarity with the smooth case: if locally the opposing segments curve away from each other, the simplex has Conley index
0
,
if they curve towards each other, it has index
2
, and if they curve together, the simplex has index
1
.

5.1
Piecewise-linear loops

Suppose we are given a set of points
x_{i}\in\R^{d}
cyclically indexed over
i=0,\ldots,n-1\in\Z_{n}
for
n>3
. We consider a piecewise linear loop interpolating these points
f:S^{1}\to\R^{d}
. In order to parametrise
f
explicitly, we set out some notation. Let
u_{i}=x_{i+1}-x_{i}
denote the finite difference vector between successive points in the cycle
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
. We define
l_{i}=\norm{u_i}
to be the length of each linear segment interpolated between
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
. Let us also consider the total length along the loop
L=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}l_{i}
, and partial lengths
L_{i}=\sum_{j<i}l_{i}/L
. We also recall the universal covering
\scre(t):=e^{2\pi\imath t}
of
S^{1}
.

A piecewise linear interpolation of
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
is then a map
f:S^{1}\to\R^{d}
that sends
f(\scre(L_{i}))=x_{i}
, and

f\circ\scre(t)=\frac{L}{l_{i}}\quantity((t- L_i)x_{i+1} - (t - L_{i+1})x_i )=\frac{L}{l_{i}}(t-L_{i})u_{i}+x_{i}\quad\text{for\ }t\in[L_{i},L_{i+1}].

(81)

Non-degenerate Piecewise Linear Loops

We restrict ourselves to piecewise linear interpolations
f:S^{1}\to\R^{d}
that satisfy the following properties. We call a cyclic sequence of data points
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}

non-degenerate
, if

(C1)

No distinct pair of points in the sequence are identical:
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
for
i\neq j
;

(C2)

f
is a topological embedding: the interiors of interpolated segments may not cross one another; and

(C3)

No pair of distinct segments are parallel or anti-parallel:
|\langle{u_{i}},{u_{j}}\rangle|<\norm{u_i}\norm{u_j}
for
i\neq j
.

For
d>2
, any ‘generic’ cyclic sequence
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
is non-degenerate: we only require the set
\left\{{x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}}\right\}
to be in
general position
: no
k
of such points lie in a
(k-2)
-dimensional affine subspace of
\R^{d}
for
k=2,\ldots,d+1
. The general position requirement immediately implies
(C1)
regardless of dimension
d
, as a pair of points lying on a
0
-dimensional affine subspace implies they are identical. The following lemmas show that
(C2)
and
(C3)
also follow from the general position assumption, if
d>2
. Indeed, these observations extend to the case where points
x_{i}
are vertices of a geometric graph, where edges between vertices are interpolated by linear segments.

Lemma 5.1
.

Consider a PL geometric graph in
\R^{d}
with vertex set
X=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})\subset\R^{d}
. If
d>2
, and the vertex set
X
is in general position, then the PL geometric graph is an embedding of the underlying graph — that is, the relative interior of an edge does not intersect another edge.

Proof.

If
n=1
(only one vertex) the map sending a vertex into
\R^{d}
is trivially an embedding; if
n=2
(two vertices), then the general position requirement requires the two vertices two be distinct, and thus the interpolation
\overline{x_{0}x_{1}}
is an embedding of the graph with one edge.

Let us then consider the case where
n>2
. First, we note that the general position requirement implies all points in
X
are distinct. So for
i<j
, two closed segments
\overline{x_{i}x_{i+1}}
and
\overline{x_{j}x_{j+1}}
can either intersect only at single point
x_{j}
(with
j=i+1
) at the end of segment; or the relative interior of one segment intersects a single point along the other, which may either be in the relative interior or ends (with
j>i+1
). The latter requires the four distinct points
x_{i},x_{i+1},x_{j},x_{j+1}
to lie on a two dimensional affine subspace of
\R^{d}
, which contradicts the general position requirement on
X
for
D>2
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

If
d>2
, a PL geometric graph in
\R^{d}
in general position has no two edges parallel.

Proof.

Suppose we have a PL geometric graph with edges

\overline{x_{i}x_{i+1}}
and
\overline{x_{j}x_{j+1}}
parallel.
Then for some
\lambda\in\R
,
x_{j+1}=x_{j}+\lambda(x_{i+1}-x_{i})

hence
x_{i+1}
is in the affine span of
x_{i},x_{i+1}
, and
x_{j}
which has affine dimension no more than 2,
thus the graph is not in general position, a contradiction.
∎

5.2
The Distance Function

We now consider the chordal distance transform
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
of a piecewise linear interpolated loop
\gamma:S^{1}\to\R
, satisfying the embedding conditions
(C1)
and
(C2)
. Since
\gamma
is piecewise linear,
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is a piecewise smooth on
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. Here we define
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
explicitly on pieces
V_{ij}\subset\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
associated to pairs of points from segments
\overline{x_{i},x_{i+1}}
and
\overline{x_{j},x_{j+1}}
. Given we have chosen
\mathcal{U}
to be a good open cover, let
\Phi
and
\Theta
be the compatible atlases for
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
and
S^{1}
given in
Proposition

2.18
. On a chart
\varphi_{ij}:W_{ij}\to\R^{2}
, recall from the previous section that we can express
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
in local coordinates
(t_{1},t_{2})
as

\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(t_{1},t_{2}):=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\gamma(t_1) - \gamma(t_2)}^{2}

We can define
V_{ij}\subset W_{ij}
to be the subset described in local coordinates by
t_{1}\in[L_{i},L_{i+1}]
and
t_{2}\in[L_{j},L_{j+1}]
:

\displaystyle V_{ij}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{ij}^{-1}(([L_{i},L_{i+1}]\times[L_{j},L_{j+1}])\cap[0,1]\times\R).

(82)

The distance transform
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
thus evaluates on
V_{ij}
the distance between a pair of points from segments
\overline{x_{i},x_{i+1}}
and
\overline{x_{j},x_{j+1}}
respectively. We give an illustration of the elements
V_{ij}
in
Figure

8
. If
i=j
, we observe that
V_{ii}=\mathbf{F}_{2}([L_{i},L_{i+1}])
is a triangle; otherwise, it is a rectangle. If
i
and
j
are adjacent on the cycle
\Z_{n}
, then it intersects the boundary
\partial\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
at one of its corners, corresponding to
\lbag{z_{i}=z_{j}}\rbag
. If
i
and
j
are at least one element apart on the cycle
\Z_{n}
, then
V_{ij}\subset\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
. We note that
\bigcup_{ij\in\Z_{n}}V_{ij}=\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. That is, the collection of subsets
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{ij}\}_{ij\in\Z_{n}}
form a closed cover of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
.

We can now describe
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
by prescribing it on each piece
V_{ij}
with local coordinates. If we consider
t_{1}\in[L_{i},L_{i+1}]
and
t_{2}\in[L_{j},L_{j+1}]
,

\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(t_{1},t_{2})=\frac{L^{2}}{2}\norm{s_1\tau_i - s_2\tau_j + \frac{x_i - x_j}{L}}^{2}

(83)

where as a shorthand we write
s_{1}=t_{1}-L_{i}
,
s_{2}=t_{2}-L_{j}
, and let
\tau_{k}=u_{k}/l_{k}
denote the unit tangent vector.

Remark 5.3
.

Note that while
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is piecewise smooth, the piecewise smooth Morse theory of
9
;
3
cannot be applied to characterise all Clarke critical points of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. If
(L_{i},L_{j})
is a Clarke critical point of
f
, then it is incident on 4 smooth pieces
V_{i-1,j-1},V_{i-1,j},V_{i,j-1},V_{ij}
, which violates the condition for non-degeneracy; which requires any three of the four gradients of the smooth functions on
V_{i-1,j-1},V_{i-1,j},V_{i,j-1},V_{ij}
evaluated at
(L_{i},L_{j})
to be linearly independent (see
(
3
, Def 2.2 ND1))
or
(
9
, Def 1.3(ND1))
). As
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
is a two-dimensional manifold, this any collection of three gradients cannot be linearly independent. Therefore, if
(L_{i},L_{j})
is a Clarke critical point, it cannot be non-degenerate in the framework of
9
;
3
.

Sublevel sets of the distance function

Having defined
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
piecewise on each closed cover element
V_{ij}
, we now describe sublevel sets
\Gamma^{a}
by their restrictions to each piece
V_{ij}

V_{ij}^{a}:=\Gamma^{a}\cap V_{ij}.

(84)

We note that
\mathcal{V}^{a}:=\{V_{ij}^{a}\}_{i,j\in\Z_{n}}
is a cover of
\Gamma^{a}
. We recall there are charts that take
V_{ij}
to a convex subset of
\R^{2}
. We show in the lemma below that this is also true for sublevel sets
V_{ij}^{a}
, and intersections with other cover elements of
\mathcal{V}^{a}
. We recall a good cover is a cover where any finite intersection of cover elements are contractible.

Lemma 5.4
.

\mathcal{V}^{a}
is a good cover of
\Gamma^{a}
by closed subsets; furthermore, there is an atlas
\Phi=\{(\varphi_{ij},W_{ij})\}
of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
that takes any non-empty intersection
V_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{a}\cap\cdots\cap V_{i_{m}j_{m}}^{a}
homeomorphically to a closed convex subset

\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}}(V_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{a}\cap\cdots\cap V_{i_{m}j_{m}}^{a})\subset\R^{2}.

Proof.

W.l.o.g. we normalise
L=1
. We first prove that each
V_{ij}^{a}
is convex. Let us choose compatible atlases
\Theta=\{(\vartheta_{i},U_{i})\}
and
\Phi=\{(\varphi_{ij},W_{ij})\}
as described in
Proposition

2.18
, where each
[L_{i},L_{i+1}]
is contained in some unique open arc
U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
. Evaluating
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
on local coordinates
s_{i},s_{j}

eq.

83
. the derivatives and Hessians of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
are given by

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\DT_\gamma}{s_i}

\displaystyle=s_{i}-\langle{\tau_{i}},{\tau_{j}}\rangle s_{j}+\langle{x_{i}-x_{j}},{\tau_{i}}\rangle

(85)

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\DT_\gamma}{s_j}

\displaystyle=-\langle{\tau_{i}},{\tau_{j}}\rangle s_{i}+s_{j}+\langle{x_{j}-x_{i}},{\tau_{j}}\rangle

(86)

\displaystyle H_{\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & -\innerprod{\tau_i}{\tau_j} \\ -\innerprod{\tau_i}{\tau_j} & 1 \endlx@physics@matrix).

(87)

Since
|\langle\tau_{i},\tau_{j}\rangle|\leq 1
, the Hessian of the squared distance function is positive semi-definite everywhere; sublevel sets of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(s_{i},s_{j})
(
eq.

83
as a function extended to
\R^{2}
) are convex subsets. Thus the restriction of a non-empty sublevel set of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
to a closed, convex subset
\varphi_{ij}(V_{ij})
is also closed and convex.

Consider a non-empty intersection between
m
cover elements. Recall the transition maps of the atlas are given by the
\Z
-action that yielded the covering
q:[0,1]\times\R\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. Then there are elements
k_{2},\ldots,k_{m}\in\Z
, such that

\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}}(V_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{a}\cap\cdots\cap V_{i_{m}j_{m}}^{a})=\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}}(V_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{a})\cap\left(k_{2}\cdot\varphi_{i_{2}j_{2}}(V_{i_{2}j_{2}}^{a})\right)\cap\cdots\cap\left(k_{m}\cdot\varphi_{i_{m}j_{m}}(V_{i_{m}j_{m}}^{a})\right).

(88)

Because the
\Z
-action is an affine transformation in
\R^{2}
, each
\left(k_{l}\cdot\varphi_{i_{l}j_{l}}(V_{i_{l}j_{l}}^{a})\right)
is still a closed convex subset, and thus the intersection is still closed and convex. Since
\varphi_{i_{1},j_{1}}
is a homeomorphism, we conclude that any non-empty intersection of cover elements in
\mathcal{V}^{a}
is contractible, and thus
\mathcal{V}^{a}
is a good cover of
\Gamma^{a}
.
∎
