Figure 8
:
Left: A closed cover
\mathcal{V}
of the Möbius band
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
. Centre: its nerve
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})
. Right: the nerve of the level set
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}=0
. given by
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{0}})
.

Homotopy type of sublevel sets from Nerve

The description of sublevel sets
\Gamma^{a}
with a cover
\mathcal{V}^{a}
satisfying the properties described
Lemma

5.4
allows us to apply the
unified nerve theorem

(
10
, Theorem 5.9)
to deduce the homotopy type of sublevel sets with a discrete construct called the nerve. Recall the nerve
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})
of
\mathcal{V}
is the simplicial complex whose
k
-simplices are non-empty
k
-fold intersections of cover elements. We let
|\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})|
denote the geometric realisation of the nerve.

We now state a specialised version of the unified nerve theorem
(
10
, Theorem 5.9)
, derived from the its simplification in
(
10
, Table 1)
. We first recall some technical language in order to state the theorem. For a cover
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of a space
X
, let us denote
V_{J}=\cap_{j\in J}V_{j}
. For
V_{J}
, its
latching space

L(V_{J})\subset V_{J}
is the union
\cup_{I\supsetneq J}V_{I}
. We say the cover
\mathcal{V}
satisfies the
latching condition
, if for any
J\subset\mathcal{I}
, the pair
(V_{J},L(V_{J}))
satisfies the homotopy extension property (see for example
(
33
, §0)
).

Theorem 5.5

(
(
10
, Theorem 5.9)
)
.

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space. Suppose
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is a finite and good cover of
X
by closed subsets that satisfies the latching condition. Then there is a functorial homotopy equivalence
X\simeq\quantity|\nerve{\cV}|
.

The properties we have shown about
\mathcal{V}^{a}
in
Lemma

5.4
are sufficient to satisfy the conditions of the nerve theorem, as closed, convex covers in Euclidean space satisfy the latching condition
(
10
, Prop 5.15&18)
.

Proposition 5.6
.

There is a functorial homotopy equivalence
\Gamma^{a}\simeq\quantity|\nerve{\cV^a}|
.

Proof.

Recall from
Lemma

5.4
that
\mathcal{V}^{a}
is a closed, good cover. By construction it is also finite.
Recall the latching space condition. Since we have a chart
\varphi_{ij}
that takes
V^{a}
and its intersections with other cover elements to a homeomorphic closed, convex subset, the chart takes the pair
(V_{J}^{a},L(V_{J}^{a}))
to a homeomorphic pair generated by a cover of closed convex subset in
\R^{2}
, we can apply
(
10
, Prop 5.15&18)
and deduce that
\mathcal{V}^{a}
satisfies the latching condition. Thus
Theorem

5.5
implies there is a functorial homotopy equivalence
\Gamma^{a}\simeq\quantity|\nerve{\cV^a}|
.
∎

Consequently, the persistence homology of the sublevel set filtration of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
can be recovered from a filtration of the nerve. For
a\leq b
, the inclusion of cover elements of
\mathcal{V}^{a}
into
\mathcal{V}^{b}
induces a filtration of simplicial complexes

\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}}):\quad\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{0}})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a}})\subseteq\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{b}})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\infty}}):=\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}}).

(89)

If we take the geometric realisation of the nerves, we get a similar filtration of topological spaces. Because of the functorial equivalence of simplicial and singular homology, the persistent simplicial homology of the filtration above is equivalent to the persistent singular homology of the geometric realisation of the filtration nerve
\pershomf_{i}(\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}}))\cong\pershomf_{i}(|\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})|)
. Since we have a functorial homotopy equivalence
\Gamma^{a}\simeq\quantity|\nerve{\cV^a}|
by
Proposition

5.6
, we conclude that the persistence homology of the nerve filtration is isomorphic to the persistence homology arising from the sublevel set filtration of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
:

\pershomf_{i}(\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}}))\cong\pershomf_{i}(\Gamma^{\bullet})=:\pershomf_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}).

(90)

Since the homology of a finite simplicial complex
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a}})
is finite dimensional,
Proposition

5.6
directly implies that
\pershomf_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
is tame.

Corollary 5.7
.

If
\gamma
is a PL embedding of
S^{1}
(i.e. it satisfies conditions
(C1)
and
(C2)
), then
\pershomf_{i}(\mathcal{T}_{\gamma})
is tame.

5.3
The Filtration of the Nerve

In the preceding section, we have show that the persistent homology of the filtration of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is isomorphic to that of a filtration of
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
. To give an account of
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
, we first describe the full nerve
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})
explicitly. We then show how the geometry of the PL loop prescribes the times at which each simplex in the nerve enters the filtration. Finally, we show how homological critical Morse sets in the nerve filtration correspond to ‘critical points’ in the underlying sublevel set filtration
\Gamma^{\bullet}
.

5.3.1
The Nerve

We now describe the
nerve
of
\mathcal{V}
by considering the intersection between such squares and triangles (see
Figure

8
). Recall the simplices of the nerve are generated by finite elements of cover elements. Let us first consider pairwise intersections of cover elements. The cover elements
V_{ij}
and
V_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
have non-empty intersection only if at least one of the indices from each pair are the same or adjacent in
\Z_{n}
. Excluding the trivial case
\quantity{ij}=\quantity{i',j'}
, there are two cases of non-empty intersection to consider:

(E1)

j=j^{\prime}
, and
i,i^{\prime}
are distinct and adjacent in
\Z_{n}
; or

(E2)

j,j^{\prime}
are distinct and adjacent in
\Z_{n}
, and
i,i^{\prime}
are also distinct and adjacent in
\Z_{n}
.

We recall the quotient map
q:[0,1]\times\R\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, and describe these intersections as homeomorphic images of subsets of
[0,1]\times\R
. In the case described by
(E1)
, where one index element is shared, the intersections are along closed intervals:

V_{ij}\cap V_{i+1,j}=q(\{L_{i}\}\times[L_{j},L_{j+1}])

(91)

Note that addition of indices is modulo
n
here. We also remark that no intersection of cover elements other than
V_{ij}
and
V_{i+1,j}
is equal to
q_{2}(\{L_{i}\}\times[L_{j},L_{j+1}])
. In the second case described by
(E2)
, the two cover elements intersect at one of their corners:

V_{ij}\cap V_{i+1,j+1}=q(L_{i+1},L_{j+1}).

(92)

The characterisation of two-way intersections by
(E1)
and
(E2)
and
eqs.

91
and
92
constrain the intersections between three and four elements to only take place at points
q(L_{i},L_{j})
(see
Figure

8
). If
i\neq j
, then
q(L_{i},L_{j})
lies in the interior
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
. Otherwise,
q(L_{i},L_{i})
is a boundary point. For an interior point
i\neq j
, the point
q(L_{i+1},L_{j+1})\in\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
is also the four way intersection

V_{ij}\cap V_{i+1,j}\cap V_{ij+1}\cap V_{i+1,j+1}=q(L_{i+1},L_{j+1}),

(93)

along with three way intersections between cover elements indexed by three-subsets of
\{\{i,j\},\{i+1,j\},\{i,j+1\},\{i+1,j+1\}\}
. Otherwise, if
i=j
, then
q(L_{i+1},L_{i+1})
is in a unique
three way
intersection

V_{ii}\cap V_{i,i+1}\cap V_{i+1,i+1}=q(L_{i+1},L_{i+1}).

(94)

Having fully described the possible modes of intersections between cover elements of
\mathcal{V}
, we can constructively the nerve
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})
:

1.

We have vertices, corresponding to single cover elements of
\mathcal{V}
, indexed over
(i,j)
with
0\leq i\leq j\leq n-1
;

2.

We have an edge between
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
corresponding to intersections between pairs of cover elements; such edges appear in the complex if
(E1)
and
(E2)
are satisfied;

3.

We have a two simplex between
(i,i),(i,i+1),(i+1,i+1)
for
i=0,\ldots,n-1
, corresponding the intersections of cover elements at the boundary of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
(see right hand side panel of
Figure

8
).

4.

For
i\neq j
, we add a three simplex between
(i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
, and all its faces.

Remark 5.8
.

We can make a further simplification to the nerve for every three simplex
(i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
where
i\neq j
. We note that the anti-diagonal edge
(i,j+1)(i+1,j)
is a
free faces
of
(i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
in
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})
. Thus, we can perform a simplicial collapse to remove simplices that are simultaneously co-faces of
(i,j+1)(i+1,j)
and faces of
(i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
, without changing the homotopy type of
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})
. What remains of the faces of
(i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
are a pair of two-simplicies
(i,j)(i,j+1)(i+1,j+1)
and
(i,j)(i+1,j)(i+1,j+1)
and all their faces.
Thus, instead of adding a three simplex between
(i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
, and all its faces in step (4) above, we can construct a smaller simplicial complex by only adding a pair of two-simplicies
(i,j)(i,j+1)(i+1,j+1)
and
(i,j)(i+1,j)(i+1,j+1)
for
i\neq j
, as the faces of such simplices are added already in steps (1)-(3).

5.3.2
The Filtration

We now give an explicit description of the filtered nerve
\quantity(\nerve{\cV^a})_{a\in\R}
. For a simplex
J=(i_{0},j_{0}),\ldots,(i_{m},j_{m})
in the nerve
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})
, consider
F:\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})\to\R
given by

F(J)=\inf\{a\in\R\ :\ J\in\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a}})\}

(95)

Recall any simplex
J\in\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a}})
corresponds to a non-empty intersection of cover elements
V_{J}^{a}
. Recall by definition that
V_{J}^{a}
is a compact set generated by

V_{J}^{a}=V_{J}\cap\Gamma^{a}.

(96)

By compactness,
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
attains a minimum on
V_{J}
. Thus, a simplex
J
is present in
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a}})
, if and only if
a\geq\min_{V_{J}}\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
. In other words,

F(J)=\min_{V_{J}}\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}.

(97)

Note that
F
is monotone with respect to the face poset of the nerve: for
I\subset J
a face of
J
, because
V_{J}\subseteq V_{I}
, it follows from the definition of
F
as a minimum that
F(I)\leq F(J)
. In particular, this implies
F
is a filter function of the filtration
\quantity(\nerve{\cV^a})_{a\in\R}
, and the sublevel set
F^{-1}(-\infty,{a}]
of simplices gives the nerve
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a}})
.

Having characterised the intersections of cover elements in
eqs.

92
,
91
,
93
and
94
, we can evaluate the minima
\min_{V_{J}}\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
on each simplex and derive
F
. We give a description of these cases below.

0-simplices

For a 0-simplex
J=(i,j)
, the filtration value
F((i,j))
can simply be inferred from the minimal distance between two line segments
\overline{x_{i}x_{i+1}}
and
\overline{x_{j}x_{j+1}}
:

F((i,j))=\frac{1}{2}d(\overline{x_{i}x_{i+1}},\overline{x_{j}x_{j+1}})^{2}.

(98)

We remark that if
V_{ij}
intersects the boundary, i.e.
j=i
or
j=i+1
, then the two line segments overlap at a point, and
F((i,j))=0
.

1-simplices

We recall from
eqs.

91
and
92
that the intersection between two cover adjacent cover elements can either happen along an edge or a corner. For the case of the edge in
eq.

91
, the minimum of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
can be inferred from the distance between
x_{i}
and the line segment
\overline{x_{j},x_{j+1}}
. Otherwise if the cover elements intersect at a corner, then we can simply infer it with point wise distances:

(E1)

\displaystyle:\quad F((i,j)(i+1,j))=\frac{1}{2}d(x_{i},\overline{x_{j}x_{j+1}})^{2}

(99)

(E2)

\displaystyle:\quad F((i,j)(i+1,j+1))=\frac{1}{2}\norm{x_{i+1} - x_{j+1}}^{2}.

(100)

2-simplices and 3-simplices

For
J
a 2-simplex or 3-simplex, the intersection
V_{J}
is a single point
eqs.

93
and
94
. For the interior case
i\neq j
described in
eq.

93
, a 3-simplex
J=(i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1)
and all 2-simplices in its faces enters the filtration at value

F((i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1),(i+1,j+1))=\frac{1}{2}\norm{x_{i+1} - x_{j+1}}^{2}.

(101)

In the boundary where
i=j
as described in
eq.

94
, since
q(L_{i+1},L_{i+1})
is on the boundary,

F((i,i),(i,i+1),(i+1,i+1))=0.

(102)

This thus accounts for all simplices in
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}})
and we have defined the filtration
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
as a sublevel set filtration of the filter function
F
.

5.4
Persistent Homology

We turn our attention then to the persistent homology of the sublevel set filtration of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
, which is isomorphic to the filtration of the nerve
\pershomf_{i}(\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}}))\cong\pershomf_{i}(\Gamma^{\bullet})
(
eq.

90
). We are interested in homological critical values of the nerve filtration
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
. Since we have a finite simplicial complex, there are only finitely many values in the filtration
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
at which the simplicial complex actually changes by addition of simplices. Let us denote those distinct values by
0=a_{0}<a_{1}<\ldots<a_{m}=\max F
,
which is the ordered set of values
a\in\R
where
F^{-1}{\left({a}\right)}\neq\emptyset
. For all values
s,t
such that
a_{i}\leq s\leq t<a_{i+1}
, the nerve is constant, and thus
t\in(a_{i},a_{i+1})
are trivially homological regular values. We can thus restrict our attention to
a_{0},\ldots,a_{m}
.

In analogy with the smooth case where Morse theory relates how critical points of smooth functions changes the homotopy type of sublevel sets, we now discuss how the addition of individual simplices in the filtration
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
changes the homology of the sublevel set. We recall the from
Section

2.2.3
how we can characterise them with the Conley indices of Morse sets. In the following, we shall say a Morse set
S_{i}
of a simplicial complex is
generated by a
k
-simplex
if
\mathrm{cl}(S_{i})
is a the closure of a
k
-simplex. We shall also define the subcomplex
S_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
as the union of all (closures of) 2-simplices that are not faces of a 3-simplex, i.e. those lying along the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Consider a PL embedded loop in
\R^{d}

satisfying
(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
,
with corresponding filtration
\quantity(\nerve{\cV^a})_{a\in\R}
.
For values
a<0
,
no Morse sets exist.
For
a=0
, the only Morse set is
S_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
.
For values
a>0
, all Morse sets are generated by 0, 1, or 3-simplices.

For the case
a>0
we shall split this into cases by the dimension of the simplex being added.
In the following, we shall say a vertex of
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{U}})
is an
interior
vertex if its corresponding cover element
does not intersect the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
.

The first lemma shows that Morse sets that are top-dimension 1 (only edges and vertices) consist of no more than one edge.

Lemma 5.10
.

If a pair of edges
e,e^{\prime}
that are incident on the same interior vertex
v
are added to the filtration at the same time, then either

•

v
is in the link of both edges; or

•

edges
e,e^{\prime}
are faces of a 3-simplex, and
v,e,e^{\prime}
are added along with the unique three simplex containing
e,e^{\prime}
in its faces.

Proof.

Suppose
v
is not in the link of
e=(v,w)
and
e^{\prime}=(v,w^{\prime})
. Then
e,e^{\prime}
being added to the filtration at the same time implies the unique minimum (as
v
in interior, and
(C3)
condition) of the convex function on the cover element associated to
v
is contained in the intersection between the squares belonging to
v,w,w^{\prime}
. Since the intersection is a single point corresponding to the intersection between cover elements corresponding
v,w,w^{\prime}
and some other vertex
w^{\prime\prime}
, the 3-simplex containing
e,e^{\prime}
in its faces is added at the same time.
∎

The lemma above shows that if an edge is added at a step in the filtration, along with other simplices, then either it is in the face of a 3-simplex being added; or other simplices added at the same time (apart from vertices) are not connected to the edge.
For the 2-simplex case,
we observe that by the structure of the cover, 2-simplices are always added as a face of a 3-simplex.
The next lemma now deals with the 3-simplex case.

Lemma 5.11
.

If a pair of interior 3-simplices
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
incident on the same edge
e
are added at the same time, then the edge is in the link of both
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Consider
\sigma=uvab
and
\sigma^{\prime}=uva^{\prime}b^{\prime}
. The cover elements
u
and
v
intersect at a closed line segment, with end points being the quadruple intersections between cover elements
u,v,w,z
and
u,v,w^{\prime},z^{\prime}
respectively. If the end points have the same function value
a
, then by the convexity of the distance function on individual cover elements, the function along the relative interior of the segment is either equal to
a
, or strictly less than
a
. Since the distance function is positive definite quadratic when restricted to each cover element (due to the
(C3)
condition), the distance function cannot be constant along line segments, and thus the relative interior of the segment must take value strictly less than
a
. Since the segment is the intersection between cover elements indexed by
u
and
v
, the edge
uv
in the nerve must then be added at an earlier point in the filtration than
a
. Thus the edge
uv
is in the link of both
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.9
.

For
a<0
, as the filtration is non-negative,
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a}})
is empty.
For
a=0
, as shown in Figure
8
,
the only component
S
is
S_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
.
For
a>0

The lemmas above
constrain the Morse sets of the filtration. The two observations,
imply that
when a simplex is added to the filtration
not as a face
when an edge or a 3-simplex is added to the filtration, the simplex is disconnected with other simplices added at the same time, other than those in its faces. Thus, the Morse sets
S_{i}
at each step of the filtration (connected components of simplices added) are either a single vertex, edge, or 3-simplex, along with a subset of simplices in their faces.
∎

For
S_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
, its link is empty and
S_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
is homotopy equivalent to
S^{1}
,
hence its Conley index is isomorphic to
H_{\bullet}(S^{1})
. Note that this is not reduced homology, so
S_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
is not a
1
-saddle.

If the Morse set enters the filtration at
a>0
, since the closure of each Morse set is a closed simplex, it is contractible. Thus we can infer its Conley indices via the links of the Morse set.
Let
\sigma\in\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a}})
.
We describe the possible
the possible subcomplexes
K\leq\mathrm{cl}(\sigma)

that are realisable as
\mathrm{cl}(\sigma)\cap\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{a-\varepsilon}})

for some
\varepsilon
such that no simplices
are added in
(a-\varepsilon,a)
.
For vertices and edges, all possible subcomplexes are realisable.
Let
(i,j),(i+1,j),(i,j+1)
and
(j+1,j+1)
be the indices of the cover
giving rise to a 3-simplex in the nerve.
The possible subcomplexes (apart from
\mathrm{cl}(\sigma)
) we need to consider are the subgraphs of the complete graph on these vertices.
Subgraphs containing isolated vertices
are not realisable:
a vertex
v

being isolated implies
that the minimum of the distance function
restricted to
V_{v}
is in the interior of
V_{v}
.
As the sublevel set restricted to
U^{v}
is a non-degenerate
ellipse (intersected with
V_{v}
) by condition
(C3)
,
these can not intersect with the corner

V_{ij}\cap V_{i+1,j}\cap V_{ij+1}\cap V_{i+1,j+1}

without first intersecting one of the line segments (
V_{ij}\cap V_{i+1,j}
etc.),
thus the 3-simplex cannot be added immediately after a vertex.
Any subgraph containing a diagonal edge

(i,j),(i+1,j+1)
or
(i+1,j),(i,j+1)

is also not realisable as the existence of this edge implies the existence of the 3-simplex.
The remaining subgraphs are realisable.
This is tabulated in Table
2
where we observe these Morse sets are
n
-saddles.

5.4.1
Geometric characterisation of critical points

In this section we will show a bijective
correspondence between certain points in
\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
whose image under an embedding
\gamma

satisfies certain geometric conditions (given in
definition

5.13
)
and homological critical simplices (
n
-saddles)
of
\mathsf{N}({\mathcal{V}^{\bullet}})
.

Local Geometry of PL Loops

In subsequent discussions, we find it useful to describe the local geometry of a PL loop using the following quantities, illustrated in
Figure

9
. On each chart
\vartheta_{i}:U_{i}\to\R
of
S^{1}
, we define the one-sided unit ‘tangent’ vectors as the limiting tangents,

\tau^{\pm}(t)=\lim_{h\to 0^{\pm}}\frac{f\circ\vartheta_{i}^{-1}(t+h/L)-f\circ\vartheta_{i}^{-1}(t)}{h}\in\R^{d}.

(103)

If
t\in(L_{i},L_{i+1})
, i.e.
f\circ\vartheta_{i}^{-1}
is not one of the vertices
x_{j}
, then the limits agree
\tau^{+}(t)=\tau^{-}(t)
. Furthermore, if we assume that the vertices of
f
are in general position
(C3)
, then the limits agree iff
f(z)
is in the interior of a segment, as no three vertex points are allowed to be collinear. Note that due to the parametrisation of
f

eq.

81
,
\tau^{\pm}=u_{i}/l_{i}
for some
i
, and thus
\norm{\tau^\pm}=1
are always unit tangent vectors. In subsequent discussions, we can assume the atlas
\Theta
is fixed for given cyclic data, and abuse notation and let
\tau^{\pm}(z)=\tau^{\pm}(t)
for
t=\vartheta_{i}(z)
.

We find it useful to characterise the relative directions of the loop between two points
z_{1},z_{2}\in S^{1}
. Letting
v_{1,2}=f(z_{2})-f(z_{1})
denote the relative position between two points on the loop, we define the
tangent angles of
f

at
z_{1}
relative to
z_{2}

as the angles
\theta^{\pm}_{f}(z_{1},z_{2})\in[0,\pi)

between
v_{1,2}
and
\pm\tau^{\pm}(z_{1})
, as illustrated in
Figure

9
:

\displaystyle\cos(\theta^\pm_f(z_1, z_2))

\displaystyle=\pm\langle{\tau^{\pm}(z_{1})},{v_{1,2}}\rangle,

(104)

\displaystyle\cos(\theta^\pm_f(z_2, z_1))

\displaystyle=\pm\langle{\tau^{\pm}(z_{2})},{v_{2,1}}\rangle=\mp\langle{\tau^{\pm}(z_{2})},{v_{1,2}}\rangle.

(105)

v_{1,2}
-\tau^{-}(z_{2})
\tau^{+}(z_{2})
-\tau^{-}(z_{1})
\tau^{+}(z_{1})
f(z_{1})
f(z_{2})
\theta^{+}_{f,z_{1},z_{2}}
\theta^{-}_{f,z_{1},z_{2}}
\theta^{-}_{f,z_{2},z_{1}}
\theta^{+}_{f,z_{2},z_{1}}

Figure 9
:

Tangent angles of
f
at
z_{1}
relative to
z_{2}
and
z_{2}
relative to
z_{1}
(Definition
5.12
).
The picture shows the two segments of a clockwise-oriented loop
f:S^{1}\to\R
corresponding to
z_{1},z_{2}
.
Here,
f
curves towards
f(z_{2})
at
f(z_{1})

and curves away from
f(z_{1})
at
f(z_{2})
.
Hence
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag
satisfies condition
(K1)
of Theorem
5.14
and so corresponds to a homological critical
point of index 1, a saddle point.

Definition 5.12
.

If
\theta^{+}_{f}(z_{1},z_{2})
and
\theta^{-}_{f}(z_{1},z_{2})

are both acute, then we shall say

f
curves towards
f(z_{2})
at
f(z_{1})
.
If neither are acute we shall say

f
curves away from
f(z_{2})
at
f(z_{1})
.

Definition 5.13
.

For
f:S^{1}\to\R^{d}
piecewise-linear,
we define three conditions for a point
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag\in\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})
:

(K0)

f
curves away from
f(z_{2})
at
f(z_{1})
, and away from
f(z_{1})
at
f(z_{2})
;

(K1)

f
curves towards
f(z_{2})
at
f(z_{1})
, and away from
f(z_{1})
at
f(z_{2})
, or vice versa;

(K2)

f
curves towards
f(z_{2})
at
f(z_{1})
, and towards
f(z_{1})
at
f(z_{2})
.

Note that at most one of these conditions may hold.
If
(K0)
,
(K1)
, or
(K2)
hold
we define the index
\lambda(\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag)
as 0, 1, or 2 respectively.
We let
C_{a}^{i}\subset f^{-1}{\left({a}\right)}
be the points in the level set at
a
which satisfy condition
(Ki)
.

Theorem 5.14
.

Suppose
\gamma:S^{1}\to\R^{d}
is a piecewise linear interpolation of cyclic data
(x_{i})_{i\in\Z_{n}}
satisfying the non-degeneracy conditions
(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
. For
a>0
and
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small,

H_{\bullet}(\Gamma^{a},\Gamma^{a-\epsilon})\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{2}\bigoplus_{w\in C_{a}^{i}}\tilde{H}_{\bullet}(S^{i}),

(106)

where
C_{a}^{i}
are points in
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{-1}{\left({a}\right)}
described in Definition
5.13
satisfying the geometric properties
(K0)
,
(K1)
and
(K2)
. For
a=0
, we also have
H_{\bullet}(\Gamma^{0},\Gamma^{-\epsilon})\cong H_{\bullet}(S^{1})
.

Proof.

From Proposition
5.9
,
the only critical Morse sets are
S_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
at
a=0

and those generated by simplices for
a>0
.
For
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag\in\mathrm{UConf}_{2}(S^{1})

define

\sigma\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag=\bigcap_{V_{ij}:\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag\in V_{ij}}V_{ij},

the highest dimensional simplex containing
\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag
.
Suppose
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag)=a

so
\sigma\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag

is in the filtration at value
a
.
We will build a correspondence, given in Table
3
, between
\theta^{\pm}_{\gamma,z_{1},z_{2}},\theta^{\pm}_{\gamma,z_{2},z_{1}}

and the subcomplex
of
\sigma(z_{1},z_{2})

that exists at filtration value

,
for

sufficiently small that no simplices
are added within
[a-\epsilon,a)
.
Unless this subcomplex is
\sigma\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag

itself,
\sigma\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag

also first appears at

.
In this case, from the proof of Proposition

,

\sigma\lbag{z_{1},z_{2}}\rbag
will be a Morse set.
We can then refer to Table

to see if

is critical

We shall now show the correspondence in Table

Suppose

and

Then using the cosine rule

Likewise, the simplices

,

,

6
Higher Order Finite Subset Spaces and the Volume Transform

In order to generalise our approach to higher order spaces, we need to know more about their topological structure. If higher order finite subset spaces could be triangulated then they would be more tractable for computations, such as the pipeline we have constructed for the CDT. However, to do this we must first understand what these higher order finite subset spaces are. Difficulties arise from the fact that even if you start with a manifold
M
, higher order finite subset spaces need not be manifolds or manifolds with boundary (See section 7 of
38
and the last remark of section 1.2 of
54
). A detailed understanding of the topology of these spaces will allow us to create a generalisation of our pipeline of the CDT.

Remark 6.1
.

We should note that, in the generalized approach there are two options, either factor through the symmetric product or the
n
-th finite subset space. If one was to factor to just the symmetric product, then for functions depending only on the set, you would introduce duplicate computations, as we have that the
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is a quotient of
SP^{n}(X)
.

6.1
Topological properties of higher order finite subset spaces

In
Section

2.1.1
we discussed how finite subset spaces are stratified and how they fit together. We might also like to know about the overall topological structure of these spaces. For a thorough overview,
38
,
43
,
32
are recommended. What we find, is that for
n\geq 3
, we have that
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is simply connected
38
. A recent result from Lazovskis
43
, provides a different proof approach for
n\geq 4
, using the result that the map
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{F}_{n}(X))\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathbf{F}_{n+2}(X))
induced by the inclusion of spaces is trivial for all
n
.

Theorem 6.2
.

38

43
For every positive integer
n\geq 3
,
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{F}_{n}(X))
is trivial.

So when considering a filtration of a higher order
n
-th finite subset space, there can be no point of the form
(a,\infty)
in the
H_{1}
persistence diagram.

As was mentioned in
Section

2.1
, if we start with a manifold
X
for higher orders we are not guaranteed that
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is a manifold or manifold with boundary. If we restrict ourselves to closed manifolds (compact without boundary) then we have a complete description as follows:

Theorem 6.3
.

38
Let
X
be a closed manifold of dimension
d\geq 1
, then
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is a closed manifold if and only if,
d=1
and
n=3

or
,
d=2
and
n=2
.

This result excludes the trivial case of
n=1
. To prove this result, a lemma is used that doesn’t require
X
to be a closed manifold.

Lemma 6.4
.

38
If
X
is a manifold of dimension
d>2
, then
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
is never a manifold for
n\geq 2
.

For higher order
n
-th finite subset spaces we may not have a manifold structure, but we do still have the following:

Lemma 6.5
.

38
For
X
a simplicial complex then,
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
has a CW-decomposition with top cells in dimension
n\ \mathrm{dim}(X)
, so that
H_{*}(\mathbf{F}_{n}(X))
is trivial for
*>n\ \mathrm{dim}(X)
.

Furthermore, in section 5.3 of
56
we have that if
X
is triangulated then so is
\mathbf{F}_{n}(X)
. Hence, although in most cases we lose the manifold structure we are theoretically able to construct a triangulation. This would enable us to perform computations when considering filtrations on the higher order finite subset spaces

6.2
Volume functions

Above we saw that we can generalize our approach from the CDT to higher order spaces. To do this we need to consider higher order functions. In this subsection we show the continuity and stability of a higher order volume function, namely the square volume of the
k
-dimensional simplex of
k+1
points.

The natural generalization from the chordal distance transform is to consider generalisations of distance functions to
(k+1)
-points where
k>1
, which are invariant with respect to the isometries of the space, and are only dependent on the set of points. We call such functions
geometric functions
. A natural candidate is the
k
-dimensional volume of convex hulls of
(k+1)
points: for a pair of points, this reduces to the distance between points.

We first show how these geometric functions induce a function on
\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)
. Let
X
be a compact space and let
Y
be a metric space and a continuous function
\mathfrak{e}:X\rightarrow Y
. Consider a geometric function
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathfrak{e}}:X^{k+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, defined using
\mathfrak{e}
and such that it is invariant when we permute
(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
, and is only dependent on the set of points, then it will factor through a quotient map from
X^{k+1}
to the
(k+1)
-th finite subset space
\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)
(note that if we do not wish it to be only dependent on the set of points then one could factor to the symmetric product
SP^{k+1}(X)
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{k+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\mathfrak{e}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!g_{\mathfrak{e}}}

(107)

Here
g_{\mathfrak{e}}:\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)\to[0,\infty)
is the unique, continuous map such that the diagram commutes, and is given explicitly by

\displaystyle g_{\mathfrak{e}}(\{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\mathfrak{e}}(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k}).

(108)

In particular, we focus on the following case. For
k+1
points on a space
X
, and a map
\mathfrak{e}:X\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can consider the set of points
\{\varphi(x_{0}),\ldots,\varphi(x_{k})\}
, and consider the
k
-dimensional square volume of the convex hull of those points. We define this map as
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{k}:X^{k+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for
k\leq d
by,

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{k}(x_{0},\dots,x_{k}):=Vol(\Delta^{k}(\mathfrak{e}(x_{0}),\dots,\mathfrak{e}(x_{k})))^{2}

We then define the volume transform,
\mathsf{Vol}_{k}:C(X,\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow C(\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X),\mathbb{R})
by,
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto g_{\mathfrak{e}}
. We have the following continuity result, if we impose the following metrics on the function spaces induced by the sup-norm, which is attained by maxima as
X
and
\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)
are both compact. For
\mathfrak{e}_{1},\mathfrak{e}_{2}\in C(X,\mathbb{R}^{d})
we define
d(\mathfrak{e}_{1},\mathfrak{e}_{2}):=\max_{x\in X}\|\mathfrak{e}_{1}(x)-\mathfrak{e}_{2}(x)\|_{2}
; for
h_{1},h_{2}\in C(\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X),\mathbb{R})
, we let
d(h_{1},h_{2}):=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)}|h_{1}(\mathbf{x})-h_{2}(\mathbf{x})|
.

Theorem 6.6
.

If
X
is a compact space, then the volume transform

\mathsf{Vol}_{k}:C(X,\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow C(\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X),\mathbb{R})

is continuous for both spaces equipped with the sup-norm. In particular, if
d(\mathfrak{e}_{1},\mathfrak{e}_{2})\leq M
for
\mathfrak{e}_{1},\mathfrak{e}_{2}\in C(X,\R^{d})
, and

D=\max(\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}),\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{2})),

we have a bound

d(\mathsf{Vol}_{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}),\mathsf{Vol}_{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}))\leq\frac{(k+2)\sqrt{k(k+1)}}{(k!)^{2}2^{k-2}}M(M+D)\ \sqrt{(k^{2}+2k+2)D^{2}+(2k+2)}^{k+1}.

(109)

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{e}_{1},\mathfrak{e}_{2}\in C(X,\mathbb{R}^{N})
, with
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{k}
and
g_{\mathfrak{e}}
as above. The volume squared,
Vol(\Delta^{k}(\mathfrak{e}(x_{0}),\dots,\mathfrak{e}(x_{k})))^{2}
can be given by using the determinant of the Cayley-Menger matrix:

A_{\mathfrak{e}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{pmatrix}0&d_{01}^{2}&d_{02}^{2}&\cdots&d_{0k}^{2}&1\\
d_{01}^{2}&0&d_{12}^{2}&\cdots&d_{1k}^{2}&1\\
d_{02}^{2}&d_{12}^{2}&0&\cdots&d_{2k}^{2}&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
d_{0k}^{2}&d_{1k}^{2}&d_{2k}^{2}&\cdots&0&1\\
1&1&1&\cdots&1&0\end{pmatrix}

where
\mathbf{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{k})

\in X^{k+1}
,
d_{ij}=\|\mathfrak{e}(x_{i})-\mathfrak{e}(x_{j})\|_{2}
; then
Vol(\Delta^{k}(\mathfrak{e}(x_{0}),\dots,\mathfrak{e}(x_{k})))^{2}=\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{(k!)^{2}2^{k}}\det{A_{\mathfrak{e}}^k(\mathbf{x})}
.

Let
B=A_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})-A_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})
, then
B
has at most
(k+2)^{2}-3k-4=k(k+1)
non-zero terms. So now we wish to bound the difference of the squares of
d_{ij}=\|\mathfrak{e}(x_{i})-\mathfrak{e}(x_{j})\|_{2}
for
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{2}
, i.e wishing to bound
b_{ij}=\|\mathfrak{e}_{1}(x_{i})-\mathfrak{e}_{1}(x_{j})\|_{2}^{2}-\|\mathfrak{e}_{2}(x_{i})-\mathfrak{e}_{2}(x_{j})\|_{2}^{2}
. If we let
M:=\max_{x\in X}\|\mathfrak{e}_{1}(x)-\mathfrak{e}_{2}(x)\|_{2}
, we find that

|b_{ij}|\leq 4M(M+\text{min}\{\text{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}(X)),\text{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}(X))\})

So,

\|B\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{k(k+1)}\ \ 4M(M+\text{min}\{\text{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}(X)),\text{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}(X))\})

Let us then bound
d(\mathsf{Vol}_{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}),\mathsf{Vol}_{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}))=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in X^{k+1}}|\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}(\mathbf{x})-\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}(\mathbf{x})|
.

\displaystyle\bigl|\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}(\mathbf{x})-\Gamma^{k}_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}(\mathbf{x})\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|Vol\bigl(\Delta^{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}(x_{0}),\dots,\mathfrak{e}_{1}(x_{k}))\bigr)^{2}-Vol\bigl(\Delta^{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}(x_{0}),\dots,\mathfrak{e}_{2}(x_{k}))\bigr)^{2}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{(k!)^{2}2^{k}}\det A_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})-\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{(k!)^{2}2^{k}}\det A_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(k!)^{2}2^{k}}\bigl|\det A_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})-\det A_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(k!)^{2}2^{k}}(k+2)\bigl\|A_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})-A_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})\bigr\|_{2}\max\!\left\{\bigl\|A_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})\bigr\|_{2},\bigl\|A_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})\bigr\|_{2}\right\}^{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(k!)^{2}2^{k}}(k+2)\sqrt{k(k+1)}\,4M\bigl(M+\min\{\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}(X)),\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}(X))\}\bigr)

\displaystyle\cdot\max\!\left\{\bigl\|A_{\mathfrak{e}_{1}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})\bigr\|_{2},\bigl\|A_{\mathfrak{e}_{2}}^{k}(\mathbf{x})\bigr\|_{2}\right\}^{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(k+2)\sqrt{k(k+1)}M\bigl(M+\min\{\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}(X)),\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}(X))\}\bigr)}{(k!)^{2}2^{k-2}}

\displaystyle\cdot\max\!\Bigl\{\sqrt{(k^{2}+2k+2)\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}(X))^{2}+(2k+2)^{2}},

\displaystyle\sqrt{(k^{2}+2k+2)\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}(X))^{2}+(2k+2)^{2}}\Bigr\}^{k+1}.

With the first inequality coming from theorem 2.12 of
37
.
This then provides an upper bound on
d(\mathsf{Vol}_{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}),\mathsf{Vol}_{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}))
as the supremum of the left hand side of the inequality.

Let us fix
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
. Because
|\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{1})-\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{2})|\leq 2M
, for any
\epsilon>0
, we can find a sufficiently small
M>0
, such that for all
\mathfrak{e}_{2}
in an
M
-ball of
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
, the right hand side of the equality is bounded below
\epsilon
. Thus
\mathsf{Vol}
is continuous with respect to the sup-norm metrics.

Let
D=\text{max}\{\text{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}(X)),\text{diam}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}(X))\}
. We can then simplify the inequality to

d(\mathsf{Vol}_{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{1}),\mathsf{Vol}_{k}(\mathfrak{e}_{2}))\leq\frac{(k+2)\sqrt{k(k+1)}}{(k!)^{2}2^{k-2}}M(M+D)\ \sqrt{(k^{2}+2k+2)D^{2}+(2k+2)}^{k+1}.

∎

Having defined a continuous function
g_{\mathfrak{e}}:\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)\to\R
, we can construct a sublevel set filtration and consider its persistent homology. Since
\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)
is compact and and trianguable (see 5.3 Discussion of
56
), continuous functions on
\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(X)
always have
q
-tame persistent homology
(
16
, Theorem 2.22)
. Thus, we can consider the persistence diagrams
\pershomf_{i}(g_{\mathfrak{e}})
in the metric space of diagrams, equipped with bottleneck distance. Because the map
\pershomf
is a continuous transformation of functions from
Theorem

6.6
to the space of diagrams, the composition of
\mathsf{Vol}_{k}:C(X,\mathbb{R}^{d})\to C(\mathbf{F}_{k+1}(S^{1}),\R)
with
\pershomf
to yield
\pershomf_{i}(g_{\mathfrak{e}})
is a continuous function.

Corollary 6.7
.

\pershomf\circ\mathsf{Vol}_{k}:C(X,\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbf{Dgm}
continuous w.r.t. bottleneck distance on
\mathbf{Dgm}
, for
X
triangulable.

7
Discussion and Future Work

The motivation for this work was to find a geometric descriptor of embedded loops that would be invariant under internal symmetries like diffeomorphisms of
S^{1}
and external symmetries such as euclidean transformations, and stable under perturbation. In this article, we have proved that the composition
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}
of the chordal distance transform
\mathcal{T}
and persistent homology
\pershomf
satisfies these desirable properties. Furthermore, we have characterised the behaviour of the map
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}
for generic
C^{2}
and finite piecewise linear embeddings, and shown such persistence diagrams are generically tame. In the generic setting, we have also given a geometric description of the critical points that generate the birth and death of homological features in the persistence diagram
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}(\gamma)
, allowing practitioners who apply this method to interpret
\pershomf\circ\mathcal{T}(\gamma)
in terms of the geometry of the embedded loop. While the chordal distance transform captures geometric features associated to pairs of points on the embedded loop, we show that it is a special case of the volume transformation, a generalisation that captures higher order geometric information associated to multiple points along the loop. We show the volume transform of a compact space embedded in Euclidean space is also continuous and stable.

As future work, we would like to apply a similar Morse-theoretic approach to understanding generalisations of the chordal distance transform to other spaces and higher order point configurations. An immediate generalisation of the chordal distance transform of
S^{1}
is to that of
S^{2}
, where the two-point finite subspace is a manifold
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{2})=\mathbb{C}P^{2}
; we anticipate a lot of the Morse-theoretic techniques in this work can be transferred easily to analyse that case. However, challenges arise for other spaces as finite subset spaces are manifolds only in a few particular cases (see
Theorem

6.3
). Nonetheless, we can explore a similar approach with stratified Morse theory
29
or a recent analogous theory for CW complexes
51
.
Finally, we would like to put this methodology to the test on real world scientific problems, such as the analysis of cells morphologies, ring polymers, and organoid shape where the interpretability of the critical points of the chordal distance transform and the invariance of our descriptor could prove useful in classification tasks.

Appendix A
Morton’s Construction of the 2
nd
Symmetric Product of
S^{1}

In this section, we show constructively that
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
is homeomorphic to the Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
, via the construction by Morton
48

Morton’s construction involves deriving the universal covering
\bar{\scre}
of
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by an infinite strip
[0,1]\times\R
, through the universal covering
\tilde{\scre}
of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
by
\R^{2}
: they are related by a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\R^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,1]\times\R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{1}\times S^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/(\Z\rtimes S_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/\Z^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\screii}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/\Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\scre}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/S_{2}}

(
eq.

20
)

The main idea behind the construction is to decompose the successive quotients
q\circ\tilde{e}
into another pair of successive quotients
\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q}
, where
\bar{\scre}
is the universal covering of the Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
by a
\Z
-action on
[0,1]\times\R
.

We leverage this construction to induce an atlas of the
\Z
-covering
\bar{\scre}
from an atlas for the
\Z
-covering
\scre:\R\to S^{1}
. An atlas of
\scre
allows us to give local coordinates for
S^{1}
, and an atlas it induces on
\bar{\scre}
gives local coordinates for
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
in terms of local coordinates of
S^{1}
. We show that in the subsequent section. Here we set out, in concise terms, the technical apparatus needed for the construction of such an atlas. We develop Morton’s construction in two steps. First, we show that
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
can be written as the quotient of
\R^{2}
by an action of the semi-direct product
G=\Z^{2}\rtimes S_{2}
. We then show that this quotient is a composition of the two quotients maps
\bar{\scre}\circ\tilde{q}
. The first is a quotient of
\R^{2}
by the action of a normal subgroup
N\cong\Z\rtimes S_{2}
of
G
. This yields an orbit space
\R^{2}/N
homeomorphic to the diagonal strip
[0,1]\times\R
. The second quotient is that of the diagonal strip by a
\Z
-action, which gives the universal covering of the Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
, as illustrated in
Figure

6
.

\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
as a Quotient of
\R^{2}
by
\Z^{2}\rtimes S_{2}

Recall
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
is the quotient of the torus
q:S^{1}\times S^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
by the action of the symmetric group
S_{2}
. Because the torus is itself the quotient
\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow S^{1}\times S^{1}
of
\R^{2}
by a
\Z^{2}
-covering action, we can express
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
as a quotient
p:\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, obtained by composing the quotient
\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow S^{1}\times S^{1}
with
q
. We recall the quotient
\screii:=\scre\times\scre:\R^{2}{\twoheadrightarrow}S^{1}\times S^{1}
is given by the product of
\scre:\R\to S^{1}
where

\scre(t)=e^{2\pi\imath t}.

(110)

Following
48
, we first show that the quotient map
p
is equivalent to the quotient of
\R^{2}
by an action of the semi-direct product
G=\Z^{2}\rtimes S_{2}
. The group
G
has presentation

G=\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho\ |\ \rho^{2}=e,\ \rho\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}\rho,\ \alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}\rangle

(111)

where we identify
\alpha_{i}=(t_{i},e)
with the generators of
\Z^{2}
,

t_{1}=(1,0),\ t_{2}=(0,1),

and
\rho=(0,r)
with the generator
r
of
S_{2}=\langle r\ |\ r^{2}=e\rangle
. Because
\Z^{2}
generated by
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
is a normal subgroup of
G
, we can express any element
g\in G
uniquely as
g=\alpha_{1}^{k_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{k_{2}}\rho^{k_{3}}
for
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\Z
.

Let us define an action of
G
on
\R^{2}
. Denoting coordinates in
\R^{2}
by
(x_{1},x_{2})
, we have

\alpha_{1}\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1}+1,x_{2}),\quad\alpha_{2}\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1},x_{2}+1),\quad\rho\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{2},x_{1}).

(112)

Thus, for any element
g=\alpha_{1}^{k_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{k_{2}}\rho^{k_{3}}
,

g\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{r^{k_{3}}(1)}+k_{1},x_{r^{k_{3}}(2)}+k_{2}).

(113)

Let us denote the quotient of
\R^{2}
by the
G
-action
p^{\prime}:\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\R^{2}/G
. We claim that
p^{\prime}=p
up to homeomorphism. We can check by direct computation that the quotient
p=q\circ\screii:\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
sends points along an orbit
G\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})
to the same point
\{\scre(x_{1}),\scre(x_{2})\}\in\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
, and the fibres
p^{-1}{\left({\{\scre(x_{1}),\scre(x_{2})\}}\right)}
are precisely the orbits
G\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})
. Thus, the universal property of quotient maps imply we have a homeomorphism
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\approx\R^{2}/G
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\R^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}\times S^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\R^{2}/G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\screii}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/\Z^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\approx}

(114)

Henceforth we consider
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})
and
\R^{2}/G
interchangeably.

The Diagonal Presentation

We give another presentation of
G
. If we define
\mu=\rho\alpha_{1}
and
\nu=\alpha_{1}^{-1}\alpha_{2}
, then we can write

G=\langle\mu,\nu,\rho\ |\ \rho^{2}=e,\ \nu\rho\nu=\rho,\ \mu\rho=\rho\nu\mu\rangle

(115)

We observe from this presentation of
G
that we can write
G
as another semi-direct product
G\cong N\rtimes H
, where
N=\Z\rtimes S_{2}=\langle\nu,\rho\ |\ \nu\rho\nu=\rho\rangle
and
G/N=H\cong\langle\mu\rangle\cong\Z
. We are also free to write any element of
G
uniquely as
g=\mu^{n_{1}}\nu^{n_{2}}\rho^{n_{3}}
.

Let us consider the action of
N
on
\R^{2}
. We relate the action of
N
to the full action of
G
by considering how the
G
-action acts on the
N
-orbits. The action of
G
on
N
-orbits
(N\cdot x)\subset\R^{2}
is a well-defined map sending
N
-orbits to
N
-orbits, as
N
being a normal subgroup of
G
implies

g\cdot(N\cdot x)=N\cdot(g\cdot x).

(116)

Furthermore, the action on
\R^{2}/N
is given by that of
H\cong\langle\mu\rangle
: because we can write any element of
G
as
g=\mu^{k}n
, for some
n\in N
, the action of
g
sends

g\cdot(N\cdot x)=\mu^{k}\cdot(N\cdot x)=N\cdot(\mu^{k}\cdot x).

(117)

This implies the
G
-action on
N
-orbits is a
\Z
-action. Furthermore, because
\langle\mu\rangle\cap N=e
, the
N
-orbit of
\mu^{k}\cdot x
is distinct from that of
x
for
k\neq 0
, hence the
\Z
-action is a free action on
\R^{2}/N
. Henceforth, let us denote the
\Z
-action on
\R^{2}/N
by

k\star(N\cdot x):=\mu^{k}\cdot(N\cdot x).

(118)

Because the
\Z
-action on
N
-orbits sends
N\cdot x
in
\R^{2}
to
G\cdot x
, the composition of the
\Z
-action with the
N
-action is the
G
-action on
\R^{2}
:

(119)

The Infinite Strip and the Möbius band

We now show that the orbit space
\R^{2}/N
is homeomorphic to
[0,1]\times\R:=[0,1]\times\R
. Let us consider the action of generators
\mu,\nu,\rho
of
G
on
\R^{2}
. If we perform a change of coordinates
u=x_{2}-x_{1}
and
v=x_{1}+x_{2}
, the action of generators
\mu,\nu,\rho
can be given by

\mu\cdot(u,v)=(1-u,v+1),\quad\nu\cdot(u,v)=(u+2,v),\quad\rho\cdot(u,v)=(-u,v).

(120)

Since
N
is generated by
\nu
and
\rho
, its action is restricted to fibres
v^{-1}{\left({c}\right)}\approx\R
of the trivial bundle
v:\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\R
, as the action of
\nu
and
\rho
keeps the
v
coordinate constant, and only shifts the
u
coordinate parametrising the fibres
v^{-1}{\left({c}\right)}
. Furthermore, the action on each fibre
v^{-1}{\left({c}\right)}
is independent of
c
. Writing
\R^{2}=\R\times\R
with the first factor the
u
coordinate parametrising fibre
v^{-1}{\left({c}\right)}
, and the second factor the
v
coordinate, we can thus express the
N
-orbit space as

\R^{2}/N=\R/N\times\R.

The reader can verify that the subset where
u\in[0,1]
in each fibre
v^{-1}{\left({c}\right)}
is a fundamental domain of the action of
N
. In fact
(
48
, Lemmas 1 & 2)
show that
[0,1]\approx\R/N
, and we have a homeomorphism

\zeta:[0,1]\times\R=u^{-1}[0,1]\hookrightarrow\R^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\R^{2}/N=\R/N\times\R

(121)

The forward map
\zeta
send
x\in u^{-1}[0,1]
to
N\cdot x
, and its inverse
\zeta^{-1}
sends orbits
N\cdot x
to its unique intersection with
u^{-1}[0,1]
.

Let us then consider the
\Z
-action on
[0,1]\times\R
, given by
k\star x:=\zeta^{-1}{\left({k\star\zeta(x)}\right)}
: For
(u,v)\in[0,1]\times\R
,

\displaystyle 1\star(u,v)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{-1}{\left({1\star\zeta(u,v)}\right)}=\zeta^{-1}{\left({1\star(N\cdot(u,v))}\right)}=\zeta^{-1}{\left({N\cdot(\mu\cdot(u,v))}\right)}

(122)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{-1}{\left({N\cdot(1-u,v+1)}\right)}=(1-u,v+1).

We note that
1\star(u,v)\in[0,1]\times\R
, hence the
\Z
-action on
[0,1]\times\R
is well-defined. Moreover, the
\Z
-action is that of the
glide reflection
; the action of a generator
1
restricts to a homeomorphism between fibres
v^{-1}{\left({c}\right)}\cap u^{-1}[0,1]
and
v^{-1}{\left({c+1}\right)}\cap u^{-1}[0,1]
, where the
u
coordinate is reflected
u\mapsto 1-u
in its image. The quotient
\bar{\scre}:[0,1]\times\R\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{M}
by this
\Z
-action is precisely the Möbius band
\mathcal{M}
. This implies
(\R^{2}/N)/\Z\approx([0,1]\times\R)/\Z=\mathcal{M}
. Furthermore,
\bar{\scre}
is the universal covering of the Möbius band.

Since we have also shown that
\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\approx\R^{2}/G
in
eq.

114
and
\R^{2}/G\approx(\R^{2}/N)/\Z
in
eq.

119
, we have a sequence of homeomorphisms that establishes the two-finite subset space is homeomorphic to the Möbius band:

\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})\approx\R^{2}/G\approx(\R^{2}/N)/\Z\approx([0,1]\times\R)/\Z=\mathcal{M}.

(123)

Combining and summarising the commutative diagrams
eqs.

114
and
119
, we obtain a
bundle morphism

\tilde{q}:\screii\to\bar{\scre}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\R^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{-1}[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{1}\times S^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{2}(S^{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/(\Z\rtimes S_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/\Z^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\screii}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/\Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\scre}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/S_{2}}

(
eq.

20
)
